\displaystyle\bm{z}^{(\ell)}=[z^{(\ell)}_{1},\cdots,z^{(\ell)}_{m_{(\ell)}}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{(\ell)}\times 1}\ ,\quad\bm{b}^{(\ell)}=[b^{(\ell)}_{1},\cdots,b^{(\ell)}_{m_{(\ell)}}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{(\ell)}\times 1}\ .

(32)

Both the weights and the biases are collectively known as the network parameters, defined in the following matrices for layer
(\ell)
:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}_{i}^{(\ell)}=\left[\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}\right]=\left[\theta_{i1}^{(\ell)},\ldots,\theta_{im_{(\ell-1)}}^{(\ell)}\ |\ \theta_{i(m_{(\ell-1)}+1)}^{(\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[w_{i1}^{(\ell)},\ldots,w_{im_{(\ell-1)}}^{(\ell)}\ |\ b_{i}^{(\ell)}\right]

\displaystyle=\left[\bm{w}_{i}^{(\ell)}\ |\ b_{i}^{(\ell)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times[m_{(\ell-1)}+1]}

(33)

\displaystyle\bm{\Theta}^{(\ell)}=\left[\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}\right]=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\theta}_{1}^{(\ell)}\\
\vdots\\
\bm{\theta}_{m_{(\ell)}}^{(\ell)}\end{bmatrix}=\left[\left.\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\ \right|\ \bm{b}^{(\ell)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{(\ell)}\times[m_{(\ell-1)}+1]}

(34)

For simplicity and convenience, the set of all parameters in the network is denoted by
\bm{\theta}
, and the set of all parameters in layer
(\ell)
by
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
:
65
65

65

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}=\{\bm{\theta}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\bm{\theta}^{(L)}\}=\{\bm{\Theta}^{(1)},\cdots,\bm{\Theta}^{(\ell)},\cdots,\bm{\Theta}^{(L)}\}\,,\text{ such that }\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}\equiv\bm{\Theta}^{(\ell)}

Note that the set

Similar to the definition of the parameter matrix

in Eq. (

\bm{b}^{(\ell)}

in Eq. (

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{(\ell)}=\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}+\bm{b}^{(\ell)}\equiv\left[\left.\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\ \right|\ \bm{b}^{(\ell)}\right]\begin{bmatrix}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}\\
1\end{bmatrix}=:\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}\ \overline{\bm{y}}^{(\ell-1)}\ ,

(36)

with

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}:=\left[\left.\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\ \right|\ \bm{b}^{(\ell)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{(\ell)}\times[m_{(\ell-1)}+1]}\ ,\text{ and }\overline{\bm{y}}^{(\ell-1)}:=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}\\
1\end{bmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{[m_{(\ell-1)}+1]\times 1}\ .

(37)

The total number of parameters of a fully-connected feedforward network is then

\displaystyle P_{T}:=\sum_{\ell=1}^{\ell=L}m_{(\ell)}\times[m_{(\ell-1)}+1]\ .

(38)

But why using a linear (additive) combination (or superposition) of inputs with weights, plus biases, as expressed in Eq. (
30
) ? See Section
0.13.2
.

Activation functions

An activation function
a:{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
, which is a nonlinear real-valued function, is used to decide when the information in its argument is relevant for a neuron to activate.
In other words, an activation function filters out information deemed insignificant, and is applied
element-wise
to the matrix
\bm{z}^{(\ell)}
in Eq. (
30
), obtained as a linear combination of the inputs plus the biases:

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{(\ell)}=a(\bm{z}^{(\ell)})\text{ such that }y^{(\ell)}_{i}=a(z^{(\ell)}_{i})

(39)

Without the activation function, the neural network is simply a linear regression, and cannot learn and perform complex tasks, such as image classification, language translation, guiding a driver-less car, etc.
See Figure
32
for the block diagram of a one-layer network.

An example is a linear one-layer network, without activation function, being unable to represent the seemingly simple XOR (exclusive-or) function, which brought down the first wave of AI (cybernetics), and that is described in Section
0.4.5
.

Rectified linear units (ReLU).

Nowadays, for the choice of activation function
a(\cdot)
,
Most modern
large
deep-learning networks use the default,

66

66

“In modern neural networks,
The default recommendation is to use the rectified linear unit, or ReLU,”
[

]
, p. 168.

rectified linear function
(more often known as the ‘‘positive part’’ function) defined as

\displaystyle a(z)=z^{+}=[z]_{+}=\max(0,z)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ for }z\leq 0\\
z&\text{ for }0<z\end{cases}

(40)

Therefore, in this section, we discuss in detail the rectified linear function, with careful explanation and motivation.
It is important to note that ReLU is superior for
large

The reason for less computational effort with ReLU is due to (1) it being an identity map for positive argument, (2) zero for negative argument, and (3) its first derivative being the

Mathematically, a periodic function remains periodic after passing through a (nonlinear) rectifier (active function):

\displaystyle z(x+T)=z(x)\Longrightarrow y(x+T)=a(z(x+T))=a(z(x))=y(x)

(41)

where

z
.

Biological neurons encode and transmit information over long distance by generating (firing) electrical pulses called action potentials or spikes with a wide range of frequencies
[
19
]
, p. 1; see Figure
27
. “To reliably encode a wide range of signals, neurons need to achieve a broad range of firing frequencies and to move smoothly between low and high firing rates”
[
114
]
[
114
]
. From the neuroscientific standpoint, the rectified linear function could be motivated as an idealization of the “Type I” relation between the firing rate (F) of a biological neuron and the input current (I), called the FI curve. Figure
27
describes three types of FI curves, with Type I in the middle subfigure, where there is a continuous increase in the firing rate with increase in input current.

Figure 27
:

FI curves
(Sections
0.4.4
,
0.13.2
).
Firing rate frequency (F) versus applied depolarizing current (I), thus FI curves. Three types of FI curves. The time histories of voltage
V_{m}
provide a visualization of the spikes, current threshold, and spike firing rates. The applied (input) current
I_{app}
in increased gradually until it passes a current threshold, then the neuron begins to fire. Two input current levels (two black dots on FI curves at the bottom) near the current threshold are shown, with one just below the threshold (black-line time history for
I_{app}
) and and one just above the threshold (blue line). two corresponding histories of voltage
V_{m}
(flat black line, and blue line with spikes) are also shown. Type I displays a continuous increase in firing frequency from zero to higher values when the current continues to increase past the current threshold. Type II displays a discontinuity in firing frequency, with a sudden jump from zero to a finite frequency, when the current passes the threshold. At low concentration
\bar{g}_{A}
of potassium, the neuron exhibits Type-II FI curve, then transitions to Type-I FI curve as
\bar{g}_{A}
is increased, and returns to Type-II
⋆
for higher concentration
\bar{g}_{A}
. see
[
114
]
. the
scaled
rectified linear unit (scaled ReLU, Figure
25
and Figure
26
) can be viewed as approximating Type-I FI curve, see also Figure
28
and Eq. (
520
) where the FI curve is used in biological neuron firing-rate models.

. On the other hand for deep-learning networks, the above results are more than sufficient to motivate the use of ReLU, which has deep roots in neuroscience.

The Shockley equation for a current
I
going through a diode
\mathcal{D}
, in terms of the voltage
V_{D}
across the diode, is given in mathematical form as:

\displaystyle I=p\left[e^{qV_{D}}-1\right]\Longrightarrow\displaystyle V_{D}=\frac{1}{q}\log\left(\frac{I}{p}+1\right)\ .

(42)

With the voltage across the resistance being
V_{R}=RI
, the voltage across the diode and the resistance in series is then

\displaystyle-V=V_{D}+V_{R}=\displaystyle V_{D}=\frac{1}{q}\log\left(\frac{I}{p}+1\right)+RI\ ,

(43)

which is plotted in Figure
29
.

(a)

.

: Voltage

across resistance
\mathcal{R}
versus current
I
.

:

\mathcal{R}

+V

).

The rectified linear function could be seen from Figure
29
as a very rough approximation of the current-voltage relation in a halfwave rectifier circuit in Figure
26
, in which a diode and a resistance are in series.
In the Shockley model, the diode is leaky in the sense that there is a small amount of current flow when the polarity is reversed, unlike the case of an ideal diode or ReLU (Figure
24
), and is better modeled by the Leaky ReLU activation function, in which there is a small positive (instead of just flat zero) slope for negative
z
:

\displaystyle a(z)=\max(0.01z,z)=\begin{cases}0.01\,z&\text{ for }z\leq 0\\
z&\text{ for }0<z\end{cases}

(44)

Prior to the introduction of ReLU, which had been long widely used in neuroscience as activation function prior to 2011,
71
71

71

See, e.g.,
[
19
]
, p. 14, where ReLU was called the “half-wave rectification operation”, the meaning of which is explained above in Figure
26
.
The logistic sigmoid function (Figure
30
) was also used in neuroscience since the 1950s.

the state-of-the-art for deep-learning activation function was the hyperbolic tangent (Figure
31
), which performed better than the widely used, and much older, sigmoid function
72
72

72

See Section
0.13.3
for a history of the sigmoid function, which dated back at least to 1974 in neuroscience.

(Figure
30
); see
[
113
]
, in which it was reported that

“While logistic sigmoid neurons are more biologically plausible than hyperbolic tangent neurons, the latter work better for training multilayer neural networks. Rectifying neurons are an even better model of biological neurons and yield equal or better performance than hyperbolic tangent networks in spite of the hard non-linearity and non-differentiability at zero.”

Figure 30
:

Logistic sigmoid function
(Sections
0.4.4
,
0.5.1
,

0.5.3
,
0.13.3
):
\mathfrak{s}(z)=[1+\exp(-z)]^{-1}=[\tanh(z/2)+1]/2
(red), with the tangent at the origin
z=0
(blue).
See also Remark
0.5.3
and Figure
46
on the softmax function.

Figure 31
:

Hyperbolic tangent function
(Section
0.4.4
):

g(z)=\tanh(z)=2\mathfrak{s}(2z)-1
(red) and its tangent
g(z)=z
at the coordinate origin (blue), showing that this activation function is
identity
for small signals.

The hard non-linearity of ReLU is localized at zero, but otherwise ReLU is a very simple function—identity map for positive argument, zero for negative argument—making it highly efficient for computation.

Also, due to errors in numerical computation, it is rare to hit exactly zero, where there is a hard non-linearity in ReLU:

“In the case of
g(z)=\max({0,z})
, the left derivative at
z=0
is
0
, and the right derivative is
1
. Software implementations of neural network training usually return one of the one-sided derivatives rather than reporting that the derivative is undefined or raising an error. This may be heuristically justified by observing that gradient-based optimization on a digital computer is subject to numerical error anyway. When a function is asked to evaluate
g(0)
, it is very unlikely that the underlying value truly was
0
. Instead, it was likely to be some small value that was rounded to 0.”
[
78
]
, p. 186.

Thus, in addition to the ability to train deep networks, another advantage of using ReLU is the high efficiency in computing both the layer outputs and the gradients for use in optimizing the parameters (weights and biases) to lower cost or loss, i.e., training; see Section
0.6
on Training, and in particular Section
0.6.3
on Stochastic Gradient Descent.

The activation function ReLU approximates closer to how biological neurons work than other activation functions (e.g., logistic sigmoid, tanh, etc.), as it was established through experiments some sixty years ago, and have been used in neuroscience long (at least ten years) before being adopted in deep learning in 2011. Its use in deep learning is a clear influence from neuroscience; see Section
0.13.3
on the history of activation functions, and Section
0.13.3
on the history of the rectified linear function.

Deep-learning networks using ReLU mimic biological neural networks in the brain through a trade-off between two competing properties
[
113
]
:

(1)

Sparsity
.
Only 1% to 4% of brain neurons are active at any one point in time. Sparsity saves brain energy. In deep networks, ‘‘rectifying non-linearity gives rise to real zeros of activations and thus truly sparse representations.’’ Sparsity provides representation robustness in that the non-zero features
73
73

73

See the definition of image “predicate” or image “feature” in Section
0.13.2
, and in particular Footnote
303
.

would have small changes for small changes of the data.

(2)

Distributivity
.
Each feature of the data is represented distributively by many inputs, and each input is involved in distributively representing many features. Distributed representation is a key concept dated since the revival of connectionism with
[
119
]

[
120
]
and others; see Section
0.13.2
.

as the number of processing units (rows or neurons), and thus the width of this layer, representing the layer processing (input-to-output) function
f^{(\ell)}
in Figure
34
.

Artificial neuron

And finally, we now complete our
top-down
descent from the big picture of the overall multilayer neural network with
L
layers in Figure

, through Figure

for a typical layer
(\ell)
and Figure

for the lower-level details of layer
(\ell)
, then down to the most basic level, a neuron in Figure
36
as one row in layer
(\ell)
in Figure
35
.

:

(Sections

,

,
0.13.1
), row

in layer
(\ell)
in Figure

with
\bm{x}=[x_{1},x_{2},\cdots,x_{n}]^{T}

, and in

]
.

for the corresponding biological neuron in Section

on “Early inspiration from biological neurons”.

0.4.5
Representing XOR function with two-layer network

The XOR (exclusive-or) function played an important role in bringing down the first wave of AI, known as the cybernetics wave (
[
78
]
, p. 14) since
it was shown in
[
121
]

that Rosenblatt’s perceptron (1958
[
119
]
, 1962
[
120
]

[
2
]
) could not represent the XOR function, defined in Table
2
:

Table 2
:

Exclusive-or (XOR) function
(Section
0.4.5
) produces the
True
value only if two arguments are different. The symbol
\oplus
(“Oh-plus”) denotes the XOR operator. A concrete example of the XOR function would be that there is one and only one of two poker player would be the winner, and there is no tie possible, i.e., both players cannot win, and both cannot lose.

j

\bm{x}_{j}=[x_{1j},x_{2j}]^{T}

y_{i}=f(\bm{x}_{j})=x_{1j}\oplus x_{2j}
(XOR)

1

[0,0]^{T}

0

2

[1,0]^{T}

1

3

[0,1]^{T}

1

4

[1,1]^{T}

0

The dataset or design matrix
74
74

74

See
[
78
]
, p. 103.

\bm{X}
is the collection of the coordinates of all four points in Table
2
:

\displaystyle\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&1&0&1\\
0&0&1&1\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{x}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 1}

An approximation (or prediction) for the XOR function
y=f(\bm{x})
with
\bm{\theta}
parameters is denoted by
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.98613pt\hbox{${f}$}\hskip-2.98613pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.05559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}(\bm{x},\bm{\theta})
, with mean squared error (MSE) being:

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left[\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.98613pt\hbox{${f}$}\hskip-2.98613pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.05559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{\theta})-f(\bm{x}_{i})\right]^{2}=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left[\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{i}-y_{i}\right]^{2}

(47)

We begin with a one-layer network to show that it cannot represent the XOR function,
75
75

75

This one-layer network is not the Rosenblatt perceptron in Figure
132
due to the absence of the Heaviside function as activation function, and thus Section
0.4.5
is not the proof that the Rosenblatt perceptron cannot represent the XOR function. For such proof, see
[
121
]
.

then move on to a two-layer network, which can.

One-layer network

Consider the following one-layer network,
76
76

76

See
[
78
]
, p. 167.

in which the output

{y}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

is a linear combination of the coordinates
(x_{1},x_{2})
as inputs:

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=f(\bm{x},\bm{\theta})=f^{(1)}(\bm{x})=\bm{w}_{1}^{(1)}\bm{x}+b_{1}^{(1)}=\bm{w}\bm{x}+b=w_{1}x_{1}+w_{2}x_{2}+b\ ,

(48)

with the following matrices

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{1}^{(1)}=\bm{w}=\left[w_{1},w_{2}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 2}\ ,\quad\bm{x}=\left[x_{1},x_{2}\right]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 1}\ ,\quad b_{1}^{(1)}=b\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 1}

(49)

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}=\left[\theta_{1},\theta_{2},\theta_{3}\right]^{T}=\left[w_{1},w_{2},b\right]^{T}

(50)

since
it is written in
[
78
]
, p. 14:

“Model based on the
f(\bm{x},\bm{w})=\sum_{i}w_{i}x_{i}
used by the perceptron and ADALINE are called linear models. Linear models have many limitations… Most famously, they cannot learn the XOR function… Critics who observed these flaws in linear models caused a backlash against biologically inspired learning in general (Minsky and Papert, 1969). This was the first major dip in the popularity of neural networks.”

First-time learners, who have not seen the definition of
Rosenblatt’s (1958) perceptron
[
119
]
, could confuse Eq. (
48
) as the perceptron---which was not a linear model, but more importantly the Rosenblatt perceptron was a network with many neurons
77
77

77

See Section
0.13.2
on the history of the linear combination (weighted sum) of inputs with biases.
—because Eq. (
48
) is only a linear unit (a single neuron), and does not have an (nonlinear) activation function.
A neuron in the Rosenblatt perceptron is Eq. (
502
) in Section
0.13.2
, with the Heaviside (nonlinear step) function as activation function; see Figure
132
.

Figure 37
:

Representing XOR function
(Sections
0.4.5
,
0.13.2
). This one-layer network (which is not the Rosenblatt perceptron in Figure
132
) cannot perform this task. For each input matrix
\bm{x}_{i}
in the design matrix
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}
, with
i=1,\ldots,4
(see Table
2
), the linear unit (neuron)
z^{(1)}(\bm{x}_{i})=\bm{w}\bm{x}_{i}+b\in\mathbb{R}
in Eq. (
48
) predict a value
\widetilde{y}_{i}=z^{(1)}(\bm{x}_{i})
as output, which is collected in the output matrix
\widetilde{\bm{y}}=[\widetilde{y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}_{4}]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 4}
. The MSE cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
in Eq. (
47
) is used in a gradient descent to find the parameters
\bm{\theta}=[\bm{w},b]
. The result is a constant function,
\widetilde{y}_{i}=\frac{1}{2}
, for
i=1,\ldots,4
, which cannot represent the XOR function.

The MSE cost function in Eq. (
47
) becomes

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{4}\sum_{i=1}^{4}\left[b^{2}+(1-w_{1}-b)^{2}+(1-w_{2}-b)^{2}+(w_{1}+w_{2}+b)^{2}\right]^{2}

(51)

Setting the gradient of the cost function in Eq. (
51
) to zero and solving the resulting equations, we obtain the weights and the bias:

\displaystyle\nabla_{\bm{\theta}}J(\bm{\theta})=\left[\frac{\partial J}{\theta_{1}},\frac{\partial J}{\theta_{2}},\frac{\partial J}{\theta_{3}}\right]^{T}=\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial w_{1}},\frac{\partial J}{\partial w_{2}},\frac{\partial J}{\partial b}\right]^{T}

(52)

\displaystyle\left.\begin{array}[]{l}\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial w_{1}}=0\Longrightarrow 2w_{1}+w_{2}+2b=1\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial w_{2}}=0\Longrightarrow w_{1}+2w_{2}+2b=1\\
\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial b}=0\Longrightarrow w_{1}+w_{2}+2b=1\end{array}\right\}\Longrightarrow w_{1}=w_{2}=0,\quad b=\frac{1}{2}\ ,

from which the predicted output

{y}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

in Eq. (
48
) is a constant for any points in the dataset (or design matrix)
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}]
:

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{i}=f(\bm{x}_{i},\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}\ ,\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,4

(56)

and thus this one-layer network cannot represent the XOR function.
Eqs. (
0.4.5
) are called the ‘‘normal’’ equations.
78
78

78

In least-square linear regression, the normal equations are often presented in matrix form, starting from the errors (or residuals) at the data points, gathered in the matrix

\bm{e}=\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{\theta}
.
To minimize the squared of the errors represented by

\parallel\bm{e}\parallel^{2}
, consider a perturbation

\bm{\theta}_{\epsilon}=\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\gamma
and

\bm{e}_{\epsilon}=\bm{y}-\bm{X}\bm{\theta}_{\epsilon}
, then set the directional derivative of

\parallel\bm{e}\parallel^{2}
to zero, i.e.,

\left.\frac{d}{d\epsilon}\parallel\bm{e}_{\epsilon}\parallel^{2}\right|_{\epsilon=0}=\bm{X}^{T}(\bm{d}-\bm{X}\bm{\theta})=0
, which is the “normal equation” in matrix form, since the error matrix

\bm{e}
is required to be “normal” (orthogonal) to the span of

\bm{X}
.
For the above XOR function with four data points, the relevant matrices are (using the Matlab / Octave notation)

\bm{e}=[e_{1},e_{2},e_{3},e_{4}]^{T}
,

\bm{y}=[0,1,1,0]^{T}
, and

\bm{X}=[[0,0,1];[1,0,1];[0,1,1];[1,1,1]]
, which also lead to
Eq. (
0.4.5
).
See, e.g.,
[
122
]

[
123
]
and

[
78
]
, p. 106.

Two-layer network

The four points in Table
2
are not linearly separable, i.e., there is no straight line that separates these four points such that the value of the XOR function is zero for two points on one side of the line, and one for the two points on the other side of the line. One layer could not represent the XOR function, as shown above.
Rosenblatt (1958)

[
119
]
wrote:

“It has, in fact, been widely conceded by psychologists that there is little point in trying to ‘disprove’ any of the major learning theories in use today, since by extension, or a change in parameters, they have all proved capable of adapting to any specific empirical data. In considering this approach, one is reminded of a remark attributed to Kistiakowsky, that
‘given seven parameters, I could fit an elephant
.’ ”

So we now add a second layer, and thus more parameters in the hope to be able to represent the XOR function, as shown in Figure
38
.
79
79

79

Our presentation is more detailed and more general than in
[
78
]
, pp. 167-171, where there was no intuitive explanation of how the numbers were obtained, and where only the activation function ReLU was used.

Figure 38
:

Representing XOR function
(Sections
0.4.5
).
This two-layer network can perform this task. The four points in the design matrix
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}
(see Table
2
) are converted into three points that are linearly separable by the two nonlinear units (neurons or rows) of Layer (1), i.e.,
\bm{Y}^{(1)}=[\bm{y}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{y}_{1}^{(4)}]=f^{(1)}(\bm{X}^{(1)})=a(\bm{Z}^{(1)})=\bm{X}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}
, with
\bm{Z}^{(1)}=\bm{w}^{(1)}\bm{X}^{(1)}+\bm{B}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}
, as in Eq. (
0.4.5
), and
a(\cdot)
a nonlinear activation function.
Layer (2) consists of a single linear unit (neuron or row) with three parameters, i.e.,
\widetilde{\bm{y}}=[\widetilde{y}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}_{4}]=f^{(2)}(\bm{X}^{(2)})=\bm{w}_{1}^{(2)}\bm{X}^{(2)}+b_{1}^{(2)}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 4}
.
The three non-aligned points in
\bm{X}^{(2)}
offer three equations to solve for the three parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(2)}=[\bm{w}_{1}^{(2)},b_{1}^{(2)}]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 3}
; see Eq. (
74
).

Layer (1):
six parameters (4 weights, 2 biases), plus a (nonlinear) activation function. The purpose is to change coordinates to move the four input points of the XOR function into three points, such that the two points with XOR value equal 1 are coalesced into a single point, and such that these three points are aligned on a straight line. Since these three points remain not linearly separable, the activation function then moves these three points out of alignment, and thus linearly separable.

\displaystyle\bm{z}_{i}^{(1)}=\bm{w}_{i}^{(1)}\bm{x}_{i}+\bm{b}_{i}^{(1)}=\bm{w}^{(1)}\bm{x}_{i}+\bm{b}^{(1)}\ ,\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,4

(57)

\displaystyle\bm{w}_{i}^{(1)}=\bm{w}^{(1)}=\begin{bmatrix}1&1\\
1&1\end{bmatrix}\ ,\quad\bm{b}_{i}^{(1)}=\bm{b}^{(1)}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
-1\end{bmatrix}\ ,\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,4

(58)

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{(1)}=\left[\bm{z}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{z}_{4}^{(1)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ ,\quad\bm{X}^{(1)}=\left[\bm{x}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}^{(1)}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&1&0&1\\
0&0&1&1\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}

\displaystyle\bm{B}^{(1)}=\left[\bm{b}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{b}_{4}^{(1)}\right]=\left[\begin{array}[]{r r r r}0&0&0&0\\
-1&-1&-1&-1\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{(1)}=\bm{w}^{(1)}\bm{X}^{(1)}+\bm{B}^{(1)}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ .

(63)

To map the two points
\bm{x}_{2}=[1,0]^{T}
and
\bm{x}_{3}=[0,1]^{T}
, at which the XOR value is 1, into a single point, the two rows of
\bm{w}^{(1)}
are selected to be identically
[1,1]
as shown in Eq. (
58
). The first term in Eq. (
63
) yields three points aligned along the bisector in the first quadrant (i.e., the line
z_{2}=z_{1}
in the z-plane), with all positive coordinates, Figure
39
:

\displaystyle\bm{w}^{(1)}\bm{X}^{(1)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&1&1&2\\
0&1&1&2\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ .

Figure 39
:

Two-layer network for XOR representation
(Sections
0.4.5
).

Left
: XOR function, with

A=\bm{x}_{1}^{(1)}=[0,0]^{T}
,
B=\bm{x}_{2}^{(1)}=[0,1]^{T}
,

C=\bm{x}_{3}^{(1)}=[1,0]^{T}
,
D=\bm{x}_{4}^{(1)}=[1,1]^{T}
; see Eq. (
0.4.5
).
The XOR value for the solid red dots is 1, and for the open blue dots 0.

Right
: Images of points
A,B,C,D
in the
z
-plane due
only
to the first term of
Eq. (
63
),
i.e.,
\bm{w}^{(1)}\bm{X}^{(1)}
, which is shown in Eq. (
0.4.5
).
See also Figure
40
.

For activation functions such as ReLu or Heaviside
80
80

80

In general, the Heaviside function is not used as activation function since its gradient is zero, and thus would not work for gradient descent. But for this XOR problem
without
using gradient descent, the Heaviside function offers a workable solution as the rectified linear function.

to have any effect, the above three points are next translated in the negative
z_{2}
direction using the biases in Eq. (
0.4.5
), so that Eq. (
63
) yields:

\displaystyle\bm{Z}^{(1)}=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&1&1&2\\
-1&0&0&1\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ ,

and thus

\displaystyle\bm{Y}^{(1)}=\left[\bm{y}_{1}^{(1)},\ldots,\bm{y}_{4}^{(1)}\right]=a(\bm{Z}^{(1)})=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}0&1&1&2\\
0&0&0&1\end{array}\right]=\bm{X}^{(2)}=\left[\bm{x}_{1}^{(2)},\ldots,\bm{x}_{4}^{(2)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ ,\hfill

For general activation function
a(\cdot)
, the outputs of Layer (1) are:

\displaystyle\bm{Y}^{(1)}=\bm{X}^{(2)}=a(\bm{Z}^{(1)})=\left[\begin{array}[]{c c c c}a(0)&a(1)&a(1)&a(2)\\
a(-1)&a(0)&a(0)&a(1)\end{array}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{2\times 4}\ .

Layer (2):
three parameters (2 weights, 1 bias), no activation function. Eq. (
72
) for this layer is identical to Eq. (
48
) for the one-layer network above, with the output
\bm{y}^{(1)}
of Layer (1) as input
\bm{x}^{(2)}=\bm{y}^{(1)}
, as shown in Eq. (
0.4.5
):

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{j}=f(\bm{x}_{j}^{(2)},\bm{\theta}^{(2)})=f^{(2)}(\bm{x}_{j}^{(2)})=\bm{w}_{1}^{(2)}\bm{x}_{j}^{(2)}+b_{1}^{(2)}=\bm{w}\bm{x}_{j}^{(2)}+b=w_{1}x_{1j}^{(2)}+w_{2}x_{2j}^{(2)}+b\ ,

(72)

with three distinct points in Eq. (
0.4.5
), because
\bm{x}_{2}^{(2)}=\bm{x}_{3}^{(2)}=[1,0]^{T}
, to solve for these three parameters:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{(2)}=[\bm{w}_{1}^{(2)},b_{1}^{(2)}]=[w_{1},w_{2},b]

(73)

We have three equations:

\displaystyle\begin{bmatrix}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{1}\\
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{2}\\
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}a(0)&a(-1)&1\\
a(1)&a(0)&1\\
a(2)&a(1)&1\end{bmatrix}\begin{bmatrix}w_{1}\\
w_{2}\\
b\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}y_{1}\\
y_{2}\\
y_{4}\end{bmatrix}=\begin{bmatrix}0\\
1\\
0\end{bmatrix}\ ,

(74)

for which the exact analytical solution for the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(2)}
is easy to obtain, but the expressions are rather lengthy. Hence, here we only give the numerical solution for
\bm{\theta}^{(2)}
in the case of the logistic sigmoid function in Table
3
.

We conjecture that any (nonlinear) function
a(\cdot)
in the zoo of activation functions listed, e.g., in “Activation function”, Wikipedia,

or in

]
(see Figure

\bm{\theta}^{(2)}
for Eq. (

).

Remark 0.4.4
.

Number of parameters.

In 1953, Physicist Freeman Dyson (Princeton Institute of Advanced Study) once consulted with Nobel Laureate Enrico Fermi about a new mathematical model for a difficult physics problem that Dyson and his students had just developed. Fermi asked Dyson how many parameters they had. “Four”, Dyson replied. Fermi then gave his now famous comment “I remember my friend Johnny von Neumann used to say, with four parameters I can fit an elephant, and with five I can make him wiggle his trunk”
[
124
]
.

But it was only more than sixty years later that physicists were able to plot an elephant in 2-D using a model with four complex numbers as parameters
[
125
]
[
125
]
.

With nine parameters, the elephant can be made to walk (representing the XOR function), and with a billion parameters, it may even perform some acrobatic maneuver in 3-D; see Section
0.4.6
on depth of multilayer networks.

\hfill\blacksquare

0.4.6
What is “deep” in “deep networks” ? Size, architecture

Depth, size

The concept of network depth turns out to be more complex than initially thought.
While for a
fully-connected
feedforward neural network (in which all outputs of a layer are connected to a neuron in the following layer), depth could be considered as the number of layers, there is in general no consensus on the accepted definition of depth.
It was stated in
[
78
]
, p. 8, that:
81
81

81

There are two viewpoints on the definition of depth, one based on the computational graph, and one based on the conceptual graph. From the computational-graph viewpoint, depth is the number of sequential instructions that must be executed in an architecture. From the conceptual-graph viewpoint, depth is the number of concept levels, going from simple concepts to more complex concepts.
See also
[
78
]
, p. 163, for the depth of fully-connected feedforward networks as the “length of the chain” in Eq. (
22
) and Eq. (
27
), which is the number of layers.

‘‘There is no single correct value for the depth of an architecture,
82
82

82

There are several different network architectures.
Convolutional neural networks
(CNN) use sparse connections, have achieved great success in image recognition, and contributed to the burst of interest in deep learning since winning the ImageNet competion in 2012 by almost halving the image classification error rate; see

[
13
,
12
,
75
]
.

Recurrent neural networks
(RNN) are used to process a sequence of inputs to a system with changing states as in a dynamical system, to be discussed in Section
0.7
. there are other networks with skip connections, in which information flows from layer
(\ell)
to layer
(\ell+2)
, skipping layer
(\ell+1)
; see
[
78
]
, p. 196.

just as there is no single correct value for the length of a computer Program. Nor is there a consensus about how much depth a model requires to Qualify as ‘deep.’ ”

For example, keeping the number of layers the same, then the “depth” of a
sparsely-connected
feedforward network (in which not all outputs of a layer are connected to a neuron in the following layer) should be smaller than the “depth” of a
fully-connected
feedforward network.

The lack of consensus on the boundary between “shallow” and “deep” networks is echoed in
[
12
]
:

“At which problem depth does
Shallow Learning
end, and
Deep Learning
begin? Discussions with DL experts have not yet yielded a conclusive response to this question. Instead of committing myself to a precise answer, let me just define for the purposes of this overview: problems of depth
>
10 require
Very Deep Learning
.”

Remark 0.4.5
.

Action depth, state depth.

In view of Remark
0.4.3
, which type of layer (action or state) were they talking about in the above quotation? We define here
action depth
as the number of action layers, and
state depth
as the number of state layers. The abstract network in Figure
23
has action depth
L
and state depth
(L+1)
, with
(L-1)
as the number of hidden (state) layers.

\hfill\blacksquare

The review paper
[
13
]
was attributed in
[
38
]
for stating that “training neural networks with more than three hidden layers is called deep learning”, implying that a network is considered “deep” if its number of hidden (state) layers

(L-1)>3
.
In the work reported in
[
38
]
, the authors
used networks with number of hidden (state) layers
(L-1)
varying from one to five, and with a constant hidden (state) layer width of
m_{(\ell)}=50
, for all hidden (state) layers
\ell=1,\ldots,5
; see Table 1 in

[
38
]
,
reproduced in Figure
99
in Section
0.10.2
.

An example of recognizing multidigit numbers in photographs of addresses, in which the test accuracy increased (or test error decreased) with increasing depth, is provided in
[
78
]
, p. 196; see Figure
41
.

Figure 41
:

Test accuracy versus network depth
(Section

[

]
, p. 196.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

But it is not clear where
in
[
13
]
that it was actually said that a network is “deep” if the number of hidden (state) layers is greater than three.
An example in image recognition having more than three layers was, however, given in
[
13
]
(emphases are ours):

“An image, for example, comes in the form of an array of pixel values, and the learned features in the
first layer
of representation typically represent the presence or absence of edges at particular orientations and locations in the image. The
second layer
typically detects motifs by spotting particular arrangements of edges, regardless of small variations in the edge positions. The
third layer
may assemble motifs into larger combinations that correspond to parts of familiar objects, and
subsequent layers
would detect objects as combinations of these parts.”

But the above was not a criterion for a network to be considered as “deep”.
It was further noted on the number of the model parameters (weights and biases) and the size of the training dataset for a “typical deep-learning system” as follows
[
13
]
(emphases are ours):

“ In a typical
deep-learning
system, there may be

hundreds of millions
of these adjustable
weights
, and
hundreds of millions
of labelled examples with which to train the machine.”

See Remark
0.7.2
on recurrent neural networks (RNNs) as equivalent to “very deep feedforward networks”.
Another example was also provided in
[
13
]
:

‘‘Recent ConvNet [convolutional neural network, or CNN]
83
83

83

A special type of deep network that went out of favor, then now back in favor, among the computer-vision and machine-learning communities after the spectacular success that ConvNet garnered at the 2012 ImageNet competition; see
[

]

[

]

[

. Since we are reviewing in detail some specific applications of deep networks to computational mechanics, we will not review ConvNet here, but focus on MultiLayer Neural (MLN)—also known as MultiLayer Perceptron (MLP)—networks.

architectures have 10 to 20 layers of ReLUs [rectified linear units], hundreds of millions of weights, and billions of connections between Units.’’
84
84

84

A network processing “unit” is also called a “neuron”.

A neural network with 160 billion parameters was perhaps the largest in 2015
[
126
]
:

“Digital Reasoning, a cognitive computing company based in Franklin, Tenn., recently announced that it has trained a neural network consisting of 160 billion parameters—more than 10 times larger than previous neural networks.

The Digital Reasoning neural network easily surpassed previous records held by Google’s 11.2-billion parameter system and Lawrence Livermore National Laboratory’s 15-billion parameter system.”

As mentioned above, for general network architectures (other than feedforward networks), not only that there is no consensus on the definition of depth, there is also no consensus on how much depth a network must have to qualify as being “deep”; see
[
78
]
, p. 8, who offered the following intentionally vague definition:

“Deep learning can be safely regarded as the study of models that involve a greater amount of composition of either learned functions or learned concepts than traditional machine learning does.”

Figure

.
In

Architecture

The architecture of a network is the number of layers (depth), the layer width (number of neurons per layer), and the connection among the neurons.
85
85

85

See
[
78
]
, p. 166.

We have seen the architecture of fully-connected feedforward neural networks above; see Figure
23
and Figure
35
.

One example of an architecture different from that fully-connected feedforward networks is convolutional neural networks, which are based on the convolutional integral (see Eq. (
512
) in Section
0.13.2
on “Dynamic, time dependence, Volterra series”), and which had proven to be successful long before deep-learning networks:

“Convolutional networks were also some of the first neural networks to solve important commercial applications and remain at the forefront of commercial applications of deep learning today. By the end of the 1990s, this system deployed by NEC was reading over 10 percent of all the checks in the United States. Later, several OCR and handwriting recognition systems based on convolutional nets were deployed by Microsoft.”

[
78
]
, p. 360.

“Fully-connected networks were believed not to work well. It may be that the primary barriers to the success of neural networks were psychological (practitioners did not expect neural networks to work, so they did not make a serious effort to use neural networks).
Whatever the case, it is fortunate that convolutional networks performed well decades ago. In many ways, they carried the torch for the rest of deep learning and paved the way to the acceptance of neural networks in general.”

[
78
]
, p. 361.

Here, we present a more recent and successful network architecture different from the fully-connected feedforward network.
Residual network was introduced in
[
127
]
to address the problem of vanishing gradient that plagued “very deep” networks with as few as 16 layers during training (see Section
0.5
on Backpropagation) and the problem of increased training error and test error with increased network depth as shown in Figure
43
.

Figure 43
:

Training/test error vs. iterations, depth

(Sections
0.4.6
,
0.6
).
The training error and test error of deep fully-connected networks increased when the number of layers (depth) increased
[
127
]
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Remark 0.4.6
.

Training error, test (generalization) error.

Using a set of data, called training data, to find the parameters that minimize the loss function (i.e., doing the training) provides the training error, which is the least square error between the predicted outputs and the training data. Then running the optimally trained model on a different set of data, which was not been used for the training, called test data, provides the test error, also known as generalization error. More details can be found in Section
0.6
, and in
[
78
]
, p. 107.

\hfill\blacksquare

The basic building block of residual network is shown in Figure
44
, and a full residual network in Figure
45
.

Figure 44
:

Residual network
(Sections
0.4.6
,
0.6
), basic building block having two layers with the rectified linear activation function (ReLU), for which the input is
\bm{x}
, the output is
\mathcal{H}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{F}(\bm{x})+\bm{x}
, where the internal mapping function
\mathcal{F}(\bm{x})=\mathcal{H}(\bm{x})-\bm{x}
is called the residual. Chaining this building block one after another forms a deep residual network; see Figure
45

[
127
]
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Figure 45
:

Full residual network
(Sections
0.4.6
,
0.6
) with 34 layers, made up from 16 building blocks with two layers each (Figure
44
), together with an input and an output layer. This residual network has a total of 3.6 billion floating-point operations (FLOPs with fused multiply-add operations), which could be considered as the network “computational depth”
[
127
]
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

The rationale for residual networks was that, if the identity map were optimal, it would be easier for the optimization (training) process to drive the residual
\mathcal{F}(\bm{x})
down to zero than to fit the identity map with a bunch of nonlinear layers; see
[
127
]
, where it was mentioned that deep residual networks won 1st places in several image recognition competitions.

Remark 0.4.7
.

The identity map that jumps over a number of layers in the residual network building block in Figure
44
and in the full residual network in Figure
45
is based on a concept close to that for the path of the cell state
\bm{c}^{[k]}
in the Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) unit for recurrent neural networks (RNN), as described in Figure
81
in Section
0.7.2
.

\hfill\blacksquare

A deep residual network with more than 1,200 layers was proposed in
[
128
]
.
A wide residual-network architecture that outperformed deep and thin networks was proposed in
[
129
]
:
“For instance, [their] wide 16-layer deep network has the same accuracy as a 1000-layer thin deep network and a comparable number of parameters, although being several times faster to train.”

It is still not clear why some architecture worked well, while others did not:

“The design of hidden units is an extremely active area of research and does not yet have many definitive guiding theoretical principles.”

[
78
]
, p. 186.

0.5
Backpropagation

Backpropagation, sometimes abbreviated as “backprop”, was a child
of whom many could claim to be the father,
and is used to compute the gradient of the cost function with respect to the parameters (weights and biases); see Section
0.13.4
for a history of backpropagation. This gradient is then subsequently used in an optimization process, usually the Stochastic Gradient Descent method, to find the parameters that minimize the cost or loss function.

0.5.1
Cost (loss, error) function

Two types of cost function are discussed here: (1) the mean squared error (MSE), and (2) the maximum likelihood (probability cost).
86
86

86

For other types of loss function, see, e.g., (1) Section “Loss functions” in “torch.nn — PyTorch Master Documentation” (
Original website
,
Internet archive
), and (2) Jah 2019, A Brief Overview of Loss Functions in Pytorch (
Original website
,
Internet archive
).

Mean squared error

For a given input
\bm{x}
(a single example) and target output
\bm{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
, the squared error (SE) of the predicted output
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\in\mathbb{R}^{m}
for use in least squared error problem is defined as half the squared error:

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{SE}=\frac{1}{2}\parallel\bm{y}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\parallel^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=1}^{k=m}\left(y_{k}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{k}\right)^{2}\ .

(75)

The factor
\frac{1}{2}
is for the convenience of avoiding to carry the factor
2
when taking the gradient of the cost (or loss) function
J
.
87
87

87

There is an inconsistent use of notation in
[
78
]
that could cause confusion, e.g., in
[
78
]
, Chap. 5, p. 104, Eq. (5.4), the notation
\widehat{\bm{y}}
(with the hat) was defined as the network outputs, i.e., predicted values, with
\bm{y}
(without the hat) as target values, whereas later in Chap. 6, p. 163, the notation
\bm{y}
(without the hat) was used for the network outputs.
Also,
in
[
78
]
, p. 105,
the cost function was defined as the mean squared error, without the factor
\frac{1}{2}
.
See also Footnote
52
.

While the components
y_{k}
on the output matrix
\bm{y}
cannot be
independent and identically distributed
(
i.i.d.
), since
\bm{y}
must represent a recognizable pattern (e.g., an image), in the case of training with
m
examples as inputs:
88
88

88

In our notation,
m
is the dimension of the output array
\bm{y}
, whereas
m
(in a different font) is here the number of examples in Eq. (
76
), and
later represents the minibatch size in Eqs. (
149
)-(
151
). The size of the whole training set, called the “full batch” (Footnote
117
), is denoted by
M
(Footnote
144
).

\displaystyle\mathbb{X}=\{\bm{x}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|\text{m}|}\}\text{ and }\mathbb{Y}=\{\bm{y}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{y}^{|\text{m}|}\}

(76)

where
\mathbb{X}
is the set of
m
examples, and
\mathbb{Y}
the set of the corresponding outputs, the examples
\bm{x}^{|k|}
can be
i.i.d.
, and the half MSE cost function for these outputs is half the expectation of the SE:

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{MSE}=\frac{1}{2}\,\mathbb{E}(\{SE^{|k|},k=1,\cdots,\text{m}\})=\frac{1}{2\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}\ .

(77)

Maximum likelihood (probability cost)

Many (if not most) modern networks employed a probability cost function based in the principle of maximum likelihood, which has the form of negative log-likelihood, describing the cross-entropy between the training data with probability distribution
\hat{p}_{data}
and the model with probability distribution
p_{model}
(
[
78
]
, p. 173):

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=-\mathbb{E}_{{\textup{\bf x}},{\textup{\bf y}}\sim\hat{p}_{data}}\log p_{model}(\bm{y}\,|\,\bm{x};\bm{\theta})\ ,

(78)

where
\mathbb{E}
is the expectation;
x
and
y
are random variables for training data with distribution
\hat{p}_{data}
; the inputs
\bm{x}
and the target outputs
\bm{y}
are values of
x
and
y
, respectively, and
p_{model}
is the conditional probability of the distribution of the target outputs
\bm{y}
given the inputs
\bm{x}
and the parameters
\bm{\theta}
, with the predicted outputs

{\bm{y}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

given by the model
f
(neural network), having as arguments the inputs
\bm{x}
and the parameters
\bm{\theta}
:

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=f(\bm{x},\bm{\theta})\Leftrightarrow\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{k}=f_{k}(\bm{x},\bm{\theta})

(79)

The expectations of a function
g(\textup{x}=x)
of a random variable
x
, having a probability distribution
P(\textup{x}=x)
for the discrete case, and the probability distribution density
p(\textup{x}=x)
for the continuous case, are respectively
89
89

89

The simplified notation
\langle\cdot\rangle
for expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot)
, with implied probability distribution, is used in Section
0.6.3
on step-length decay and simulated annealing (Remark
0.6.9
, Section
0.6.3
) as an add-on improvement to the stochastic gradient descent algorithm.

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{{\textup{\bf x}}\sim{P}}\,g(x)=\sum_{x}g(x)P(x)=\langle g(x)\rangle\ ,\quad\mathbb{E}_{{\textup{\bf x}}\sim{p}}\,g(x)=\int g(x)p(x)dx=\langle g(x)\rangle\ .

(80)

Remark 0.5.1
.

Information content, Shannon entropy, maximum likelihood.

The expression in Eq. (
78
)—with the minus sign and the log function—can be abstract to readers not familiar with the probability concept of maximum likelihood, which is related to the concepts of information content and Shannon entropy. First, an event
x
with low probability (e.g., an asteroid will hit the Earth tomorrow) would have higher information content than an event with high probability (e.g., the sun will rise tomorrow morning). since the probability of
x
, i.e.,
P(x)
, is between 0 and 1, the negative of the logarithm of
P(x)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle I(x)=-\log P(x)\ ,

(81)

called the information content of
x
, would have large values near zero, and small values near 1. In addition, the probability of two independent events to occur is the product of the probabilities of these events, e.g., the probability of having two heads in two coin tosses is

\displaystyle P(\textup{x}=\text{head},\textup{y}=\text{head})=P(\text{head})\times P(\text{head})=\frac{1}{2}\times\frac{1}{2}=\frac{1}{4}

(82)

The product (chain) rule of conditional probabilities consists of expressing a joint probability of several random variables
\{\bm{\textup{x}}^{|1|},\bm{\textup{x}}^{|2|},\cdots,\bm{\textup{x}}^{|n|}\}
as the product
90
90

90

See, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 57.
The notation
\bm{\textup{x}}^{|k|}
(with vertical bars enclosing the superscript
k
) is used to designate example
k
in the set
\mathbb{X}
of examples in Eq. (
86
), instead of the notation
\bm{x}^{(k)}
(with parentheses), since the parentheses were already used to surround the layer number
k
, as in Figure
35
.

\displaystyle P(\bm{\textup{x}}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{\textup{x}}^{|n|})=P(\bm{\textup{x}}^{|1|})\prod_{k=2}^{k=n}P(\bm{\textup{x}}^{|k|}|\bm{\textup{x}}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{\textup{x}}^{|k-1|})

(83)

The logarithm of the products in Eq. (
82
) and Eq. (
83
) is the sum of the factor probabilities, and provides another reason to use the logarithm in the expression for information content in Eq. (
81
): Independent events have additive information. Concretely, the information content of two asteroids independently hitting the Earth should double that of one asteroid hitting the Earth.

The parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}
that minimize the probability cost
J(\bm{\theta})
in Eq. (
78
) can be expressed as
91
91

91

A tilde is put on top of
\bm{\theta}
to indicate that the matrix
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}
contains the estimated values of the parameters (weights and biases), called the estimates, not the true parameters. Recall from Footnote
87
that
[
78
]
used an overhead “hat” (
\hat{\cdot}
) to indicate predicted value; see
[
78
]
, p. 120, where
\bm{\theta}
is defined as the true parameters, and
\hat{\bm{\theta}}
the predicted (or estimated) parameters.

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}

\displaystyle=-\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}\mathbb{E}_{{\textup{\bf x}},{\textup{\bf y}}\sim\hat{p}_{data}}\log p_{model}(\bm{y}\,|\,\bm{x};\bm{\theta})=\arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\log p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})

(84)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}\log\prod_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}P_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})=\arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}p_{model}(\mathbb{Y}\,|\,\mathbb{X};\bm{\theta})

(85)

\displaystyle\text{with }\mathbb{X}=\{\bm{x}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|\text{m}|}\}\text{ and }\mathbb{Y}=\{\bm{y}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{y}^{|\text{m}|}\}\ ,

(86)

where
\mathbb{X}
is the set of
m
examples that are
independent and identically distributed
(
i.i.d.
), and
\mathbb{Y}
the set of the corresponding outputs.
The final form in Eq. (
85
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}=\arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}p_{model}(\mathbb{Y}\,|\,\mathbb{X};\bm{\theta})

(87)

is called the
Principle of Maximum Likelihood
, in which the model parameters are optimized to maximize the likelihood to reproduce the empirical data.
92
92

92

See, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 128.

\hfill\blacksquare

Remark 0.5.2
.

Relation between Mean Squared Error and Maximum Likelihood.

The MSE is a particular case of the Maximum Likelihood.
Consider having
m
examples
\mathbb{X}=\{\bm{x}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|\text{m}|}\}
that are independent and identically distributed (
i.i.d.
), as in Eq. (
76
).
If the model probability
p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})
has a normal distribution, with the predicted output

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}=f(\bm{x}^{|k|},\bm{\theta})

(88)

as in Eq. (
79
), predicting the mean of this normal distribution,
93
93

93

The normal (Gaussian) distribution of scalar random variable
x
, mean
\mu
, and variance
\sigma^{2}
is written as

{\mathcal{N}}(x;\mu,\sigma^{2})=(2\pi\sigma^{2})^{-1/2}\exp[-(x-\mu)^{2}/(2\sigma^{2})]
; see, e.g.,
[
130
]
, p. 24.

then

\displaystyle p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{y}^{|k|};f(\bm{x}^{|k|},\bm{\theta}),\sigma^{2})=\mathcal{N}(\bm{y}^{|k|};\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|},\sigma^{2})=\frac{1}{[2\pi\sigma^{2}]^{\frac{1}{2}}}\exp\left[-\frac{\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\right]\ ,

(89)

with
\sigma
designating the standard deviation,
i.e., the error between the target output
\bm{y}^{|k|}
and the predicted output
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}
is normally distributed.
By taking the negative of the logarithm of
p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})
, we have

\displaystyle\log p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^{2})-\frac{\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\ .

(90)

Then summing Eq. (
90
) over all examples
k=1,\cdots,\text{m}
as in the last expression in Eq. (
84
) yields

\displaystyle\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\log p_{model}(\bm{y}^{|k|}\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})=\frac{\text{m}}{2}\log(2\pi\sigma^{2})-\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\frac{\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}\ ,

(91)

and thus the minimizer
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}
in Eq. (
84
) can be written as

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}=-\arg\max_{\bm{\theta}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\frac{\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}=\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\frac{\parallel\bm{y}^{|k|}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}\parallel^{2}}{2\sigma^{2}}=\arg\min_{\bm{\theta}}J(\bm{\theta})\ ,

(92)

where the MSE cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
was defined in Eq. (
77
), noting that constants such as
m
or
2m
do not affect the value of the minimizer
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}
.

Thus finding the minimizer of the maximum likelihood cost function in Eq. (
78
) is the same as finding the minimizer of the MSE in Eq. (
75
); see also
[
78
]
, p. 130.

\hfill\blacksquare

Remark
0.5.2
justifies the use of Mean Squared Error as
a
Maximum Likelihood estimator.
94
94

94

See, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 130.

For the purpose of this review paper, it is sufficient to use the MSE cost function in Eq. (
47
) to develop the backpropagation procedure.

Classification loss function

In classification tasks—such as used
in

[
38
]
, Section
0.10.2
, and Footnote
266
—a neural network is trained to predict which of
k
different classes (categories) an input
\bm{x}
belongs to.
The most simple classification problem only has two classes (
k=2
), which can be represented by the values
\{0,1\}
of a single binary variable
y
.
The probability distribution of such single boolean-valued variable is called
Bernoulli distribution
.
95
95

95

See, e.g.,
[
130
]
, p. 68.

The Bernoulli distribution is characterized by a single parameter
p(y=1\,|\,\bm{x})
, i.e., the conditional probability of
\bm{x}
belonging to the class
y=1
.
To perform binary classification, a neural network is therefore trained to estimate the conditional probability distribution
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=p(y=1\,|\,\bm{x})
using the principle of maximum likelihood (see Section
0.5.1
, Eq. (
78
)):

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})

\displaystyle=-\mathbb{E}_{{\textup{\bf x}},{\textup{\bf y}}\sim\hat{p}_{data}}\log p_{model}(y\,|\,\bm{x};\bm{\theta})

(93)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\left\{y^{|k|}\log p_{model}(y^{|k|}=1\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta})+(1-y^{|k|})\log(1-p_{model}(y^{|k|}=1\,|\,\bm{x}^{|k|};\bm{\theta}))\right\}.

The output of the neural network is supposed to represent the probability
p_{model}(y=1\,|\,\bm{x};\bm{\theta})
, i.e., a real-valued number in the interval
[0,1]
.
A linear output layer
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=y^{(L)}=z^{(L)}=\bm{W}^{(L)}\bm{y}^{(L-1)}+b^{(L)}
does not meet this constraint in general.
To squash the output of the linear layer into the range of
[0,1]
, the logistic sigmoid function
\mathfrak{s}
(see Figure
30
) can be added to the linear output unit to render
z^{(L)}
a probability

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=y^{(L)}=a(z^{(L)}),\qquad a(z^{(L)})=\mathfrak{s}(z^{(L)}).

(94)

In case more than two categories occur in a classification problem, a neural network is trained to estimate the probability distribution over the discrete number (
k>2
) of classes.
Such distribution is referred to as
multinoulli
or
categorial
distribution, which is parameterized by the conditional probabilities
p_{i}=p(y=i\,|\,\bm{x})\in[0,1],i=1,\ldots,k
of an input
\bm{x}
belonging to the
i
-th category. The output of the neural network accordingly is a
k
-dimensional vector
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times 1}
, where
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{i}=p(y=i\,|\,\bm{x})
.
In addition to the requirement of each component
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{i}
being in the range
[0,1]
, we must also guarantee that all components sum up to 1 to satisfy the definition of a probability distribution.

For this purpose, the idea of
exponentiation and normalization
, which can be expressed as a change of variable in the logistic sigmoid function
\mathfrak{s}
(Figure
30
, Section
0.5.3
), as in the following example
[
78
]
, p. 177:

\displaystyle p(y)=\mathfrak{s}[(2y-1)z]=\frac{1}{1+\exp[-(2y-1)z]}\ ,\text{ with }y\in\{0,1\}\text{ and }z=\text{constant}\ ,

(95)

\displaystyle p(0)+p(1)=\mathfrak{s}(-z)+\mathfrak{s}(z)=\frac{1}{1+\exp(z)}+\frac{1}{1+\exp(-z)}=1\ ,

(96)

and is generalized then to vector-valued outputs; see also Figure
46
.

The
softmax function
converts the vector formed by a linear unit
\bm{z}^{(L)}=\bm{W}^{(L)}\bm{y}^{(L-1)}+\bm{b}^{(L)}\in\mathbb{R}^{k}
into the vector of probabilities

{\bm{y}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

by means of

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{i}=(\softmax\bm{z}^{(L)})_{i}=\frac{\exp z_{i}^{(L)}}{\sum_{j=1}^{j=k}\exp z_{j}^{(L)}}\ ,

(97)

and is a smoothed version of the max function
[
130
]
, p. 198.
96
96

96

See also
[
78
]
, p. 179 and p. 78, where the softmax function is used to stabilize against the underflow and overflow problem in numerical computation.

Figure 46
:

Sofmax function for two classes, logistic sigmoid
(Section
0.5.1
,
0.5.3
):

\mathfrak{s}(z)=[1+\exp(-z)]^{-1}
and
\mathfrak{s}(-z)=[1+\exp(z)]^{-1}
, such that
\mathfrak{s}(z)+\mathfrak{s}(-z)=17
.
See also Figure
30
.

Remark 0.5.3
.

Softmax function from Bayes’ theorem.

For a classification with multiple classes
\{{\mathcal{C}}_{k}{},k=1,\ldots,K\}
, particularized to the case of two classes with
K=2
, the probability for class
{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{}
, given the input column matrix
\bm{x}
, is obtained from Bayes’ theorem
97
97

97

Since the probability of
x
and
y
is
p(x,y)=p(y,x)
, and since
p(x,y)=p(x|y)p(y)
(which is the product rule), where
p(x|y)
is the probability of
x
given
y
, we have
p(x|y)p(y)=p(y|x)p(x)
, and thus
p(y|x)=p(x|y)p(y)/p(x)
. The sum rule is
p(x)=\sum_{y}p(x,y)
. See, e.g.,
[
130
]
, p. 15.
The right-hand side of the second equation in Eq. (
98
)
2
makes common sense in terms of the predator-prey problem, in which
p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})
would be the percentage of predator in the total predator-prey population, and
p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})
the percentage of prey, as the self-proclaimed “best mathematician of France” Laplace said “probability theory is nothing but common sense reduced to calculation”
[
130
]
, p. 24.

as follows (
[
130
]
, p. 197):

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{C}}_{1}{}|\bm{x})=\frac{p(\bm{x}|{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})p({\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})}{p(\bm{x})}=\frac{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})}{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})+p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})}=\frac{\exp(z)}{\exp(z)+1}=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-z)}=\mathfrak{s}(z)\ ,

(98)

\displaystyle\text{ with }z:=\ln\frac{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})}{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})}\Rightarrow\frac{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})}{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})}=\exp(z)\ ,

(99)

where the product rule was applied to the numerator of Eq. (
98
)
2
, the sum rule to the denominator, and
\mathfrak{s}
the logistic sigmoid.
Likewise,

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{C}}_{2}{}|\bm{x})=\frac{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})}{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{1}{})+p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{2}{})}=\frac{1}{\exp(z)+1}=\mathfrak{s}(-z)

(100)

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{C}}_{1}{}|\bm{x})+p({\mathcal{C}}_{2}{}|\bm{x})=\mathfrak{s}(z)+\mathfrak{s}(-z)=1\ ,

(101)

as in Eq. (
96
), and
\mathfrak{s}
is also called a normalized exponential or softmax function for
K=2
. Using the same procedure, for
K>2
, the softmax function (version 1) can be written as
98
98

98

See also
[
130
]
, p. 115, version 1 of softmax function, i.e.,
\mu_{k}=\sum\exp(\eta_{k})/[1+\sum_{j}\exp\eta_{j}]
and
\sum_{k}\mu_{k}\leq 1
, had “1” as a summand in the denominator, similar to Eq. (
102
) while version 2 did not, similar to Eq. (
103
)
[
130
]
, p. 198, and was the same as Eq. (
97
).

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{C}}_{i}{}|\bm{x})=[{1+\sum_{j=1,j\neq i}^{K}\exp(-z_{ij})}]^{-1}\ ,\text{ with }z_{ij}:=\ln\frac{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{i}{})}{p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{j}{})}\ .

(102)

Using a different definition, the softmax function (version 2) can be written as

\displaystyle p({\mathcal{C}}_{i}{}|\bm{x})=\frac{\exp(z_{i})}{\sum_{j=1}^{K}\exp(z_{j})}\ ,\text{ with }z_{i}:=\ln p(\bm{x},{\mathcal{C}}_{i}{})\ ,

(103)

which is the same as Eq. (
97
).

\hfill\blacksquare

0.5.2
Gradient of cost function by backpropagation

The gradient of a cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
with respect to the parameters
\bm{\theta}
is obtained using the chain rule of differentiation, and backpropagation is an efficient way to compute the chain rule.
In the forward propagation, the computation (or function composition) moves from the first layer
(1)
to the last layer
(L)
; in the backpropagation, the computation moves in reverse order, from the last layer
(L)
to the first layer
(1)
.

Remark 0.5.4
.

We focus our attention on developing backpropagation for fully-connected networks, for which
an explicit derivation was not provided in
[
78
]
, but would help clarify the pseudocode.
99
99

99

See
[
78
]
, Section 6.5.4, p. 206, Algorithm 6.4.

The approach in
[
78
]
was based on computational graph, which would not be familiar to first-time learners from computational mechanics, albeit more general in that it was applicable to networks with more general architecture, such as those with skipped connections, which require keeping track of parent and child processing units for constructing the path of backpropagation.
See also Appendix
.15
where the backprop Algorithm
1
below is rewritten in a different form to explain the equivalent Algorithm 6.4 in
[
78
]
, p. 206.

\hfill\blacksquare

Figure 47
:

Backpropagation building block, typical layer
(\ell)
(Section
0.5.2
, Algorithm
1
, Appendix
.15
).
The forward propagation path is shown in blue, with the backpropagation path in red. The update of the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
in layer
(\ell)
is done as soon as the gradient
\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
is available using a gradient descent algorithm. The row matrix
\bm{r}^{(\ell)}=\partial J/\partial\bm{z}^{(\ell)}
in Eq. (
117
) can be computed once for use to evaluate both the gradient
\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
in Eq. (
118
) and the gradient
\partial J/\partial\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}
in Eq. (
119
), then discarded to free up memory. See pseudocode in Algorithm
1
.

.

It is convenient to recall here some equations developed earlier (keeping the same equation numbers) for the computation of the gradient
\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
of the cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
with respect to the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
in layer
(\ell)
, going backward from the last layer
\ell=L,\cdots,1
.

\bullet
Cost function
J(\theta)
:

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}\,\text{MSE}=\frac{1}{2\text{m}}\parallel\bm{y}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\parallel^{2}=\frac{1}{2\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\left(y_{k}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{k}\right)^{2}\ ,

(
75
)

\bullet
Inputs
\bm{x}=\bm{y}^{(0)}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(0)}\times 1}
with
0pt_{(0)}=n
and predicted outputs
\bm{y}^{(L)}=\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(L)}\times 1}
with
0pt_{(0)}=m
:

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{(0)}=\bm{x}^{(1)}=\bm{x}\text{ (inputs) }

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{(\ell)}=f^{(\ell)}(\bm{x}^{(\ell)})=\bm{x}^{(\ell+1)}\text{ with }\ell=1,\ldots,L-1

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{(L)}=f^{(L)}(\bm{x}^{(L)})=\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\text{ (predicted outputs) }

(
23
)

\bullet
Weighted sum of inputs and biases
\bm{z}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 1}
:

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{(\ell)}=\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}+\bm{b}^{(\ell)}\text{ such that }z^{(\ell)}_{i}=\bm{w}^{(\ell)}_{i}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}+b^{(\ell)}_{i}\ ,\text{ for }i=1,\ldots,m_{(\ell)}\ ,

(
30
)

Backpropagation pseudocode
(Section

).
Compute the gradient of cost function
J
relative to the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
, with
\ell=L,\cdots,1
, by backpropagation in one step of a gradient-descent algorithm to find parameters that decrease the cost function. The focus here is backpropagation, not the overall optimization. So the algorithm starts at the end of
any
forward propagation to begin backpropagation, from the last layer
(L)
back to the first layer
(1)
, to update the parameters to decrease the cost function. See also the block diagrams in Figure
47
and Figure
