, and
Appendix
.15
where an alternative backprop Algorithm
9
is used to explain the equivalent Algorithm 6.4 in
[
78
]
, p. 206.

The above equations are valid for the last layer
\ell=L
, since since the predicted output

{\bm{y}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

is the same as the output of the last layer
(L)
, i.e.,
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\equiv\bm{y}^{(L)}
by Eq. (
23
). Similarly, these equations are also valid for the first layer
(1)
since the input for layer
(1)
is
\bm{x}^{(1)}=\bm{x}=\bm{y}^{(0)}
. using Eq. (
39
), we obtain (no sum on
k
)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}=a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{k}^{(\ell)})\frac{\partial z_{k}^{(\ell)}}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}=\sum_{p}a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{k}^{(\ell)})\frac{\partial\theta_{kp}^{(\ell)}}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}\overline{y}_{p}^{(\ell-1)}=\sum_{p}a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{k}^{(\ell)})\delta_{ki}\delta_{pj}\overline{y}_{p}^{(\ell-1)}=a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{k}^{(\ell)})\delta_{ki}\overline{y}_{j}^{(\ell-1)}

(106)

Using Eq. (
106
) in Eq. (
104
) leads to the expressions for the gradient, both in component form (left) and in matrix form (right):

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\overline{y}_{j}^{(\ell-1)}\ \text{(no sum on $i$)}\Leftrightarrow\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}\right]=\left[\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}\right]^{T}\odot\left[a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\right]\right]\left[\overline{y}_{j}^{(\ell-1)}\right]^{T}\ ,

(107)

where
\odot
is the elementwise multiplication, known as the Hadamard operator, defined as follows:

\displaystyle\left[p_{i}\right]\ ,\left[q_{i}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 1}\Rightarrow\left[p_{i}\right]\odot\left[q_{i}\right]=\left[(p_{i}q_{i})\right]=\left[p_{1}q_{1},\cdots,p_{m}q_{m}\right]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times 1}\text{ (no sum on $i$)}\ ,

(108)

and

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell)}}\text{ (row)}\ ,\ \bm{z}^{(\ell)}=\left[\bm{z}_{k}^{(\ell)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 1}\text{ (column)}\Rightarrow a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 1}\text{ (column)}

(109)

\displaystyle\left[\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}\right]^{T}\odot\left[a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\right]\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 1}\text{ (column, no sum on $i$)}\ ,\text{ and}\left[\overline{y}_{j}^{(\ell-1)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{[0pt_{(\ell-1)}+1]\times 1}\text{ (column)}

(110)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial\theta_{ij}^{(\ell)}}\right]=\left[\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}\right]^{T}\odot\left[a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\right]\right]\left[\overline{y}_{j}^{(\ell-1)}\right]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times[0pt_{(\ell-1)}+1]}\ ,

(111)

which then agrees with the matrix dimension in the first expression for
{\partial J}/{\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}}
in Eq. (
105
). For the last layer
\ell=L
, all terms on the right-hand side of Eq. (
111
) are available for the computation of the gradient
\partial J/\partial\theta_{ij}^{(L)}
since

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{(L)}=\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\Rightarrow\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{y}^{(L)}}=\frac{\partial J}{\partial\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}}\left(\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.13657pt\hbox{${\bm{y}}$}\hskip-3.13657pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}^{|k|}-\bm{y}^{|k|}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(L)}}\text{ (row)}

(112)

with
m
being the number of examples and
0pt_{(L)}
the width of layer
(L)
, is the mean error from the expression of the cost function in Eq. (
77
), with
z_{i}^{(L)}
and
\overline{y}_{j}^{(L-1)}
already computed in the forward propagation. To compute the gradient of the cost function with respect to the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(L-1)}
in layer
(L-1)
, we need the derivative
\partial J/\partial y_{i}^{(L-1)}
, per Eq. (
111
).
Thus, in general, the derivative of cost function
J
with respect to the output matrix
\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}
of layer
(\ell-1)
, i.e.,
\partial J/\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}
, can be expressed in terms of the previously computed derivative
\partial J/\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}
and other quantities for layer
(\ell)
as follows:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}=\sum_{k}\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}\frac{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}=\sum_{k}\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}a^{\prime}(z_{k}^{(\ell)})w_{ki}^{(\ell)}=\sum_{k}\frac{\partial J}{\partial z_{k}^{(\ell)}}\frac{\partial z_{k}^{(\ell)}}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}

(113)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}\right]=\left[\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}\right]\odot\left[a^{\prime}(z_{k}^{(\ell)})\right]^{T}\right]\left[w_{ki}^{(\ell)}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell-1)}}\text{ (no sum on $k$)}

(114)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell-1)}}\ ,\quad\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell)}}\text{ (row)}\ ,\quad\left[a^{\prime}(z_{k}^{(\ell)})\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 1}\text{ (column)}

(115)

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{k}^{(\ell)}}\right]\odot\left[a^{\prime}(z_{k}^{(\ell)})\right]^{T}=\frac{\partial J}{\partial z_{k}^{(\ell)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell)}}\text{ (no sum on $k$)}\ ,\quad\left[w_{ki}^{(\ell)}\right]=\frac{\partial z_{k}^{(\ell)}}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell-1)}}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times 0pt_{(\ell-1)}}\ .

(116)

Comparing Eq. (
114
) and Eq. (
111
), when backpropagation reaches layer
(\ell)
, the same row matrix

\displaystyle\bm{r}^{(\ell)}:=\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{z}^{(\ell)}}=\left[\left[\frac{\partial J}{\partial y_{i}^{(\ell)}}\right]\odot\left[a^{\prime}(\bm{z}_{i}^{(\ell)})\right]^{T}\right]=\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{y}^{(\ell)}}\odot a^{\prime}(\bm{z}^{(\ell)T})\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell)}}\text{ (row)}

(117)

is only needed to be computed once for use to compute both the gradient of the cost
J
relative to the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
[see Eq. (
111
) and Figure
47
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}}=\bm{r}^{(\ell)T}\overline{\bm{y}}^{(\ell-1)T}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times[0pt_{(\ell-1)}+1]}

(118)

and the gradient of the cost
J
relative to the outputs
\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}
of layer
(\ell-1)
[see Eq. (
114
) and Figure
47
]

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}}=\bm{r}^{{\ell}}\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times 0pt_{(\ell-1)}}\ .

(119)

The block diagram for backpropagation at layer
(\ell)
—as described in Eq. (

0.5.3
Vanishing and exploding gradients

To demonstrate the vanishing gradient problem,
a network is used in
[
21
]
, having an input layer containing 784 neurons, corresponding to the
28\times 28=784
pixels in the input image, four hidden layers, with each hidden layer containing 30 neurons, and an output layer containing 10 neurons, corresponding to the 10 possible classifications for the MNIST digits (’0’, ’1’, ’2’, … , ’9’).
A key ingredient is the use of the sigmoid function as active function; see Figure
30
.

We note immediately that the vanishing / exploding gradient problem can be resolved using the rectified linear function (ReLu, Figure
24
) as active function in combination with ‘‘normalized initialization’’
100
100

100

See
[
78
]
, p. 295.

and “intermediate normalization layers”, which are mentioned in
[
127
]
, and which we will not discuss here.

The speed of learning of a hidden layer
(\ell)
in Figure
49
is defined as the norm of the gradient
\bm{g}^{(\ell)}
of the cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
with respect to the parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
in the hidden layer
(\ell)
:

\displaystyle\parallel\bm{g}^{(\ell)}\parallel=\left|\kern-1.0pt\left|\frac{\partial J}{\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}}\right|\kern-1.0pt\right|

(120)

The speed of learning in each of the four layers as a function of the number of epochs
101
101

101

An epoch is when all examples in the dataset had been used in a training session of the optimization process. For a formal definition of “epoch”, see Section
0.6.3
on stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and Footnote
145
.

of training drops down quickly after less than 50 training epochs, then plateaued out, as depicted in Figure
49
, where the speed of learning of layer (1) was 100 times less than that of layer (4) after 400 training epochs.

Figure 49
:

Vanishing gradient problem
(Section
0.5.3
). Speed of learning of earlier layers is much slower than that of later layers. Here, after
400
epochs of training, the speed of learning of Layer (1) at
10^{-5}
(blue line) is 100 times slower than that of Layer (4) at
10^{-3}
(green line);

[
21
]
, Chapter 5, ‘Why are deep neural networks hard to train ?’

(CC BY-NC 3.0)
.

To understand the reason for the quick and significant decrease in the speed of learning, consider a network with four layers, having one scalar input
x
with target scalar output
y
, and predicted scalar output

{y}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

, as shown in Figure
50
, where each layer has one neuron.
102
102

102

See also
[
21
]
.

The cost function and its derivative are

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}(y-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt})^{2}\,,\quad\frac{\partial J}{\partial y}=y-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}

(121)

Figure 50
:

Neural network with four layers
(Section
0.5.3
), one neuron per layer, scalar input
x
, scalar output
y
, cost function
J(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{2}(y-\widetilde{y})^{2}
, with
\widetilde{y}=y^{(4)}
being the target output and also the output of layer
(4)
, such that
f^{(\ell)}(y^{(\ell-1)})=a(z^{(\ell)})
, with
a(\cdot)
being the active function,
z^{(\ell)}=w^{(\ell)}y^{(\ell-1)}+b^{(\ell)}
, for
\ell=1,\ldots,4
, and the network parameters are
\bm{\theta}=[w_{1},\ldots,w_{4},b_{1},\ldots,b_{4}]
.
The detailed block diagram is in Figure
51
.

The neuron in layer
(\ell)
accepts the scalar input
y^{(\ell-1)}
to produce the scalar output
y^{(\ell)}
according to

\displaystyle f^{(\ell)}(y^{(\ell-1)})=a(z^{(\ell)})\,,\text{ with }z^{(\ell)}=w^{(\ell)}y^{(\ell-1)}+b^{(\ell)}\ .

(122)

As an example of computing the gradient, the derivative of the cost function
J
with respect to the bias
b^{(1)}
of layer
(1)
is given by

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J}{\partial b^{(1)}}=(y-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.6308pt\hbox{${y}$}\hskip-2.6308pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.69449pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt})[a^{\prime}(z^{(4)})w^{(4)}][a^{\prime}(z^{(3)})w^{(3)}][a^{\prime}(z^{(2)})w^{(2)}][a^{\prime}(z^{(1)})w^{(1)}]

(123)

The back propagation procedure to compute the gradient
\partial J/\partial b^{(1)}
in Eq. (
123
) is depicted in Figure
51
, which is a particular case of the more general Figure
48
.

Figure 51
:

Neural network with four layers
in Figure
50
(Section
0.5.3
). Detailed block diagram.
Forward propagation (blue arrows) and backpropagation (red arrows).
In the forward propagation wave, at each layer
(\ell)
, the product
a^{\prime}w^{(\ell)}
is computed and stored, awaiting for the chain-rule derivative to arrive at this layer to multiply.
The cost function
J(\theta)
is computed together with its derivative
\partial J/\partial y
, which is the backprop starting point, from which, when following the backpropagation red arrow, the order of the factors are as in Eq. (
123
), until the derivative
\partial J/\partial b^{(1)}
is reached at the head of the backprop red arrow.
(Only the weights are shown, not the biases, which are not needed in the back propagation, to save space.)
The speed of learning is slowed down significantly in early layers due to vanishing gradient, as shown in Figure
49
.
See also the more general case in Figure
48
.

Whether the gradient
\frac{\partial J}{\partial b^{(1)}}
in Eq. (
123
) vanishes or explodes depends on the magnitude of its factors

\displaystyle|a^{\prime}(z^{(\ell)})w^{(\ell)}|<1,\ \forall\ell

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{ Vanishing gradient}

(124)

\displaystyle|a^{\prime}(z^{(\ell)})w^{(\ell)}|>1,\ \forall\ell

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\text{ Exploding gradient}

(125)

In other mixed cases, the problem of vanishing or exploding gradient could be alleviated by the changing of the magnitude
|a^{\prime}(z^{(\ell)})w^{(\ell)}|
, above 1 and below 1, from layer to layer.

Remark 0.5.5
.

While the vanishing gradient problem for multilayer networks (static case) may be alleviated by weights that vary from layer to layer (the mixed cases mentioned above), this problem is especially critical in the case of Recurrent Neural Networks, since the weights stay constant for all state numbers (or “time”) in a sequence of data.
See Remark
0.7.3
on “short-term memory” in Section
0.7.2
on Long Short-Term Memory.
In back-propagation through the states in a sequence of data, from the last state back to the first state, the same weight keeps being multiplied by itself. Hence, when a weight is less than 1, successive powers of its magnitude eventually decrease to zero when progressing back the first state.

\hfill\blacksquare

Logistic sigmoid and hyperbolic tangent

The first derivatives of the sigmoid function and hyperbolic tangent function depicted in Figure
30
(also in Remark
0.5.3
on the softmax function and Figure
46
) are given below:

\displaystyle a(z)=\mathfrak{s}(z)=\frac{1}{1+\exp(-z)}

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow a^{\prime}(z)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(z)=\mathfrak{s}(z)[1-\mathfrak{s}(z)]\in(0,1)

(126)

\displaystyle a(z)=\tanh(z)

\displaystyle\Longrightarrow a^{\prime}(z)=\tanh^{\prime}(z)=\frac{1}{1+z^{2}}\in(0,1]

(127)

and are less than 1 in magnitude (everywhere for the sigmoid function, and almost everywhere for the hyperbolic tangent tanh function), except at
z=0
, where the derivative of the tanh function is equal to 1; Figure
52
.
Successive multiplications of these derivatives will result in smaller and smaller values along the back propagation path.
If the weights
w^{(\ell)}
in Eq. (
123
) are also smaller than 1, then the gradient
\partial J/\partial b^{(1)}
will tend toward 0, i.e., vanish.
The problem is further exacerbated in deeper networks with increasing number of layers, and thus increasing number of factors less than 1 (i.e.,
|a^{\prime}(z^{(\ell)})w^{(\ell)})|<1
).
We have encountered the vanishing-gradient problem.

Figure 52
:

Sigmoid and hyperbolic tangent functions, derivative

(Section
0.5.3
). The derivative of sigmoid function
(
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(z)=\mathfrak{s}(z)[1-\mathfrak{s}(z)]
, green line) is less than 1 everywhere, whereas the derivative of the hyperbolic tangent (
\tanh^{\prime}(z)=(1+z^{2})^{-1}
, purple line) is less than 1 everywhere, except at the abscissa
z=0
, where it is equal to 1.

The exploding gradient problem is opposite to the vanishing gradient problem, and occurs when the gradient has its magnitude increases in subsequent multiplications, particularly at a ‘‘cliff’’, which is a sharp drop in the cost function in the parameter space.
103
103

103

See
[
78
]
, p. 281

The gradient at the brink of a cliff (Figure
53
) leads to large-magnitude gradients, which when multiplied with each other several times along the back propagation path would result in an exploding gradient problem.

Figure 53
:

Cost-function cliff
(Section
0.5.3
).
A cliff, or a sharp drop in the cost function.
The parameter space is represented by a weight
w
and a bias
b
.
The slope at the brink of the cliff leads to large-magnitude gradients, which when multiplied with each other several times along the back propagation path would result in an exploding gradient problem.

[
78
]
, p. 281.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Rectified linear function (ReLU)

The rectified linear function depicted in Figure
24
with its derivative (Heaviside function) equal to 1 for any input greater than zero, would resolve the vanishing-gradient problem, as
it is written in
[
113
]
:

“For a given input only a subset of neurons are active. Computation is linear on this subset … Because of this linearity, gradients flow well on the active paths of neurons (there is no gradient vanishing effect due to activation non-linearities of sigmoid or tanh units), and mathematical investigation is easier. Computations are also cheaper: there is no need for computing the exponential function in activations, and sparsity can be exploited.”

A problem with ReLU was that some neurons were never activated, and called “dying” or “dead”, as described in
[
131
]
:

“However, ReLU units are at a potential disadvantage during optimization because the gradient is 0 whenever the unit is not active. This could lead to cases where a unit never activates as a gradient-based optimization algorithm will not adjust the weights of a unit that never activates initially. Further, like the vanishing gradients problem, we might expect learning to be slow when training ReL networks with constant 0 gradients.”

To remedy this “dying” or “dead” neuron problem,
the Leaky ReLU, proposed in

[
131
]
,
104
104

104

According to Google Scholar,
[
113
]
(2011) received 3,656 citations on 2019.10.13 and 8,815 citations on 2022.06.23, whereas
[
131
]
(2013) received 2,154 and 6,380 citations on these two respective dates.

had the expression already given previously in Eq. (
44
), and can be viewed as an approximation to the leaky diode in Figure
29
. Both ReLU and Leaky ReLU have been known and used in neuroscience for years before being imported into artificial neural network; see Section
0.13
for a historical review.

Figure 54
:

Rectified Linear Unit
(ReLU, left) and
Parametric ReLU
(right)
(Section
0.5.3
), in which the slope
s
is a parameter to optimize; see Section
0.5.3
. See also Figure
24
on ReLU.

Parametric rectified linear unit (PReLU)

Instead of arbitrarily fixing the slope
s
of the Leaky ReLU at
0.01
for negative
z
as in Eq. (
44
),
it is proposed to leave this slope
s
as a free parameter to optimize along with the weights and biases
[
61
]
[
61
]
; see Figure
54
:

\displaystyle a(z)=\max(s\,z,z)=\begin{cases}s\,z&\text{ for }z\leq 0\\
z&\text{ for }0<z\end{cases}

(128)

and thus the network adaptively learned the parameters to control the leaky part of the activation function.
Using
the Parametric ReLU in Eq.(
128
),
a deep convolutional neural network (CNN) in
[
61
]
was
able to surpass the level of human performance in image recognition for the first time in 2015; see Figure
3
on ImageNet competition results over the years.

:

Cost-function landscape
(Section
0.6
). Residual network with 56 layers (ResNet-56) on the CIFAR-10 training set. Highly non-convex, with many local minima, and deep, narrow valleys
[
132
]
.
The training error and test error for fully-connected network increased when the number of layers was increased from 20 to 56, Figure

, motivating the introduction of residual network, Figure

and Figure

, Section
0.4.6
.

0.6
Network training, optimization methods

For network training, i.e., to find the optimal network parameters
\bm{\theta}
that minimize the cost function
J(\bm{\theta})
, we describe here both deterministic optimization methods used in full-batch mode,
105
105

105

A “full batch” is a complete training set of examples; see Footnote
117
.

and stochastic optimization methods used in minibatch
106
106

106

A minibatch is a random subset of the training set, which is called here the “full batch”; see Footnote
117
.

mode.

Figure
55
shows the highly non-convex landscape of the cost function of a residual network with 56 layers trained using the CIFAR-10 dataset (Canadian Institute For Advanced Research), a collection of images commonly used to train machine learning and computer vision algorithms, containing 60,000 32x32 color images in 10 different classes, representing airplanes, cars, birds, cats, deer, dogs, frogs, horses, ships, and trucks. Each class has 6,000 images.
107
107

107

See “CIFAR-10”, Wikipedia,
version 16:44, 14 October 2019
.

Deterministic optimization methods (Section

) include first-order gradient method (Algorithm

) and second-order quasi-Newton method (Algorithm

), with line searches based on different rules, introduced by Goldstein, Armijo, and Wolfe.

Stochastic optimization methods (Section

) include

•

First-order
stochastic gradient descent
(

) methods (Algorithm

), with

such as momentum and accelerated gradient

•

Adaptive learning-rate algorithms

(Algorithm
5
):
Adam
and variants such as
AMSGrad
,

, etc. that are popular in the machine-learning community

•

Criticism of adaptive methods
and SGD resurgence with
add-on tricks
such as effective tuning and step-length decay (or annealing)

Classical line search with stochasticity:

SGD with Armijo line search

(Algorithm
6

Newton method with Armijo-like line search
(Algorigthm

)

0.6.1
Training set, validation set, test set, stopping criteria

The classical (old) thinking—starting in 1992 with
[
133
]
and exemplified by Figures
57
,
58
,
59
,
60
(a, left)—would surprise first-time learners that
minimizing the training error is not optimal
in machine learning. A reason is that
training a neural network is
different
from using ‘‘pure optimization’’ since
it is desired to decrease not only
the error during training (called training error, and that’s pure optimization), but also the error committed by a trained network on inputs never seen before.
108
108

108

See also
[
78
]
, p. 268, Section 8.1, “How learning differs from pure optimization”; that’s the classical thinking.

Such error is called generalization error or test error. This classical thinking, known as the
bias-variance trade-off
, has been included in books since 2001
[
134
]
(p. 194) and even repeated in 2016
[
78
]
(p. 268). Models with lower number of parameters have higher bias and lower variance, whereas models with higher number of parameters have lower bias and higher variance; Figure
59
.
109
109

109

See
[
134
]
, p. 11, for a classification example using two methods: (1) linear models and least squares and (2) k-nearest neighbors. “The linear model makes huge assumptions about structure [high bias] and yields stable [low variance] but possibly inaccurate predictions [high training error]. The method of k-nearest neighbors makes very mild structural assumptions [low bias]: its predictions are often accurate [low training error] but can be unstable [high variance].”
See also
[
130
]
, p. 151, Figure 3.6.

The modern thinking is exemplified by Figure
60
(b, right) and Figure
61
, and does not contradict the intuitive notion that
decreasing the training error to zero is indeed desirable
, as overparameterizing networks beyond the interpolation threshold (zero training error) in modern practice generalizes well (small test error). In Figure
61
, the test error continued to decrease significantly with increasing number of parameters
N
beyond the interpolation threshold
N^{\star}=825
, whereas the classical regime (
N<N^{\star}
) with the bias-variance trade-off (blue) in Figure
59
was restrictive, and did not generalize as well (larger test error).
Beyond the interpolation threshold
N^{\star}
, variance can be decreased by using ensemble average, as shown by the orange line in Figure
61
.

Such modern practice was the motivation for research into shallow networks with
infinite
width as a first step to understand how overparameterized networks worked so well; see Figure
148
and Section
0.14.2
“Lack of understanding on why deep learning worked.”

Figure 57
:

Training and validation learning curves—Classical viewpoint
(Section
0.6.1
), i.e., plots of training error and validation errors versus epoch number (time). While the training cost decreased continuously, the validation cost reaches a minimum around epoch 20, then started to gradually increase, forming an “asymmetric U-shaped curve.” Between epoch 100 and epoch 240, the training error was essentially flat, indicating convergence. Adapted from
[
78
]
, p. 239.
See Figure
60
(a, left), where the classical risk curve is the classical viewpoint, whereas the modern interpolation viewpoint is on the right subfigure (b).

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

To develop a neural-network model, a dataset governed by the same probability distribution, such as the CIFAR-10 dataset mentioned above, can be typically divided into three non-overlapping subsets called
training set, validation set
, and
test set
. The validation set is also called the
development set
, a terminology used in
[
55
]
[
55
]
, in which an effective method of step-length decay was proposed; see Section
0.6.3
.

It was suggested in
[
135
]
, p. 61, to use 50% of the dataset as training set, 25% as validation set, and 25% as test set.
On the other hand, while a validation set with size about
1/4
of the training set was suggested in
[
78
]
, p. 118, there was no suggestion for the relative size of the test set.
110
110

110

Andrew Ng suggested the following partitions. For small datasets having less than
10^{4}
examples, the training/validation/test ratio of
60\%/20\%/20\%
could be used. For large datasets with order of
10^{6}
examples, use ratio
98\%/1\%/1\%
. For datasets with much more than
10^{6}
examples, use ratio
99.5\%/0.25\%/0.25\%
. See Coursera course “Improving deep neural network: Hyperparameter tuning, regularization and optimization”, at time 4:00,
video website
.

See Figure
56
for a conceptual partition of the dataset.

Figure 58
:

Validation learning curve
(Section
0.6.1
, Algorithm
4
). Validation error vs epoch number. Some validation error could oscillate wildly around the mean, resulting in an “ugly reality”. The global minimum validation error corresponded to epoch number
\tau^{\star}
. Since the stopping criteria may miss this global minimum, it was suggested to monitor the validation learning curve to find the epoch
\tau^{\star}
at which the network parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau^{\star}}
would be declared optimal.
Adapted from
[
135
]
, p. 55.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Examples in the training set are fed into an optimizer to find the network parameter estimate
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}
that minimizes the cost function estimate
\widetilde{J}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}})
.
111
111

111

The word “estimate” is used here for the more general case of stochastic optimization with minibatches; see Section
0.6.3
on stochastic gradient descent and subsequent sections on stochastic algorithms. When deterministic optimization is used with the full batch of dataset, then the cost estimate is the same as the cost, i.e.,
\widetilde{J}\equiv J
, and the network parameter estimates are the same as the network parameters, i.e.,
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}\equiv\bm{\theta}
.

As the optimization on the training set progresses from epoch to epoch,
112
112

112

An epoch is when all examples in the dataset had been used in a training session of the optimization process. For a formal definition of “epoch”, see Section
0.6.3
on stochastic gradient descent (SGD) and Footnote
145
.

examples in the validation set are fed as inputs into the network to obtain the outputs for computing the cost function
\widetilde{J}_{val}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau})
, also called validation error, at predetermined epochs
\{\tau_{k}\}
using the network parameters
\{\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau_{k}}\}
obtained from the optimization on the training set at those epochs.

Figure
57
shows the different behaviour of the training error versus that of the validation error. The validation error would decrease quickly initially, reaching a global minimum, then gradually increased, whereas the training error continued to decrease and plateaued out, indicating that the gradients got smaller and smaller, and there was not much decrease in the cost. From epoch 100 to epoch 240, the traning error was at about the same level, with litte noise. The validation error, on the other hand, had a lot of noise.

Because of the “asymmetric U-shaped curve” of the validation error, the thinking was that if the optimization process could stop early at the global mininum of the validation error, then the generalization (test) error, i.e., the value of cost function on the test set, would also be small, thus the name “
early stopping
”. The test set contains examples that have not been used to train the network, thus simulating inputs never seen before. The validation error could have oscillations with large amplitude around a mean curve, with many local minima; see Figure
58
.

The difference between the test (generalization) error and the validation error is called the generalization gap, as shown in the
bias-variance trade-off

[
133
]

Figure
59
, which qualitatively delineates these errors versus model capacity, and conceptually explains the optimal model capacity as where the generalization gap equals the training error, or the generalization error is twice the training error.

Remark 0.6.1
.

Even the best machine learning generalization capability nowadays still cannot compete with the generalization ability of human babies; see Section
0.14.6
on “What’s new? Teaching machines to think like babies”.

\blacksquare

Figure 59
:

Bias-variance trade-off
(Section
0.6.1
).
Training error (cost) and test error versus model capacity.
Two ways to change the model capacity: (1) change the number of network parameters, (2) change the values of these parameters (weight decay). The generalization gap is the difference between the test (generalization) error and the training error. As the model capacity increases from underfit to overfit, the training error decreases, but the generalization gap increases, past the optimal capacity. Figure
72
gives examples of underfit, appropriately fit, overfit. See
[
78
]
, p. 112.
The above is the classical viewpoint, which is still prevalent
[
136
]
; see Figure
60
for the modern viewpoint, in which overfitting with high capacity model generalizes well (small test error) in practice.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Figure 60
:

Modern interpolation regime
(Sections
0.6.1
,
0.14.2
).
Beyond the interpolation threshold, the test error goes down as the model capacity (e.g., number of parameters) increases, describing the observation that networks with high capacity beyond the interpolation threshold generalize well, even though overfit in training. Risk = error or cost. Capacity = number of parameters (but could also be increased by weight decay).
Figures
57
,
59
corresponds to the classical regime, i.e., old method (thinking)
[
136
]
.
See Figure
61
for experimental evidence of the modern interpolation regime, and Figure
148
for a shallow network with infinite width.

.

:

(Sections

Early-stopping criteria.
One criterion is to first define the lowest validation error from epoch 1 up to the current epoch
\tau
as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{val}^{\star}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau})=\min_{\tau^{\prime}\leq\tau}\{\widetilde{J}_{val}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau^{\prime}})\}\ ,

(129)

then define the
generalization loss
(in percentage) at epoch
\tau
as the increase in validation error relative to the minimum validation error from epoch 1 to the present epoch
\tau
:

\displaystyle G(\tau)=100\cdot\left(\frac{\widetilde{J}_{val}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau})}{\widetilde{J}_{val}^{\star}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau})}-1\right)\ .

(130)

[
135
]
then defined the “first class of stopping criteria” as follows: Stop the optimization on the training set when the generalization loss exceeds a certain threshold
s
(generalization loss lower bound):

\displaystyle G_{\text{s}}:\text{Stop after epoch }\tau\text{ if }G(\tau)>\text{s}\ .

(131)

The issue is how to determine the generalization loss lower bound
s
so not to fall into a local minimum, and to catch the global minimum; see Figure
58
.
There were many more early-stopping criterion classes in
[
135
]
. But it is not clear whether all these increasingly sophisticated stopping criteria would work to catch the validation-error global minimum in Figure
58
.

Moreover, the above discussion is for the
classical regime
in Figure
60
(a).
In the context of the
modern interpolation regime
in Figure
60
(b), early stopping means that the computation would cease as soon as the training error reaches “its lowest possible value (typically zero [beyond the interpolation threshold], unless two identical data points have two different labels)”
[
137
]
. See the green line in Figure
61
.

Computational budget, learning curves.
A simple method would be to set an epoch budget, i.e., the largest number of epochs for computation sufficiently large for the training error to go down significantly, then monitor graphically both the training error (cost) and the validation error versus epoch number. These plots are called the
learning curves
; see Figure
57
, for which an epoch budget of 240 was used. Select the global minimum of the validation learning curve, with epoch number
\tau^{\star}
(Figure
57
), and use the corresponding network parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau^{\star}}
, which were saved periodically, as optimal paramters for the network.
113
113

113

See also “Method for early stopping in a neural network”, StackExchange, 2018.03.05,

Original website
,

Internet archive
.

[
78
]
, p. 287, also suggested to monitor the training learning curve to adjust the step length (learning rate).

Remark 0.6.2
.

Since it is important to monitor the validation error during training,
a whole section is devoted in
[
78
]

(Section 8.1, p. 268) to expound on “How Learning Differs from Pure Optimization”. And also for this reason, it is not clear yet what
global optimization
algorithms such as in
[
138
]
could bring to network training, whereas the stochastic gradient descent (SGD) in Section
0.6.3
is quite efficient; see also Section
0.6.5
on criticism of adaptive methods.

\hfill\blacksquare

Remark 0.6.3
.

Epoch budget, global iteration budget.

For stochastic optimization algorithms—Sections
0.6.3
,
0.6.5
,
0.6.6
,
0.6.7
—the epoch counter is
\tau
and the epoch budget
\tau_{max}
. Numerical experiments in Figure
73
had an epoch budget of
\tau_{max}=250
, whereas numerical experiments in Figure
74
had an epoch budget of
\tau_{max}=1800
.
The computational budget could be specified in terms of global iteration counter
j
as
j_{max}
. Figure
71
had a global iteration budget of
j_{max}=5000
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Before presenting the stochastic gradient-descent (SGD) methods in Section
0.6.3
, it is important to note that classical deterministic methods of optimization in Section
0.6.2
continue to be useful in the age of deep learning and SGD.

“One should not lose sight of the fact that [full] batch approaches possess some intrinsic advantages. First, the use full gradient information at each iterate opens the door for many deterministic gradient-based optimization methods that have been developed over the past decades, including not only the full gradient method, but also accelerated gradient, conjugate gradient, quasi-Newton, inexact Newton methods, and can benefit from parallelization.”

[
80
]
, p. 237.

0.6.2
Deterministic optimization, full batch

Once the gradient
\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times[0pt_{(\ell-1)}+1]}
of the cost function
J
has been computed using backpropagation described in Section
0.5.2
, the layer parameters
\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
are updated to decrease cost function
J
using gradient descent as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}\leftarrow\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}-\epsilon\,\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}=\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}-\epsilon\,\bm{g}^{(\ell)}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{g}^{(\ell)}:=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{0pt_{(\ell)}\times[0pt_{(\ell-1)}+1]}\ .

(132)

being the gradient direction, and
\epsilon
called the learning rate.
114
114

114

See Figure
151
in Section
0.14.7
on “Lack of transparency and irreproducibility of results” in recent deep-learning papers.

The layer-by-layer update in Eq. (
132
) as soon as the gradient
\bm{g}^{(\ell)}=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}^{(\ell)}
had been computed is valid when the learning rate
\epsilon
does not depend on the gradient
\bm{g}=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}
with respect to the whole set of network parameters
\bm{\theta}
.

Otherwise, the update of the whole network parameter
\bm{\theta}
would be carried out after the complete gradient
\bm{g}=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}
had been obtained, and the learning rate
\epsilon
had been computed based on the gradient
\bm{g}
:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}\leftarrow\bm{\theta}-\epsilon\,\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}=\bm{\theta}-\epsilon\,\bm{g}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{g}:=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}\in\mathbb{R}^{P_{T}\times 1}\ ,

(133)

where
P_{T}
is the total number of network paramters defined in Eq. (
38
).
An example of a learning-rate computation that depends on the complete gradient
\bm{g}=\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}
is gradient descent with Armijo line search; see Section
0.6.2
and line
1
in Algorithm
1
.

“Neural network researchers have long realized that the learning rate is reliably one of the most difficult to set hyperparameters because it significantly affects model performance.”
[
78
]
, p. 298.

In fact, it is well known in the field of optimization, where the learning rate is often mnemonically denoted by
\lambda
, being Greek for “l” and standing for “step length”; see, e.g., Polak (1971)
[
139
]
.

“We can choose
\epsilon
in several different ways. A popular approach is to set
\epsilon
to a small constant. Sometimes, we can solve for the step size that makes the directional derivative vanish. Another approach is to evaluate
f(\bm{x}-\epsilon\nabla_{\bm{x}}f(\bm{x}))
for several values of
\epsilon
and choose the one that results in the smallest objective function value. This last strategy is called a
line search
.”
[
78
]
, p. 82.

Choosing an arbitrarily small
\epsilon
, without guidance on how small is small, is not a good approach, since exceedingly slow convergence could result for too small
\epsilon
. In general, it would not be possible to solve for the step size to make the directional derivative vanish. There are several variants of line search for computing the step size to decrease the cost function, based on the ‘‘decrease’’ conditions,
115
115

115

See, e.g.,
[
140
]
,
[
141
]
.

among which some are mentioned below.
116
116

116

See also
[
139
]
, p. 243.

Remark 0.6.4
.

Line search in deep-learning training.

Line search methods are not only important for use in deterministic optimization with full batch of examples,
117
117

117

A full batch contains all examples in the training set. There is a confusion in the use of the word “batch” in terminologies such as “batch optimization” or “batch gradient descent”, which are used to mean the full training set, and not a subset of the training set; see, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 271. Hence we explicitly use “full batch” for full training set, and mini-batch for a small subset of the training set.

but also in stochastic optimization (see Section
0.6.3
) with random mini-batches of examples
[
80
]
. The difficulty of using stochastic gradient coming from random mini-batches is the presence of noise or ‘‘discontinuities’’
118
118

118

See, e.g.,
[
80
]
. Noise is sometimes referred to as “discontinuities” as in
[
142
]
.
See also the lecture video “Understanding mini-batch gradient descent,” at time 1:20, by Andrew Ng on Coursera
website
.

in the cost function and in the gradient. Recent stochastic optimization methods—such as the sub-sampled Hessian-free Newton method reviewed in
[
80
]
, the probabilisitic line search in
[
143
]
, the first-order stochastic Armijo line search in
[
144
]
, the second-order sub-sampling line search method in
[
145
]
[
145
]
, quasi-Newton method with probabilitic line search in
[
146
]
, etc.---where line search forms a key subprocedure, are designed to address or circumvent the noisy gradient problem. For this reason, claims that line search methods have ‘‘fallen out of favor’’
119
119

119

In
[
78
]
,
a discussion on line search methods, however brief, was completely bypassed to focus on stochastic gradient-descent methods with learning-rate tuning and scheduling, such as AdaGrad, Adam, etc. Ironically, it is disconcerting to see these authors, who made important contributions to deep learning, thus helping thawing the last “AI winter”, regard with skepticism “most guidance” on learning-rate selection; see
[
78
]
, p. 287, and Section
0.6.3
. Since then, fully automatic stochastic line-search methods, without tuning runs, have been developed, apparently starting with
[
147
]
.
In the abstract of
[
142
]
, where
an interesting method using only gradients, without function evaluations, was presented, one reads
“Due to discontinuities induced by mini-batch sampling, [line searches] have largely fallen out of favor”.

would be misleading, as they may encourage students not to learn the classics. A classic never dies; it just re-emerges in a different form with additional developments to tackle new problems.

\hfill\blacksquare

In view of Remark
0.6.4
, a goal of this section is to develop a feel for some classical deterministic line search methods for readers not familiar with these concepts to prepare for reading extensions of these methods to stochastic line search methods.

Exact line search

Find a positive step length
\epsilon
that minimizes the cost function
J
along the descent direction
\bm{d}
such that the scalar (dot) product between
\bm{g}
and
\bm{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{P_{T}\times 1}
:

\displaystyle<\bm{g},\bm{d}>=\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}g_{ij}d_{ij}<0\ ,

(134)

is negative, i.e., the descent direction
\bm{d}
and the gradient
\bm{g}
form an obtuse angle bounded away from
90^{\circ}
,
120
120

120

Or equivalently, the descent direction
\bm{d}
forms an acute angle with the gradient (or steepest) descent direction
[-\bm{g}=-\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta}]
, i.e., the negative of the gradient direction.

and

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})=\min_{\lambda}\{J(\bm{\theta}+\lambda\,\bm{d})\,|\,\lambda\geq 0\}\ .

(135)

The minimization problem in Eq. (
135
) can be implemented using the Golden section search (or infinite Fibonacci search) for unimodal functions.
121
121

121

See, e.g.,
[
139
]
, p. 31, for the implementable algorithm, with the assumption that the cost function was convex. Convexity is, however, not needed for this algorithm to work; only unimodality is needed. A unimodal function has a unique minimum, and is decreasing on the left of the minimum, and increasing on the right of the minimum. Convex functions are necessarily unimodal, but not vice versa. Convexity is a particular case of unimodality.
See also
[
148
]
, p. 216, on Golden section search as infinite Fibonacci search and curve fitting line-search methods.

For more general non-convex cost functions, a minimizing step length may be non-existent, or difficult to compute exactly.
122
122

122

See
[
149
]
, p. 29, for this assertion, without examples. An example of non-existent minimizing step length
\epsilon=\arg\min_{\lambda}\{f(\theta+\lambda d)|\lambda\geq 0\}
could be
f
being a concave function. If we relax the continuity requirement, then it is easy to construct a function with no mininum and no maximum, e.g.,
f(x)=|x|
on open intervals
(-1,0)\cup(0,+1)
, and
f(-1)=f(0)=f(1)=0.5
; this function is “essentially” convex, except on the set
\{-1,0,+1\}
of measure zero where it is discontinuous. An example of a function whose minimum is difficult to compute exactly could be one with a minimum at the bottom of an extremely narrow crack.

In addition, a line search for a minimizing step length is only an auxilliary step in an overall optimization algorithm. It is therefore sufficient to find an approximate step length satisfying some decrease conditions to ensure convergence to a local minimum, while keeping the step length from being too small that would hinder a reasonable advance toward such local minimum. For these reasons, inexact line search methods (rules) were introduced, first
in
[
150
]
,
followed by
[
151
]
, then
[
152
]
and
[
153
]
. In view of Remark
0.6.4
and Footnote
119
, as we present these deterministic line-search rules, we will also immediately recall, where applicable, the recent references that generalize these rules by adding stochasticity for use as a subprocedure (inner loop) for the stochastic gradient-descent (SGD) algorithm.

Inexact line-search, Goldstein’s rule

The method is inexact since the search for an acceptable step length would stop before a minimum is reached, once the rule is satisfied.
123
123

123

The book
[
154
]
was cited in
[
139
]
, p. 33, but not the papers
[
150
]
and
[
155
]
, where Goldstein’s rule was explicitly presented in the form: Step length
\gamma\rightarrow g(x^{k},\gamma)=[f(x^{k})-f(x^{k}-\gamma\varphi(x^{k}))]/\{\gamma[\nabla f(x^{k}),\varphi(x^{k})]\}
, with
\varphi
being the descent direction,
[a,b]
the scalar (dot) product between vector
a
and vector
b
, and
\delta\leq g(x^{k},\gamma)\leq 1-\delta
, if
g(x^{k},1)\leq\delta
. The same relation was given in
[
150
]
, with different notation.

For a fixed constant
\alpha\in(0,\frac{1}{2})
, select a learning rate (step length)
\epsilon
such that
124
124

124

See also
[

, p. 33.

\displaystyle 1-\alpha\leq\frac{J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})-J(\bm{\theta})}{\epsilon\,\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}}\leq\alpha\ ,

(136)

where both the numerator and the denominator are negative, i.e.,
J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})-J(\bm{\theta})<0
and
\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}<0
by Eq. (
134
). Eq. (
136
) can be recast into a slightly more general form:
For
0<\alpha<\beta<1
, choose a learning rate
\epsilon
such that
125
125

125

See
[

]
, p. 55, and
[

\epsilon

. But that would make the computation unnecessarily stringent and costly, since the step length
\epsilon
as the root of this equation has to be solved for accurately. Again, the idea should be to make the sector bounded by the two lines,
\beta\,\epsilon\,\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}
from below and the line
\alpha\,\epsilon\,\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}
from above, as large as possible in inexact line search. See discussion below Eq. (

).

\displaystyle\beta\,\epsilon\,\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\leq\Delta J(\epsilon)\leq\alpha\,\epsilon\,\bm{g}{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\ ,\text{ with }\Delta J(\epsilon):=J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})-J(\bm{\theta})\ .

(137)

A reason could be that the sector bounded by the two lines

and

may be too narrow when

, and hence

, making a tight sector, but we could enlarge such sector by choosing
0.6<\beta<1
.

\displaystyle\epsilon(\bm{\theta})=\min_{a}\{\beta^{a}\,\rho\,|\,J(\bm{\theta}+\beta^{j}\,\bm{d})-J(\bm{\theta})\leq\alpha{\beta^{a}}\rho\,\bm{g}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\}

To satisfy the condition in Eq. (

\bm{g}

to minimize the rate

\displaystyle|J(\bm{\theta}_{i+1})-J(\bm{\theta}^{\star})|\leq r^{i}|J(\bm{\theta}_{0})-J(\bm{\theta}^{\star})|\ ,\text{ with }r=1-\left(\frac{\rho m}{M}\right)^{2}\ ,

When the Hessian
\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})=\partial^{2}J(\bm{\theta})/(\partial\bm{\theta})^{2}
is positive definite, the Newton descent direction is:

\displaystyle\bm{d}=-\bm{H}^{-1}(\bm{\theta})\,\bm{g}\ ,

(143)

When the Hessian
\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})
is not positive definite, e.g., near a saddle point, then quasi-Newton method uses the gradient descent direction
(-\bm{g}=-\partial J/\partial\bm{\theta})
as the descent direction
\bm{d}
as in Eq. (
133
),

\displaystyle\bm{d}=-\bm{g}=-\partial J(\bm{\theta})/\partial\bm{\theta}\ ,

(144)

and regularized Newton method uses a descent direction based on a regularized Hessian of the form:

\displaystyle\bm{d}=-\left[\bm{H}(\bm{\theta})+\delta\bm{I}\right]^{-1}\bm{g}\ ,

(145)

where
\delta
is a small perturbation parameter (line
3
in Algorithm
3
for deterministic Newton and line
7
in Algorithm
7
for stochastic Newton).

135

[

]
, p. 35, and
[

]
, p. 302, where both cited the Levenberg-Marquardt algorithm as the first to use regularized Hessian.

Inexact line-search, Wolfe’s rule

The rule introduced in
[
152
]
and
[
153
]
,
136
136

136

As of 2022.07.09,
[
152
]
was cited 1336 times
in various publications (books, papers) according to Google Scholar, and
559 times
in archival journal papers according to Web of Science.

sometimes called the Armijo-Goldstein-Wolfe’s rule (or conditions), particularly in
[
140
]
and
[
141
]
,
137
137

137

The authors of
[
140
]
and
[
141
]
may not be aware that Goldstein’s rule appeared before Armijo’s rule, as they cited Goldstein’s 1967 book
[
154
]
, instead of Goldstein’s 1965 paper
[
150
]
, and referred often to Polak (1971)
[
139
]
, even though it was written in
[
139
]
, p. 32, that a “step size rule [Eq. (
137
)] probably first introduced by Goldstein (1967)
[
154
]
” was used in an algorithm. See also Footnote
123
.

has been extended to add stochasticity
[
143
]
,
138
138

138

An earlier version of the 2017 paper
[
143
]
is the 2015 preprint
[
147
]
.

is stated as follows: For
0<\alpha<\beta<1
, select the step length (learning rate)
\epsilon
such that (see, e.g.,
[
149
]
, p. 55):

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})\leq J(\bm{\theta})+\alpha\epsilon\,\bm{g}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\ ,

(146)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\geq\beta\,\bm{g}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}\ .

(147)

The first Wolfe’s rule in Eq. (
146
) is the same as the Armijo’s rule in Eq. (
139
), which ensures that at the updated point
(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})
the cost function value
J(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})
is below the green line
\alpha\epsilon\,\bm{g}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}
in Figure
62
.

The second Wolfe’s rule in Eq. (
147
) is to ensure that at the updated point
(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\,\bm{d})
the slope of the cost function cannot fall below the (negative) slope of the purple line
\beta\,\epsilon\,\bm{g}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{d}
in Figure
62
.

For other variants of line search, we refer to
[
161
]
.

0.6.3
Stochastic gradient-descent (1st-order) methods

To avoid confusion,
139
139

139

See
[
78
]
, p. 271, about this terminology confusion.
The authors of

[
80
]
used “stochastic” optimization to mean optimization using random “minibatches” of examples, and “batch” optimization to mean optimization using “full batch” or full training set of examples.

we will use the terminology “full batch” (instead of just “batch”) when the entire training set is used for training. a minibatch is a small subset of the training set.

In fact, as we shall see, and as mentioned in Remark
0.6.4
, classical optimization methods mentioned in Section
0.6.2
have been developed further to tackle new problems, such as noisy gradients, encountered in deep-learning training with random mini-batches. There is indeed much room for new research on learning rate since:

“The learning rate may be chosen by trial and error. This is more of an art than a science, and most guidance on this subject should be regarded with some skepticism.”
[
78
]
, p. 287.

At the time of this writing, we are aware of two review papers on optimization algorithms for machine learning, and in particular deep learning, aiming particularly at experts in the field:
[
80
]
, as mentioned above, and

[
162
]
[
162
]
. Our review complements these two review papers. We are aiming here at bringing first-time learners up to speed to benefit from, and even to hopefully enjoy, reading these and others related papers.
To this end, we deliberately avoid the dense mathematical-programming language, not familiar to readers outside the field, as used in
[
80
]
, while providing more details on algorithms that have proved important in deep learning than
[
162
]
.

Listed below are the points that distinguish the present paper from other reviews. Similar to
[
78
]
, both
[
80
]
and
[
162
]
:

•

Standard SGD, minibatch, fixed learning-rate schedule

The stochastic gradient descent algorithm, originally introduced
by
Robbins & Monro (1951a)

[
167
]
(another classic) according to many sources,
143
143

143

See, e.g.,
[
80
]
—in which there was a short bio of
Robbins,
the first author of
[
167
]
—and
[
162
]

[
144
]
.

has been playing an important role in training deep-learning networks:

“Nearly all of deep learning is powered by one very important algorithm: stochastic gradient descent (SGD). Stochastic gradient descent is an extension of the gradient descent algorithm.”
[
78
]
, p. 147.

Minibatch.

The number
M
of examples in a training set
\mathbb{X}
could be very large, rendering prohibitively expensive to evaluate the cost function and to compute the gradient of the cost function with respect to the number of parameters, which by itself could also be very large. At iteration
k
within a training session
\tau
, let
\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
be a randomly selected set of
m
indices, which are elements of the training-set indices
[\text{M}]=\{1,\ldots,\text{M}\}
. Typically,
m
is much smaller than
M
:
144
144

144

As of 2010.04.30, the ImageNet database contained more than 14 million images; see
Original website
,
Internet archive
, Figure
3
and Footnote
14
. There is a slight inconsistency in notation in
[
78
]
, where on p. 148,
m
and
m^{\prime}
denote the number of examples in the training set and in the minibatch, respectively, whereas on p. 274,
m
denote the number of examples in a minibatch. In our notation,
m
is the dimension of the output array
\bm{y}
, whereas
m
(in a different font) is the minibatch size; see Footnote
88
. In theory, we write
\text{m}\leq\text{M}
in Eq. (
149
); in practice,
\text{m}\ll\text{M}
.

“The minibatch size
m
is typically chosen to be a relatively small number of examples, ranging from one to a few hundred. Crucially,
m
is usually held fixed as the training set size
M
grows. We may fit a training set with billions of examples using updates computed on only a hundred examples.”
[
78
]
, p. 148.

Generated as in Eq. (
149
), the random-index sets
\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
, for
k=1,2,\ldots
, are non-overlapping such that after
k_{max}=\text{M}/\text{m}
iterations, all examples in the training set are covered, and a training session, or training epoch,
145
145

145

An epoch, or training session,
\tau
is explicitly defined here as when the minibatches as generated in Eqs. (
148
)-(
150
) covered the whole dataset. In
[
78
]
, the first time the word “epoch” appeared was in Figure 7.3 caption, p. 239, where it was defined as a “training iteration”, but there was no explicit definition of “epoch” (when it started and when it ended), except indirectly as a “training pass through the dataset”, p. 274.
See Figure
151
in Section
0.14.7
on “Lack of transparency and irreproducibility of results” in recent deep-learning papers.

is completed (line
4
in Algorithm
4
).
At iteration
k
of a training epoch
\tau
, the random minibatch
\mathbb{B}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
is a set of
m
examples pulled out from the much larger training set
\mathbb{X}
using the random indices in
\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
, with the corresponding targets in the set
\mathbb{T}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
:

\displaystyle M_{1}=\{1,\ldots,\text{M}\}=:[\text{M}]\ ,\ k_{max}=\text{M}/\text{m}

(148)

\displaystyle\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}=\{i_{1,k},\ldots,i_{\text{m},k}\}\subseteq M_{k}\ ,\ M_{k+1}=M_{k}-\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,k_{max}

(149)

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}=\{\bm{x}^{|i_{1,k}|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|i_{\text{m},k}|}\}\subseteq\mathbb{X}=\{\bm{x}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|\text{M}|}\}

(150)

\displaystyle\mathbb{T}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}=\{\bm{y}^{|i_{1,k}|},\cdots,\bm{y}^{|i_{\text{m},k}|}\}\subseteq\mathbb{Y}=\{\bm{y}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{y}^{|\text{M}|}\}

(151)

Note that once the random index set
\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
had been selected, it was deleted from its superset
M_{k}
to form
M_{k+1}
so the next random set
\mathbb{I}_{j+1}^{|\text{m}|}
would not contain indices already selected in
\mathbb{I}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
.

Unlike the iteration counter
k
within a training epoch
\tau
,
the global iteration counter
j
is not reset to 1 at the beginning of a new training epoch
\tau+1
, but continues to increment for each new minibatch.
Plots versus epoch counter
\tau
and plots versus global iteration counter
j
could be confusing; see Remark
0.6.17
and Figure
78
.

Cost and gradient estimates.

The cost-function estimate is the average of the cost functions, each of which is the cost function of an example
\bm{x}^{|i_{k}|}
in the minibatch for iteration
k
in training epoch
\tau
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}J_{i_{a}}(\bm{\theta})\ ,\text{ with }J_{i_{a}}(\bm{\theta})=J(f(\bm{x}^{|i_{a}|},\bm{\theta}),\bm{y}^{|i_{a}|})\ ,\text{ and }\bm{x}^{|i_{a}|}\in\mathbb{B}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}\ ,\bm{y}^{|i_{a}|}\in\mathbb{T}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}\ ,

(152)

where we wrote the random index as
i_{a}
instead of
i_{a,k}
as in Eq. (
149
) to alleviate the notation.
The corresponding gradient estimate is:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}\frac{\partial J_{i_{a}}(\bm{\theta})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}\bm{g}_{i_{a}}\ .

(153)

The pseudocode for the standard SGD
146
146

146

See also
[
78
]
, p. 286, Algorigthm 8.1;
[
80
]
, p. 243, Algorithm 4.1.

is given in Algorithm
4
. The epoch stopping criterion (line
4
in Algorithm
4
) is usually determined by a computation “budget”, i.e., the maximum number of epochs allowed. For example,
[
145
]
set a budget of 1,600 epochs maximum in their numerical examples.

Problems and resurgence of SGD.

There are several known problems with SGD:

“Despite the prevalent use of SGD, it has known challenges and inefficiencies. First, the direction may not represent a descent direction, and second, the method is sensitive to the step-size (learning rate) which is often poorly overestimated.”
[
144
]

For the above reasons, it may not be appropriate to use the norm of the gradient estimate being small as stationarity condition, i.e., where the local minimizer or saddle point is located; see the discussion in
[
145
]
and stochastic Newton Algorithm
7
in Section
0.6.7
.

Despite the above problems, SGD has been brought back to the forefront state-of-the-art algorithm to beat, surpassing the performance of adaptive methods, as confirmed by three recent papers:
[
55
]
,
[
168
]
,
[
56
]
; see Section
0.6.5
on criticism of adaptive methods.

Add-on tricks to improve SGD.

The following tricks can be added onto the vanilla (standard) SGD to improve its performance; see also the pseudocode in Algorithm
4
:

•

Momentum and accelerated gradient: Improve (accelerate) convergence in narrow valleys, Section
0.6.3

•

Initial-step-length tuning: Find effective initial step length
\epsilon_{0}
, Section
0.6.3

•

Step-length decaying or annealing: Find an effective learning-rate schedule

See Figure
151
in Section
0.14.7
on “Lack of transparency and irreproducibility of results” in recent deep-learning papers.

to decrease the step length
\epsilon
as a function of epoch counter
\tau
or global iteration counter

, cyclic annealing, Section
0.6.3

•

Minibatch-size increase, keeping step length fixed, equivalent annealing, Section
0.6.3

•

Weight decay, Section
0.6.3

Momentum and fast (accelerated) gradient

The standard update for gradient descent is Eq. (
133
) would be slow when encountering deep and narrow valley, as shown in Figure
63
, and can be replaced by the general update with momentum as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\gamma_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}))+\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\ ,

(154)

from which the following methods are obtained (line
4
in Algorithm
4
):

•

Standard SGD update Eq. (
133
) with
\gamma_{k}=\zeta_{k}=0

[
49
]

•

SGD with classical momentum:
\gamma_{k}=0
and
\zeta_{k}\in(0,1)
(‘‘small heavy sphere’’ or heavy point mass)
148
148

148

Often called by the more colloquial “heavy ball” method; see Remark
0.6.6
.

[
3
]

•

SGD with fast (accelerated) gradient:
149
149

149

Sometimes referred to as Nesterov’s Accelerated Gradient (NAG) in the deep-learning literature.

\gamma_{k}=\zeta_{k}\in(0,1)
, Nesterov
(1983
[
50
]
, 2018
[
51
]
)

The continuous counterpart of the parameter update Eq. (
154
) with classical momentum, i.e., when
\gamma_{k}\in(0,1)
and
\zeta_{k}=0
, is the equation of motion of a heavy point mass (thus no rotatory inertia) under viscous friction at slow motion (proportional to velocity) and applied force
-\widetilde{\bm{g}}
given below with its discretization by finite difference in time, where
h_{k}
and
h_{k-1}
are the time-step sizes
[
169
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{d^{2}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}}{(dt)^{2}}+\nu\frac{d\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}}{dt}=-\widetilde{\bm{g}}\Rightarrow\displaystyle\frac{\displaystyle\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}}{h_{k}}-\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}}{h_{k-1}}}{h_{k}}+\nu\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}}{h_{k}}=-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,

(155)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})=-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\zeta_{k}=\frac{h_{k-1}}{h_{k}}\frac{1}{1+\nu h_{k}}\text{ and }\epsilon_{k}=(h_{k})^{2}\frac{1}{1+\nu h_{k}}\ ,

(156)

which is the same as the update Eq. (
154
) with
\gamma_{k}=0
. The term
\zeta_{k}(\bm{\theta}_{k}-\bm{\theta}_{k-1})
is often called the “momentum” term since it is proportional to (discretized) velocity.
[
3
]
on the other hand explained the term
\zeta_{k}(\bm{\theta}_{k}-\bm{\theta}_{k-1})
as “giving inertia to the motion,
[leading] to motion along the “essential” direction, i.e. along ‘the bottom of the trough’ ”, and recommended to select
\zeta_{k}\in(0.8,0.99)
, i.e., close to 1, without explanation. The reason is to have low friction, i.e.,
\nu
small, but not zero friction (
\nu=0
), since friction is important to slow down the motion of the sphere up and down the valley sides (like skateboarding from side to side in a half-pipe), thus accelerate convergence toward the trough of the valley; from Eq. (
156
), we have

\displaystyle h_{k}=h_{k-1}=h\text{ and }\nu\in[0,+\infty)\Rightarrow\zeta_{k}\in(0,1]\ ,\text{ with }\nu=0\Rightarrow\zeta_{k}=1

(157)

Remark 0.6.5
.

The choice of the momentum parameter
\zeta
in Eq. (
154
) is not trivial. If
\zeta
is too small, the signal will be too noisy; if
\zeta
is too large, “the average will lag too far behind the (drifting) signal”
[
165
]
, p. 212.
Even though Polyak (1964)
[
3
]
recommended to select
\zeta\in(0.8,0.99)
, as explained above,
it was reported in
[
78
]
, p. 290:
“Common values of
\zeta
used in practice include 0.5, 0.9, and 0.99. Like the learning rate,
\zeta
may also be adapted over time. Typically it begins with a small value and is later raised. Adapting
\zeta
over time is less important than shrinking
\epsilon
over time”.
The value of
\zeta=0.5
would correspond to relatively high friction
\mu
, slowing down the motion of the sphere, compared to
\zeta=0.99
.

68
from
[
170
]
shows the convergence of some adaptive learning-rate algorithms:
AdaGrad
,
RMSProp
,

SGDNesterov
(accelerated gradient),
AdaDelta
,
Adam
.

In their remarkable paper, the authors of
[
55

[

]
used a constant momentum parameter
\zeta=0.9
; see
criticism of adaptive methods
in Section
0.6.5
and Figure
73
comparing
SGD
,
SGD with momentum
,
AdaGrad
,

Original website

\hfill\blacksquare

For more insight into the update Eq. (
156
), consider the case of constant coefficients
\zeta_{k}=\zeta
and
\epsilon_{k}=\epsilon
, and rewrite this recursive relation in the form:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}=-\epsilon\sum_{i=0}^{k}\zeta^{i}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k-i}\ ,\text{ using }\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{0}=-\epsilon\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{0}\ ,

(158)

i.e., without momentum for the first term. So the effective gradient is the sum of all gradients from the beginning
i=0
until the present
i=k
weighted by the exponential function
\zeta^{i}
so there is a fading memory effect, i.e., gradients that are farther back in time have less influence than those closer to the present time.
151
151

151

See also Section
0.6.5
on time series and exponential smoothing.

The summation term in Eq. (
158
) also provides an explanation of how the “inertia” (or momentum) term work: (1) Two successive opposite gradients would cancel each other, whereas (2) Two successive gradients in the same direction (toward the trough of the valley) would reinforce each other. See also
[
171
]
, pp. 104-105, and
[
172
]
who provided a similar explanation:

“Momentum is a simple method for increasing the speed of learning when the objective function contains long, narrow and fairly straight ravines with a gentle but consistent gradient along the floor of the ravine and much steeper gradients up the sides of the ravine. The momentum method simulates a heavy ball rolling down a surface. The ball builds up velocity along the floor of the ravine, but not across the ravine because the opposing gradients on opposite sides of the ravine cancel each other out over time.”

In recent years, Polyak (1964)

3
]
(English version)

Remark 0.6.6
.

Small heavy sphere, or heavy point mass, is better name.

Because the rotatory motion is not considered in Eq. (
155
), the name “small heavy sphere” given in
[
3
]
is more precise than the more colloquial name ‘‘heavy ball’’ often given to the SGD with classical momentum,
154
154

154

See, e.g.,
[
171
]
, p. 104,
[
180
]
, p. 115,
[
169
]
,
[
181
]
.

since ‘‘small’’ implies that rotatory motion was neglected, and a ‘‘heavy ball’’ could be as big as a bowling ball
155
155

155

Or the “Times Square Ball”, Wikipedia,

.

for which rotatory motion cannot be neglected. For this reason, “heavy point mass” would be a precise alternative name.

Remark 0.6.7
.

For Nesterov’s fast (accelerated) gradient method, many references referred to
[
50
]
.
156
156

156

Reference
[
50
]
cannot be found from the Web of Science as of 2020.03.18, perhaps because it was in Russian, as indicated in Ref. [35] in
[
51
]
, p. 582, where Nesterov’s 2004 monograph was Ref. [39].

The authors of
[
78
]
, p. 291, also referred to Nesterov’s 2004 monograph, which was mentioned in the Preface of, and the material of which was included in,
[
51
]
.
For a special class of strongly convex functions,
157
157

157

A function
f(\cdot)
is strongly convex if there is a constant
\mu>0
such that for any two points
x
and
y
, we have
f(y)\geq f(x)+\langle\nabla f(x),y-x\rangle+\frac{1}{2}\mu\parallel y-x\parallel^{2}
, where
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is the inner (or dot) product,
[
51
]
, p. 74.

the step length can be kept constant, while the coefficients in Nesterov’s fast gradient method varied, to achieve optimal performance,
[
51
]
, p. 92.
“Unfortunately, in the stochastic gradient case, Nesterov momentum does not improve the rate of convergence”
[
78
]
, p. 292.
