Initial-step-length tuning

The initial step length
\epsilon_{0}
, or learning-rate initial value, is one of the two most influential hyperparameters to tune, i.e., to find the best performing values. During tuning, the step length
\epsilon
is kept constant at
\epsilon_{0}
in the parameter update Eq. (
133
) throughout the optimization process, i.e., a fixed step length is used, without decay as in Eqs. (
160
-
163
) in Section
0.6.3
.

The following simple tuning method was proposed in
[
55
]
:

“To tune the step sizes, we evaluated a logarithmically-spaced grid of five step sizes. If the best performance was ever at one of the extremes of the grid, we would try new grid points so that the best performance was contained in the middle of the parameters. For example, if we initially tried step sizes 2, 1, 0.5, 0.25, and 0.125 and found that 2 was the best performing, we would have tried the step size 4 to see if performance was improved. If performance improved, we would have tried 8
and so on.”

The above logarithmically-spaced grid was given by
2^{k}
, with
k=1,0,-1,-2,-3
. This tuning method appears effective as shown in Figure
73
on the CIFAR-10 dataset mentioned above, for which the following values for
\epsilon_{0}
had been tried for different optimizers, even though the values did not always belong to the sequence
\{a^{k}\}
, but could include close, rounded values:

•

SGD
(Section
0.6.3
): 2, 1, 0.5 (best), 0.25, 0.05, 0.01
158
158

158

The last two values
\{0.05,0.01\}
did not belong to the sequence
2^{k}
, with
k
being integers, since
2^{-3}=0.125
,
2^{-4}=0.0625
and
2^{-5}=0.03125
.

•

SGD with momentum
(Section
0.6.3
): 2, 1, 0.5 (best), 0.25, 0.05, 0.01

•

AdaGrad
(Section
0.6.5
): 0.1, 0.05, 0.01 (best, default), 0.0075, 0.005

•

RMSProp
(Section
0.6.5
): 0.005, 0.001, 0.0005, 0.0003 (best), 0.0001

•

Adam
(Section
0.6.5
): 0.005, 0.001 (default), 0.0005, 0.0003 (best), 0.0001, 0.00005

Step-length decay, annealing and cyclic annealing

In the update of the parameter
\bm{\theta}
as in Eq. (
133
), the learning rate (step length)
\epsilon
has to be reduced gradually as a function of either the epoch counter
\tau
or of the global iteration counter
j
. Let
t
represents either
\tau
or
j
, depending on user’s choice.
159
159

159

The Avant Garde font
t
is used to avoid confusion with
t
, the time variable used in relation to recurrent neural networks; see Section
0.7
on “Dynamics, sequential data, sequence modeling”, and Section
0.13.2
on “Dynamics, time dependence, Volterra series”. Many papers on deep-learning optimizers used
t
as global iteration counter, which is denoted by
j
here; see, e.g.,
[
182
]
[
182
]
,
[
56
]
.

If the learning rate
\epsilon
is a function of epoch
\tau
, then
\epsilon
is held constant in all iterations
k=1,\ldots,k_{max}
within epoch
\tau
, and we have the relation:

\displaystyle j=(\tau-1)*k_{max}+k\ .

(159)

The following learning-rate scheduling, linear with respect to
t
, is one option:
160
160

160

See
[
78
]
, p. 287, where it was suggested that
\text{t}_{c}
in Eq. (
160
) would be “set to the number of iterations required to make a few hundred passes through the training set,” and
\epsilon_{\text{t}_{c}}
“should be set to roughly 1 percent the value of
\epsilon_{0}
”. A “few hundred passes through the training set” means a few hundred epochs; see Footnote
161
. In
[
78
]
, p. 286, Algorithm 1 SGD for “training iteration
k
” should mean for “training epoch
k
”, and the learning rate “
\epsilon_{k}
” would be held constant within “epoch
k
”.

\displaystyle\epsilon(\text{t})=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\left(1-\frac{\text{t}}{\text{t}_{c}}\right)\epsilon_{0}+\frac{\text{t}}{\text{t}_{c}}\epsilon_{\text{t}_{c}}&\text{ for }0\leq\text{t}\leq\text{t}_{c}\\
\epsilon_{\text{t}_{c}}&\text{ for }\text{t}_{c}\leq\text{t}\end{cases}

(160)

\displaystyle\text{t}=\text{epoch }\tau\text{ or global iteration }j

(161)

where
\epsilon_{0}
is the learning-rate initial value, and
\epsilon_{\text{t}_{c}}
the constant learning-rate value when
\text{t}\geq\text{t}_{c}
. Other possible learning-rate schedules are:
161
161

161

See
[
182
]
, p. 3, below Algorithm 1 and just below the equation labeled “(Sgd)”. After, say, 400 global iterations, i.e.,
\text{t}=j=400
, then
\epsilon_{400}=5\%\,\epsilon_{0}
according to Eq. (
162
), and
\epsilon_{400}=0.25\%\,\epsilon_{0}
according to Eq. (
163
), whereas
\epsilon_{400}=1\%\,\epsilon_{0}
according to Eq. (
160
). See Footnote
160
, and also
Figure
151
in Section
0.14.7
on “Lack of transparency and irreproducibility of results” in recent deep-learning papers.

\displaystyle\epsilon(\text{t})=\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\sqrt{\text{t}}}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }\text{t}\rightarrow\infty\ ,

(162)

\displaystyle\epsilon(\text{t})=\frac{\epsilon_{0}}{\text{t}}\rightarrow 0\text{ as }\text{t}\rightarrow\infty\ ,

(163)

with
t
defined as in Eq. (
160
), even though authors such as
[
182
]
and
[
183
]
used Eq. (
162
) and Eq. (
163
) with
\text{t}=j
as global iteration counter.

Another step-length decay method proposed in
[
55
]
is to reduce the step length
\epsilon(\tau)
for the current epoch
\tau
by a factor
\varpi\in(0,1)
when the cost estimate
\widetilde{J}_{\tau-1}
at the end of the last epoch
(\tau-1)
is greater than the lowest cost in all previous global iterations, with
\widetilde{J}_{j}
denoting the cost estimate at global iteration
j
, and
k_{max}(\tau-1)
the global iteration number at the end of epoch
(\tau-1)
:

\displaystyle\epsilon(\tau)=\begin{cases}\varpi\,\epsilon(\tau-1)&\text{ if }\widetilde{J}_{\tau-1}>\min\limits_{j}\{\widetilde{J}_{j},j=1,\ldots,k_{max}(\tau-1)\}\\
\epsilon(\tau-1)&\text{ Otherwise}\end{cases}

(164)

Recall,
k_{max}
is the number of non-overlapping minibatches that cover the training set, as defined in Eq. (
148
).

[
55
]
set the step-length decay parameter
\varpi=0.9
in their numerical examples, in particular Figure
73
.

Cyclic annealing.
In additional to decaying the step length
\epsilon
, which is already annealing, cyclic annealing is introduced to further reduce the step length down to zero (“cooling”), quicker than decaying, then bring the step length back up rapidly (heating), and doing so for several cycles. The cosine function is typically used, such as shown in Figure
75
, as a multiplicative factor
\mathfrak{a}_{k}\in[0,1]
to the step length
\epsilon_{k}
in the parameter update, and thus the name “cosine annealing”:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\mathfrak{a}_{k}\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,

(165)

as an add-on to the parameter update for vanilla SGD Eq. (
133
), or

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\mathfrak{a}_{k}\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\gamma_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}))+\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})

(166)

as an add-on to the parameter update for SGD with momentum and accelerated gradient Eq. (
154
). The cosine annealing factor can take the form
[
56
]
:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{k}=0.5+0.5\cos(\pi T_{cur}/T_{p})\in[0,1]\ ,\text{ with }T_{cur}:=j-\sum_{q=1}^{q=p-1}T_{q}

(167)

where
T_{cur}
is the number of epochs from the start of the last warm restart at the end of epoch
\sum_{q=1}^{q=p-1}T_{q}
, where
\mathfrak{a}_{k}=1
(“maximum heating”),
j
the current global iteration counter,
T_{p}
the maximum number of epochs allowed for the current
p
th annealing cycle, during which
T_{cur}
would go from
0
to
T_{p}
, when
\mathfrak{a}_{k}=0
(“complete cooling”). Figure
75
shows 4 annealing cycles, which helped reduce dramatically the number of epochs needed to achieve the same lower cost as obtained without annealing.

Figure
74
shows the effectiveness of cosine annealing in bringing down the cost rapidly in the early stage, but there is a diminishing return, as the cost reduction decreases with the number of annealing cycle. Up to a point, it is no longer as effective as SGD with weight decay in Section
0.6.3
.

Convergence conditions.

The sufficient conditions for convergence, for convex functions, are
162
162

162

Eq. (
168
) are called the “stepsize requirements” in
[
80
]
, and “sufficient condition for convergence” in
[
78
]
, p. 287, and in
[
184
]
.
Robbins & Monro (1951b)

[
49
]
were concerned with solving
M(x)=\alpha
when the function
M(\cdot)
is not known, but the distribution of the output, as a random variable,
\textup{y}=\textup{y}(x)
is assumed known. For the network training problem at hand, one can think of
M(x)=\parallel\nabla J(x)\parallel
, i.e., the magnitude of the gradient of the cost function
J
at
x=\parallel\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}-\bm{\theta}\parallel
, the distance from a local minimizer, and
\alpha=0
, i.e., the stationarity point of
J(\cdot)
. In
[
49
]
—in which there was no notion of “epoch
\tau
” but only global iteration counter
j
—Eq. (6) on p. 401 corresponds to Eq. (
168
)
1
(first part), and Eq. (27) on p. 404 corresponds to Eq. (
168
)
2
(second part). Any sequence
\{\epsilon_{k}\}
that satisfied Eq. (
168
) was called a sequence of type
1/k
; the convergence Theorem 1 on p. 404 and Theorem 2 on p. 405 indicated that the sequence of step length
\{\epsilon_{k}\}
being of type
1/k
was only one among other sufficient conditions for convergence. In Theorem 2 of
[
49
]
, the additional sufficient conditions were Eq. (33),
M(x)=\parallel\nabla J(x)\parallel
non decreasing, Eq. (34),
M(0)=\parallel\nabla J(0)\parallel=0
, and Eq. (35),
M^{\prime}(0)=\parallel\nabla^{2}J(0)\parallel>0
, i.e., the iterates
\{x_{k}\,|\,k=1,2,\ldots\}
, fell into a local convex bowl.

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\epsilon_{j}^{2}<\infty\ ,\text{ and }\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\epsilon_{j}=\infty\ .

(168)

The inequality on the left of Eq. (
168
), i.e., the sum of the squared of the step lengths being finite, ensures that the step length would decay quickly to reach the minimum, but is valid only when the minibatch size is fixed. The equation on the right of Eq. (
168
) ensures convergence, no matter how far the initial guess was from the minimum
[
164
]
[
164
]
.

In Section
0.6.3
, the step-length decay is shown to be equivalent to minibatch-size increase and simulated annealing in the sense that there would be less fluctuation, and thus lower “temperature” (cooling) by analogy to the physics governed by the Langevin stochastic differential equation and its discrete version, which is analogous to the network parameter update.

Minibatch-size increase, fixed step length, equivalent annealing

The minibatch parameter update from Eq. (
154
), without momentum and accelerated gradient, which becomes Eq. (
133
), can be rewritten to introduce the error due to the use of the minibatch gradient estimate
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
instead of the full-batch gradient
\bm{g}
as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}

(
133
)

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\left[\bm{g}_{k}+\left(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}-\bm{g}_{k}\right)\right]\Rightarrow\frac{\Delta\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}}{\epsilon_{k}}=\frac{\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}}{\epsilon_{k}}=-\bm{g}_{k}+\left(\bm{g}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\right)=-\bm{g}_{k}+\mathfrak{e}_{k}\ ,

(169)

where
\bm{g}_{k}=\bm{g}(\bm{\theta}_{k})
and
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{b}(\bm{\theta}_{k})
, with
b=k
, and
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{b}(\cdot)
is the gradient estimate function using minibatch
b=1,\ldots,k_{max}
.

To show that the gradient error has zero mean (average), based on the linearity of the expectation function
\mathbb{E}(\cdot)=\langle\cdot\rangle
defined in Eq. (
80
) (Footnote
89
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle\alpha\bm{u}+\beta\bm{v}\rangle=\alpha\langle\bm{u}\rangle+\beta\langle\bm{u}\rangle\ ,

(170)

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{e}_{k}\rangle=\langle\bm{g}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle=\langle\bm{g}_{k}\rangle-\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle=\bm{g}_{k}-\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle\ ,

(171)

from Eqs. (
148
)-(
150
) on the definition of minibatches and Eqs. (
152
)-(
153
) on the definition of the cost and gradient estimates (without omitting the iteration counter
k
), we have

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{k}=\bm{g}(\bm{\theta}_{k})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{b=1}^{b=k_{max}}\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}}\bm{g}_{i_{a,b}}\Rightarrow\langle\bm{g}_{k}\rangle=\bm{g}_{k}=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{b=1}^{b=k_{max}}\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}}\langle\bm{g}_{i_{a,b}}\rangle=\langle\bm{g}_{i_{a,b}}\rangle

(172)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{a=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}\bm{g}_{i_{a,k}}\Rightarrow\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=k_{max}}\langle\bm{g}_{i_{a,k}}\rangle=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=k_{max}}\langle\bm{g}_{k}\rangle=\bm{g}_{k}\Rightarrow\langle\mathfrak{e}_{k}\rangle=0\ .

(173)

Or alternatively, the same result can be obtained with:

\displaystyle\bm{g}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{b=1}^{b=k_{max}}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{b}(\bm{\theta}_{k})\Rightarrow\bm{g}_{k}=\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{b}(\bm{\theta}_{k})\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}(\bm{\theta}_{k})\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle\Rightarrow\langle\mathfrak{e}_{k}\rangle=0\ .

(174)

Next, the mean value of the “square” of the gradient error, i.e.,
\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle
, in which we omitted the iteration counter subscript
k
to alleviate the notation, relies on some identities related to the covariance matrix
\langle\mathfrak{e},\mathfrak{e}\rangle
.
The mean of the square matrix
\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}^{T}\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}
, where
\{\bm{\textup{x}}_{i},\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\}
are two random
row
matrices, is the sum of the product of the mean values and the covariance matrix of these matrices
163
163

163

See, e.g.,
[
185
]
, p. 36, Eq. (2.8.3).

\displaystyle\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}^{T}\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle=\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}\rangle^{T}\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle+\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i},\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle\ ,\text{ or }\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i},\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle=\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}^{T}\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle-\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}\rangle^{T}\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle\ ,

(175)

where
\langle\bm{\textup{x}}_{i},\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}\rangle
is the covariance matrix of
\bm{\textup{x}}_{i}
and
\bm{\textup{x}}_{j}
, and thus the covariance operator
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle
is bilinear due to the linearity of the mean (expectation) operator
\langle\cdot\rangle
in Eq. (
170
):

\displaystyle\left\langle\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\bm{u}_{i},\sum_{j}\beta_{j}\bm{v}_{j}\right\rangle=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}\beta_{j}\langle\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\rangle\ ,\forall\alpha_{i},\beta_{j}\in\mathbb{R}\text{ and }\forall\bm{u}_{i},\bm{v}_{j}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}\text{ random}\ .

(176)

Eq. (
176
) is the key relation to derive an expression for the square of the gradient error
\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle
, which can be rewritten as the sum of four covariance matrices upon using Eq. (
175
)
1
and either Eq. (
173
) or Eq. (
174
), i.e.,
\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\rangle=\langle\bm{g}_{k}\rangle=\bm{g}_{k}
, as the four terms
\bm{g}_{k}^{T}\bm{g}_{k}
cancel each other out:

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle=\langle(\widetilde{\bm{g}}-\bm{g})^{T}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}-\bm{g})\rangle=\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}},\widetilde{\bm{g}}\rangle-\langle\widetilde{\bm{g}},\bm{g}\rangle-\langle\bm{g},\widetilde{\bm{g}}\rangle+\langle\bm{g},\bm{g}\rangle\ ,

(177)

where the iteration counter
k
had been omitted to alleviate the notation. Moreover, to simplify the notation further, the gradient related to an example is simply denoted by
\bm{g}_{a}
or
\bm{g}_{b}
, with
a,b=1,\ldots,\text{m}
for a minibatch, and
a,b=1,\ldots,\text{M}
for the full batch:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{\text{m}}\bm{g}_{i_{a},k}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{\text{m}}\bm{g}_{i_{a}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{\text{m}}\bm{g}_{a}\ ,

(178)

\displaystyle\bm{g}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{k_{max}}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{a=1}^{\text{m}}\bm{g}_{i_{a},k}=\frac{1}{\text{M}}\sum_{b=1}^{\text{M}}\bm{g}_{b}\ .

(179)

Now assume the covariance matrix of any pair of single-example gradients
\bm{g}_{a}
and
\bm{g}_{b}
depends only on the parameters
\bm{\theta}
, and is of the form:

\displaystyle\langle\bm{g}_{a},\bm{g}_{b}\rangle=\mathfrak{C}(\bm{\theta})\delta_{ab}\ ,\forall a,b\in\{1,\ldots,\text{M}\}\ ,

(180)

where
\delta_{ab}
is the Kronecker delta.
Using Eqs. (
178
)-(
179
) and Eq. (
180
) in Eq. (
177
), we obtain a simple expression for
\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle
:
164
164

164

Eq. (
181
) possesses a simplicity elegance compared to the expression
\langle\alpha^{2}\rangle=N(N/B-1)F(\omega)
in
[
164
]
, based on a different definition of gradient, with
N\equiv\text{M}
,
B\equiv\text{m}
,
\omega\equiv\bm{\theta}
, but
F(\omega)\neq\mathfrak{C}(\bm{\theta})
.

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle=\left(\frac{1}{\text{m}}-\frac{1}{\text{M}}\right)\mathfrak{C}\ .

(181)

The authors of
[
164
]
introduced the following stochastic differential equation as a continuous counterpart of the discrete parameter update Eq. (
169
), as
\epsilon_{k}\rightarrow 0
:

\displaystyle\frac{d\bm{\theta}}{dt}=-\bm{g}+\mathfrak{n}(t)=-\frac{dJ}{d\bm{\theta}}+\mathfrak{n}(t)\ ,

(182)

where
\mathfrak{n}(t)
is the noise function, the continuous counterpart of the gradient error
\mathfrak{e}_{k}:=\left(\bm{g}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\right)
. The noise
\mathfrak{n}(t)
is assumed to be Gaussian, i.e., with zero expectation (mean) and with covariance function of the form (see Remark
0.6.9
on Langevin stochastic differential equation):

\displaystyle\langle\mathfrak{n}(t)\rangle=0\ ,\text{ and }\langle\mathfrak{n}(t)\mathfrak{n}(t^{\prime})\rangle={F}\mathfrak{C}(\bm{\theta})\delta(t-t^{\prime}])\ ,

(183)

where
\mathbb{E}[\cdot]=\langle\cdot\rangle
is the expectation of a function,
{F}
the “noise scale” or fluctuation factor,
\mathfrak{C}(\bm{\theta})
the same gradient-error covariance matrix in Eq. (
180
), and
\delta(t-t^{\prime})
the Dirac delta. Integrating Eq. (
182
), we obtain:

\displaystyle\int\limits_{t=0}^{t=\epsilon_{k}}\frac{d\bm{\theta}_{k}}{dt}dt=\bm{\theta}_{k+1}-\bm{\theta}_{k}=-\epsilon_{k}\bm{g}_{k}+\int\limits_{t=0}^{t=\epsilon_{k}}\mathfrak{n}(t)dt\ ,\text{ and }\left\langle\int\limits_{t=0}^{t=\epsilon_{k}}\mathfrak{n}(t)dt\right\rangle=\int\limits_{t=0}^{t=\epsilon_{k}}\langle\mathfrak{n}(t)\rangle dt=0\ .

(184)

The fluctuation factor
{F}
can be identified by equating the square of the error in Eq. (
169
) to that in Eq. (
184
), i.e.,

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}\langle\mathfrak{e}^{T}\mathfrak{e}\rangle=\int\limits_{t=0}^{t=\epsilon}\int\limits_{t^{\prime}=0}^{t^{\prime}=\epsilon}\langle\mathfrak{n}(t)\mathfrak{n}(t^{\prime})\rangle dt^{\prime}dt\Rightarrow\epsilon^{2}\left(\frac{1}{\text{m}}-\frac{1}{\text{M}}\right)\mathfrak{C}=\epsilon{F}\mathfrak{C}\Rightarrow{F}=\epsilon\left(\frac{1}{\text{m}}-\frac{1}{\text{M}}\right)\ ,

(185)

Figure 64
:

Optimal minibatch size vs. training-set size
(Section
0.6.3
). For a given training-set size, the smallest minibatch size that achieves the highest accuracy is optimal.
Left figure: The optimal mimibatch size was moving to the right with increasing training-set size
M
.
Right figure:
The optimal minibatch size in
[
186
]
is linearly proportional to the training-set size
M
for large training sets (i.e.,
\text{M}\rightarrow\infty
), Eq. (
186
), but our fluctuation factor
{F}
is independent of
M
when
\text{M}\rightarrow\infty
; see Remark
0.6.8
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Remark 0.6.8
.

Fluctuation factor for large training set.

For large
M
, our fluctuation factor
{F}
is roughly proportional to the ratio of the step length over the minibatch size, i.e.,
{F}\approx\epsilon/\text{m}
. Thus step-length
\epsilon
decay, or equivalenly minibatch size
m
increase, corresponds to a decrease in the fluctuation factor
{F}
. On the other hand,
[
164
]
obtained their fluctuation factor
{G}
as
165
165

165

In
[
186
]
and
[
164
]
, the fluctuation factor was expressed, in original notation, as
g=\epsilon(N/B-1)
, where the equivalence with our notation is
g\equiv{G}
(fluctuation factor),
N\equiv\text{M}
(training-set size),
B\equiv\text{m}
(minibatch size).

\displaystyle{G}=\epsilon\left(\frac{\text{M}}{\text{m}}-1\right)=\epsilon\text{M}\left(\frac{1}{\text{m}}-\frac{1}{\text{M}}\right)=\text{M}{F}

(186)

since their cost function was not an average, i.e., not divided by the minibatch size
m
, unlike our cost function in Eq. (
152
). When
\text{M}\rightarrow\infty
, our fluctuation factor
{F}\rightarrow\epsilon/\text{m}
in Eq. (
185
), but their fluctuation factor
{G}\approx\epsilon\text{M}/\text{m}\rightarrow\infty
, i.e., for increasingly large
M
, our fluctuation factor
{F}
is bounded, but not their fluctuation factor
{G}
.

[
186
]
then went on to show empirically that their fluctation factor
{G}
was proportional to the training-set size
M
for large
M
, as shown in Figure
64
.
On the other hand, our fluctuation factor
{F}
does not depend on the training set size
M
. As a result, unlike
[
186
]
in Figure
64
, our optimal minibatch size would not depend of the training-set size
M
.

\hfill\blacksquare

]

Remark 0.6.9
.

Langevin stochastic differential equation, annealing.

Because the fluctuation factor
{F}
was proportional to the step length, and in physics, fluctuation decreases with temperature (cooling),
‘‘decaying the learning rate (step length) is simulated annealing’’
169
169

169

“In metallurgy and materials science, annealing is a heat treatment that alters the physical and sometimes chemical properties of a material to increase its ductility and reduce its hardness, making it more workable. It involves heating a material above its recrystallization temperature, maintaining a suitable temperature for a suitable amount of time, and then allow slow cooling.” Wikepedia, ‘Annealing (metallurgy)’, Version
11:06, 26 November 2019
.
The name “simulated annealing” came from the highly cited paper
[
163
]
[
163
]
, which received more than 20,000 citations on Web of Science and more than 40,000 citations on Google Scholar as of 2020.01.17. See also Remark
0.6.10
on “Metaheuristics”.

[
164
]
.
Here, we will connect the step length to “temperature” based on the analogy of Eq. (
182
), the continuous counterpart of the parameter update Eq. (
169
). In particular, we point exact references that justify the assumptions in Eq. (
183
).

Even though
the authors of

[
164
]
referred to
[
187
]
[
187
]
for Eq. (
182
), the decomposition of the parameter update in
[
187
]
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\bm{g}_{k}+\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}}\mathfrak{v}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\mathfrak{v}_{k}:=\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}}\left[\bm{g}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\right]=\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}}\mathfrak{e}_{k}\ ,

(187)

with the intriguing factor
\sqrt{\epsilon_{k}}
was consistent with the equivalent expression in
[
185
]
, p. 53, Eq. (3.5.10),
170
170

170

In original notation used in
[
185
]
, p. 53, Eq. (3.5.10) reads as
\bm{y}(t+\Delta t)=\bm{y}(t)+\bm{A}(\bm{y}(t),t)\Delta t+\bm{\eta}(t)\sqrt{\Delta t}
, in which the noise
\bm{\eta}(t)
has zero mean, i.e.,
\langle\bm{\eta}\rangle=0
, and covariance matrix
\langle\bm{\eta}(t),\bm{\eta}(t)\rangle=\bm{B}(t)
.

which was obtained from the Fokker-Planck equation:

\displaystyle\bm{\theta}(t+\Delta t)=\bm{\theta}(t)+\bm{A}(\bm{\theta}(t),t)\Delta(t)+\sqrt{\Delta t}\bm{\eta}(t)\ ,

(188)

where
\bm{A}(\bm{\theta}(t),t)
is a nonlinear operator. The noise term
\sqrt{\Delta t}\bm{\eta}(t)
is
not
related to the gradient error as in Eq. (
187
), and is Gaussian with zero mean and covariance matrix of the form:

\displaystyle\sqrt{\Delta t}\langle\bm{\eta}(t)\rangle=0\ ,\text{ and }\Delta t\langle\bm{\eta}(t),\bm{\eta}(t)\rangle=\Delta t\bm{B}(t)\ .

(189)

The column matrix (or vector)
\bm{A}(\bm{\theta}(t),t)
in Eq. (
188
) is called the
drift vector
, and the square matrix
\bm{B}
in Eq. (
189
) the
diffusion matrix
,
[
185
]
, p. 52.
Eq. (
188
) implies that
\bm{\theta}(\cdot)
is a continuous function, called the “sample path”.

To obtain a differential equation, Eq. (
188
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle\frac{\bm{\theta}(t+\Delta t)-\bm{\theta}(t)}{\Delta t}=\bm{A}(\bm{\theta}(t),t)+\frac{\bm{\eta}(t)}{\sqrt{\Delta t}}\ ,

(190)

which shows that the derivative of
\bm{\theta}(t)
does not exist when taking the limit as
\Delta t\rightarrow 0
, not only due to the factor
1/\sqrt{\Delta t}\rightarrow\infty
, but also due to the noise
\bm{\eta}(t)
,
[
185
]
, p. 53.

The last term
\bm{\eta}/\sqrt{\Delta t}
in Eq. (
190
) corresponds to the random force
X
exerted on a pollen particle by the viscous fluid molecules in the 1-D equation of motion of the pollen particle, as derived by Langevin and in his original notation,
[
188
]
:
171
171

171

See also “Langevin equation”, Wikipedia,
version 17:40, 3 December 2019
.

\displaystyle m\frac{d^{2}x}{dt^{2}}=-6\pi\mu a\frac{dx}{dt}+X(t)\Rightarrow m\frac{dv}{dt}=-\mathfrak{f}v+X(t)\ ,\text{ with }v=\frac{dx}{dt}\text{ and }\mathfrak{f}=6\pi\mu a\ ,

(191)

where
m
is the mass of the pollen particle,
x(t)
its displacement,
\mu
the fluid viscosity,
a
the particle radius,
v(t)
the particle velocity, and
\mathfrak{f}
the friction coefficient between the particle and the fluid.
The random (noise) force
X(t)
by the fluid molecules impacting the pollen particle is assumed to (1) be independent of the position
x
, (2) vary extremely rapidly compared to the change of
x
, (3) have zero mean as in Eq. (
189
). The covariance of this noise force
X
is proportional to the absolute temperature
T
, and takes the form,
[
189
]
[
189
]
, p. 12,
172
172

172

For first-time learners, here a guide for further reading on a derivation of Eq. (
192
). It is better to follow the book
[
189
]
, rather than Coffey’s 1985 long review paper, cited for equation
\overline{X(t)X(t^{\prime})}=2\mathfrak{f}kT\delta(t-t^{\prime})
on p. 12, not exactly the same left-hand side as in Eq. (
192
), and for which the derivation appeared some 50 pages later in the book. The factor
2\mathfrak{f}kT
was called the
spectral density
.
The time average
\overline{X(t)X(t^{\prime})}
was defined, and the name
autocorrelation function
introduced on p. 13. But a particular case of the more general autocorrelation function is
\langle X(t)X(t^{\prime})\rangle
was defined on p. 59, and by the ergodic theorem,
\overline{X(t)X(t^{\prime})}=\langle X(t)X(t^{\prime})\rangle
for stationary processes, p. 60, where the spectral density of
X(t)
, denoted by
\Phi_{X}(\omega)
was defined as the Fourier transform of the autocorrelation function
\langle X(t)X(t^{\prime})\rangle
. The derivation of Eq. (
192
) started from the beginning of Section 1.7, on p. 60, with the result obtained on p. 62, where the confusion of using the same notation
D
in
2D=2\mathfrak{f}kT
, the spectral density, and in
D=kT/\mathfrak{f}
, the diffusion coefficient [
[
189
]
, p. 20,
[
185
]
, p. 7] was noted.

\displaystyle\langle X(t),X(t^{\prime})\rangle=2\mathfrak{f}kT\delta(t-t^{\prime})\ ,

(192)

where
k
denotes the Boltzmann constant.

The covariance of the noise
\mathfrak{n}(t)
in Eq. (
183
) is similar to the covariance of the noise
X(t)
in Eq. (
192
), and thus the fluctuation factor
{F}
, and hence the step length
\epsilon
in Eq. (
185
), can be interpreted as being proportional to temperature
T
. Therefore, decaying the step length
\epsilon
, or increasing the minibatch size
m
, is equivalent to cooling down the temperature
T
, and simulating the physical annealing, and hence the name
simulated annealing
(see Remark
0.6.10
).

Eq. (
191
) cannot be directly integrated to obtain the velocity
v
in terms of the noise force
X
since the derivative does not exist, as interpreted in Eq. (
190
).
Langevin went around this problem by multiplying Eq. (
191
) by the displacement
x(t)
and take the average to obtain,
[
188
]
:

\displaystyle\frac{m}{2}\frac{dz}{dt}=-3\pi\mu az+\frac{RT}{N}\ ,

(193)

where
z=d\overline{(x^{2})}/dt
is the time derivative of the mean square displacement,
R
the ideal gas constant, and
N
the Avogadro number. Eq. (
193
) can be integrated to yield an expression for
z
, which led to Einstein’s result for Brownian motion.

Remark 0.6.10
.

Metaheuristics and nature-inspired optimization algorithms.

There is a large class of nature-inspired optimization algorithms that implemented the general conceptual
metaheuristics
—such as neighborhood search, multi-start, hill climbing, accepting negative moves, etc.—and that include many well-known methods such as Evolutionary Algorithms (EAs), Artificial Bee Colony (ABC), Firefly Algorithm, etc.

[
190
]
.

The most famous of these nature-inspired algorithms would be perhaps simulated annealing in
[
163
]
, which is described in
[
191
]
, p. 18, as being “inspired by the annealing process of metals. It is a trajectory-based search algorithm starting with an initial guess solution at a high temperature and gradually cooling down the system. A move or new solution is accepted if it is better; otherwise, it is accepted with a probability, which makes it possible for the system to escape any local optima”, i.e., the metaheuristic “accepting negative moves” mentioned in
[
190
]
. “It is then expected that if the system is cooled down slowly enough, the global optimal solution can be reached”,
[
191
]
, p. 18; that’s step-length decay or minibatch-size increase, as mentioned above. See also Footnotes
140
and
169
.

For applications of these nature-inspired algorithms, we cite the following works, without detailed review:

[
191
]

[
192
]
[
192
]

[
193
]
[
193
]

[
194
]
[
194
]

[
195
]

[
196
]
[
196
]

[
197
]

[
198
]
[
198
]

[
199
]
[
199
]
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Weight decay, avoiding overfit

Reducing, or decaying, the network parameters
\bm{\theta}
(which include the weights and the biases) is one method to avoid overfitting by adding a parameter-decay term to the update equation:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}-\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\ ,

(194)

where
\mathfrak{d}\in(0,1)
is the decay parameter, and there the name “weight decay”, which is equivalent to SGD with
L_{2}
regularization, by adding an extra penalty term in the cost function; see Eq. (
261
) in Section
0.6.5
on the adaptive learning-rate method
AdamW
, where such equivalence is explaned following
[
56
]
.
“Regularization is any modification we make to a learning algorithm that is intended to reduce its generalization error but not its training error”
[
78
]
, p. 117. Weight decay is only one among other forms of regularization, such as large learning rates, small batch sizes, and dropout,
[
200
]
.
The effects of the weight-decay parameter
\mathfrak{d}
in avoiding network model overfit is shown in Figure
67
.

Figure 67
:

Weight decay
(Section
0.6.3
). Effects of magnitude of weight-decay parameter
\mathfrak{d}
. Adapted from
[
78
]
, p. 116.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

It was written in
[
201
]
that:
“In the neural network community the two most common methods to avoid overfitting are early stopping and weight decay
[
175
]
[
175
]
. Early stopping has the advantage of being quick, since it shortens the training time, but the disadvantage of being poorly defined and not making full use of the available data. Weight decay, on the other hand, has the advantage of being well defined, but the disadvantage of being quite time consuming” (because of tuning). For examples of tuning the weight decay parameter
\mathfrak{d}
, which is of the order of
10^{-3}
, see
[
201
]

[
56
]
.

In the case of weight decay with cyclic annealing, both the step length
\epsilon_{k}
and the weight decay parameter
\mathfrak{d}
are scaled by the annealing multiplier
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
in the parameter update
[
56
]
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\mathfrak{a}_{k}(\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}-\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k})=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\mathfrak{a}_{k}(\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}+\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\ .

(195)

The effectiveness of SGD with weight decay, with and without cyclic annealing, is presented in Figure
74
.

Combining all add-on tricks

To have a general parameter-update equation that combines all of the above add-on improvement tricks, start with the parameter update with momentum and accelerated gradient Eq. (
154
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\gamma_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}))+\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\ ,

(
154
)

and add the weight-decay term
\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
from Eq. (
194
), then scale both the weight-decay term and the gradient-descent term
(-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})
by the cyclic annealing multiplier
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
in Eq. (
195
), leaving the momentum term
\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})
alone, to obtain:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\mathfrak{a}_{k}[\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\gamma_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1}))+\mathfrak{d}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}]+\zeta_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k-1})\ ,

(196)

which is included in Algorithm
4
.

0.6.4
Kaiming He initialization

All optimization algorithms discussed above share a crucial step, which crucially affects the convergence of training, especially as neural networks become ‘deep’:
The
initialization
of the network’s parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
is not only related to the speed of convergence, it ‘‘can determine whether the algorithm converges at all.’’
173
173

173

See
[
78
]
, p. 292.

Convergence problems are typically related to the scaling of initial parameters. Large parameters result in large activations, which leads to exploding values during forward and backward propagation, i.e., evaluation of the loss function and computation of its gradient with respect to the parameters.
Small parameters, on the other hand, may result in
vanishing gradients
, i.e., the loss function becomes insensitive to parameters, which causes the training process to stall.
To be precise, considerations regarding initialization are related to weight-matrices
\bm{W}^{(\ell)}
, see Section
0.4.4
on “Network layer, detailed construct”; bias vectors
\bm{b}^{(\ell)}
are usually initialized to zero, which is also assumed in the subsequent considerations.
174
174

174

Situations favoring a nonzero initialization of weights are explained in
[
78
]
, p. 297.

The
Kaiming He initialization
provides equally effective as simple means to overcome scaling issues observed when weights are randomly initialized using a normal distribution with fixed standard deviation.
The key idea of the authors
[
127
]
is to have the same variance of weights for each of the network’s layers.
As opposed to the
Xavier initialization
175
175

175

See
[
202
]
.

,
the nonlinearity of activation functions is accounted for.
Consider the
\ell
-th layer of a feedforward neural network, where the vector
\bm{z}^{(\ell)}
follows as affine function of inputs
\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}

\bm{z}^{(\ell)}=\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}+\bm{b}^{(\ell)},\qquad\bm{y}^{(\ell)}=a(\bm{z}^{(\ell)}),

(197)

where
\bm{W}^{(\ell)}
and
\bm{b}^{(\ell)}
denote the layer’s weight matrix and bias vector, respectively.
The output of the layer
\bm{y}^{(\ell)}
is given by element-wise application of the activation function
a
, see Sections
0.4.4
and
0.4.4
for a detailed presentation.
All components of the weight matrix
\bm{W}^{(\ell)}
are assumed to be independent of each other and to share the same probability distribution.
The same holds for the components of the input vector
\bm{y}^{(\ell-1)}
and the output vector
\bm{y}^{(\ell)}
.
Additionally, elements of
\bm{W}^{(\ell)}
and
\bm{y}^{(\ell)}
shall be mutually independent.
Further, it is assumed that
\bm{W}^{(\ell)}
and
\bm{y}^{(\ell)}
have zero mean (i.e., expectation, cf. Eq. (
80
)) and are symmetric around zero.
In this case, the variance of
\bm{z}^{(\ell)}\in\mathbb{R}^{m_{(\ell)}\times 1}
is given by

\displaystyle\Var z^{(\ell)}_{i}

\displaystyle=m_{(\ell)}\Var\left(W^{(\ell)}_{ij}y^{(\ell-1)}_{j}\right)

(198)

\displaystyle=m_{(\ell)}\left(\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}\Var y^{(\ell-1)}_{j}+\mathbb{E}(W^{(\ell)}_{ij})^{2}\Var y^{(\ell-1)}_{j}+\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}\mathbb{E}(y^{(\ell-1)}_{j})^{2}\right)

(199)

\displaystyle=m_{(\ell)}\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}\left(\Var y^{(\ell-1)}_{j}+\mathbb{E}(y^{(\ell-1)}_{j})^{2}\right),

(200)

where
m_{(\ell)}
denotes the width of the
\ell
-th layer and the fundamental relation
\Var(XY)=\Var X\Var Y+\mathbb{E}(X)^{2}\Var Y+\mathbb{E}(Y)^{2}\Var X
has been used along with the assumption of weights having a zero mean, i.e.,
\mathbb{E}(W^{(\ell)}_{ij})=0
.
The variance of some random variable
X
, which is the expectation of the squared deviation of
X
from its mean, i.e.,

\Var X=\mathbb{E}((X-\mathbb{E}(X))^{2}),

(201)

is a measure of the “dispersion” of
X
around its mean value.
The variance is the square of the standard deviation
\sigma
of the random variable
X
, or, conversely,

\sigma(X)=\sqrt{\Var X}=\sqrt{\mathbb{E}((X-\mathbb{E}(X))^{2}}.

(202)

As opposed to the variance, the standard deviation of some random variable
X
has the same physical dimension as
X
itself.

The elementary relation
\Var X^{2}=\mathbb{E}(X^{2})-\mathbb{E}(X)^{2}
gives

\Var z^{(\ell)}_{i}=m_{(\ell)}\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}\mathbb{E}((y^{(\ell-1)}_{j})^{2}),

(203)

Note that the mean of inputs does not vanish for activation functions that are not symmetric about zero as, e.g., the ReLU functions (see Section
0.5.3
).
For the ReLU activation function,
y=a(x)=max(0,x)
the mean value of the squared output and the variance of the input are related by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(y^{2})

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}y^{2}P(y)\mathrm{d}y

(204)

\displaystyle=\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\max(0,x)^{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x=\int_{0}^{\infty}x^{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{2}P(x)\mathrm{d}x

(205)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\Var x.

(206)

Substituting the above result in Eq. (
203
) provides the following relationship among the variances of the inputs to the activation function of two consecutive layers, i.e.,
\Var\bm{z}^{(\ell)}
and
\Var\bm{z}^{(\ell-1)}
, respectively:

\Var z^{(\ell)}_{i}=\frac{m_{(\ell)}}{2}\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}\Var z^{(\ell-1)}_{j}.

(207)

For a network with
L
layers, the following relation between the variance of inputs
\Var\bm{z}^{(1)}
and outputs
\Var\bm{z}^{(L)}
is obtained:

z^{(L)}_{i}=\Var z^{(1)}_{j}\prod_{\ell=2}^{L}\frac{m_{(\ell)}}{2}\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}.

(208)

To preserve the variance through all layers of the network, the following condition must be fulfilled regarding the variance of weight matrices:

\frac{m_{(\ell)}}{2}\Var W^{(\ell)}_{ij}=1\quad\forall\ell\qquad\leftrightarrow\qquad\bm{W}^{(\ell)}\sim\mathcal{N}\left(0,\frac{2}{m_{(\ell)}}\right),

(209)

where
\mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2})
denotes the normal (or Gaussian) distribution with zero mean and

\sigma^{2}=2/m_{(\ell)}
variance.
The above result, which is known as
Kaiming He initialization
, implies that the width of a layer
m_{(\ell)}
needs to be regarded in the initialization of weight matrices.
Preserving the variance of inputs mitigates exploding or vanishing gradients and improves convergence in particular for deep networks.
The authors of
[
127
]
provided analogous results for the parametric rectified linear unit (PReLU, see Section
0.5.3
).

0.6.5
Adaptive methods: Adam, variants, criticism

The Adam algorithm was introduced in
[
170
]
(version 1), and updated in 2017 (version 9), and has been “immensely successful in development of several state-of-the-art solutions for a wide range of problems,” as stated in
[
182
]
.
“In the area of neural networks, the ADAM-Optimizer is one of the most popular adaptive step size methods. It was invented in
[
170
]
. The 5865 citations in only three years shows additionally the importance of the given paper’’
176
176

176

Reference
[
170
]
introduced the Adam algorithm in 2014, received 34,535 citations on 2019.12.11, after 5 years, and a whopping 112,797 citations on 2022.07.11, after an additional period of more than 2.5 years later, according to Google Scholar.

[
203
]
[
203
]
.
The authors of

[
204
]
[
204
]
concurred: “Adam is widely used in both academia and industry. However, it is also one of the least well-understood algorithms. In recent years, some remarkable works provided us with better understanding of the algorithm, and proposed different variants of it.”

Unified adaptive learning-rate, momentum, weight decay, cyclic annealing
(for epoch
\tau
)

Data:

\bullet
Network parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
obtained from previous epoch
(\tau-1)

\bullet
Select a small stabilization parameter
\delta>0
, e.g.,
10^{-8}

\bullet
Select
t
as epoch
\tau
or global iteration
j
, Eq. (
161
), and budget
\tau_{max}
or
j_{max}

\bullet
Select learning-rate schedule
\epsilon(\text{t})\in\{

Eqs. (
160
)-(
164

\}
, and parameters
\epsilon_{0},k_{c},\varpi

\bullet
Select
\zeta
if use standard momentum, and
\gamma
if use Nesterov Accelerated Gradient, Eq. (
154
)

\bullet
Select a value for
m
the number of examples in the minibatches

Result:

Updated network parameters
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
for the next epoch
(\tau+1)
.

Blank line

1

\blacktriangleright
Begin training epoch
\tau

\blacktriangleright
Initialize
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\tau}^{\star}
(from previous training epoch) (line
4
in Algorithm
4
)

2

②

for

k=1,2,\ldots,k_{max}

do

3

4

Obtain random minibatch
\mathbb{B}_{k}^{|\text{m}|}
containing
m
examples, Eq. (

Unified adaptive learning-rate pseudocode

It was suggested in
[
182
]
a unified pseudocode, adapted in Algorithm
5
, that included not only the standard SGD in Algorithm
4
, but also a number of successful adaptive learning-rate methods:
AdaGrad
,
RMSProp
,
AdaDelta
,
Adam
, the recent
AMSGrad
,
AdamW
. Our adaptation in Algorithm
5
also includes Nostalgic Adam and AdamX.
177
177

177

In lines
5
-
5
of Algorithm
5
, use Eq. (
196
), but replacing scalar learning rate
\epsilon_{k}
with matrix learning rate
\bm{\epsilon}_{k}
in Eq. (
213
) to update parameters; the result includes Eq. (
214
) for vanilla adaptive methods and Eq. (
264
) for
AdamW
.

Four new quantities are introduced for iteration
k
in SGD:
(1)
\bm{m}_{k}
at SGD iteration
k
, as the first moment, and
(2) its correction
\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=\phi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})\ ,\quad\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}=\chi_{\phi_{k}}(\bm{m}_{k})\ ,

(210)

and (3) the second moment (variance)
178
178

178

The uppercase letter
\bm{V}
is used instead of the lowercase letter
\bm{v}
, which is usually reserved for “velocity” used in a term called “momentum”, which is added to the gradient term to correct the descent direction. Such algorithm is called gradient descent with momentum in deep-learning optimization literature; see, e.g.,
[
78
]
, Section 8.3.2 Momentum, p. 288.

\bm{V}_{k}
and (4) its correction
\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=\psi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})\ ,\quad\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\chi_{\psi_{k}}(\bm{V}_{k})\ .

(211)

The descent direction estimate
\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}
at SGD iteration
k
for each training epoch is

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=-\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}\ .

(212)

The adaptive learning rate
\epsilon_{k}
is obtained from rescaling the fixed learning-rate schedule
\epsilon(k)
, also called the “global” learning rate particularly when it is a constant, using the 2nd moment
\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}
as follows:

\displaystyle\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}+\delta}}\ ,\text{ or }\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\text{ (element-wise operations)}

(213)

where
\epsilon(k)
can be either Eq. (
160
)
179
179

179

Suggested in
[
78
]
, p. 287.

(which includes
\epsilon(k)=\epsilon_{0}=
constant) or Eq. (
162
);
180
180

180

Suggested in
[
182
]
, p. 3.

\delta
is a small number to avoid division by zero;
181
181

181

AdaDelta and RMSProp used the first form of Eq. (
213
), with
\delta
outside the square root, whereas AdaGrad and Adam used the second part, with
\delta
inside the square root. AMSGrad, Nostalgic Adam, AdamX did not use
\delta
, i.e., set
\delta=0
.

the operations (square root, addition, division) are element-wise, with both
\epsilon_{0}
and
\delta=O(10^{-6})\text{ to }O(10^{-8})
(depending on the algorithm) being constants.

Remark 0.6.11
.

A particular case is the AdaDelta algorithm, in which
\bm{m}_{k}
in Eq. (
210
) is the second moment for the network parameter increments
\{\Delta\bm{\theta}_{i},i=1,\ldots,k\}
, and
\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}
also in Eq. (
210
) the corrected gradient.

blah

\hfill\blacksquare

All of the above arrays—such as
\bm{m}_{k}
and
\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}
in Eq. (
210
),
\bm{V}_{k}
and
\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}
in Eq. (
211
), and
\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}
in Eq. (
212
)—together the resulting array
\bm{\epsilon}_{k}
in Eq. (
213
) has the same structure as the network parameter array
\bm{\theta}
in Eq. (
35
), with
P_{T}
in Eq. (
38
) being the total number of parameters. The update of the network parameter estimate in
\bm{\theta}
is written as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\odot\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\Delta\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ ,

(214)

where the Hadamard operator
\odot
(element-wise multiplication) is omitted to alleviate the notation.
182
182

182

There are no symbols similar to the Hadamard operator symbol
\odot
for other operations such as square root, addition, and division, as implied in Eq. (
213
), so there is no need to use the symbol
\odot
just for multiplication.

Remark 0.6.12
.

The element-wise operations in Eq. (
213
) and Eq. (
214
) would allow each parameter in array
\bm{\theta}
to have its own learning rate, unlike in traditional deterministic optimization algorithms, such as in Algorithm
2
or even in the Standard SGD Algorithm
4
, where the same learning rate is applied to all parameters in
\bm{\theta}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

It remains to define the functions
\phi_{t}
and
\chi_{\phi_{t}}
in Eq. (

), and
\psi_{t}
and
\chi_{\psi_{t}}
in Eq. (

) for each of the particular algorithms covered by the unified Algorithm

.

SGD.

To obtain Algorithm
4
as a particular case, select the following functions for Algorithm
5
:

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\chi_{\phi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ (Identity)}\Rightarrow\bm{m}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}=\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}\ ,

(215)

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})=\chi_{\psi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ and }\delta=0\Rightarrow\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\bm{I}\text{ and }\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\epsilon(k)\bm{I}\ ,

(216)

together with learning-rate schedule
\epsilon(k)
presented in Section
0.6.3
on step-length decay and annealing. In other words, from Eqs. (

)-(
214
), the parameter update reduces to that of the vanilla SGD with the fixed learning-rate schedule
\epsilon(k)
, without scaling:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ .

(217)

Similarly for SGD with momentum and accelerated gradient (Section

), step-length decay and cyclic annealing (Section
0.6.3
), weight decay (Section

).

AdaGrad: Adaptive Gradient

Starting the line of research on adaptive learning-rate algorithms,
the authors of

[
52
]
[
52
]
183
183

183

As of 2019.11.28,
[
52
]
was cited 5,385 times on Google Scholars, and 1,615 times on Web of Science.
By 2022.07.11,
[
52
]
was cited 10,431 times on Google Scholars, and 3,871 times on Web of Science.

selected the following functions for Algorithm
5
:

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\chi_{\phi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ (Identity)}\Rightarrow\bm{m}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}=\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}\ ,

(218)

\displaystyle\psi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})=\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}\odot\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i})^{2}\text{ (element-wise square)}\ ,

(219)

\displaystyle\chi_{\psi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ and }\delta=10^{-7}\Rightarrow\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\sum_{i=1}^{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i})^{2}\text{ and }\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ ,

(220)

leading to an update with adaptive scaling of the learning rate

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ ,

(221)

in which each parameter in
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
is updated with its own learning rate. For a given network parameter, say,
\theta_{pq}
, its learning rate
\epsilon_{k,pq}
is essentially
\epsilon(k)
scaled with the inverse of the square root of the sum of all historical values of the corresponding gradient component
(pq)
, i.e.,
(\delta+\sum_{i=1}^{k}\widetilde{g}_{i,pq}^{2})^{-1/2}
, with
\delta=10^{-7}
being very small. A consequence of such scaling is that a larger gradient component would have a smaller learning rate and a smaller per-iteration decrease in the learning rate, whereas a smaller gradient component would have a larger learning rate and a higher per-iteration decrease in the learning rate, even though the relative decrease is about the same.
184
184

184

See
[
54
]
. For example, compare the sequence
\{\frac{1}{5},\frac{1}{5+5}\}
to the sequence
\{\frac{1}{2},\frac{1}{2+2}\}
.
The authors of

[
78
]
, p. 299, mistakenly stated “The parameters with the largest partial derivative of the loss have a correspondingly rapid decrease in their learning rate, while parameters with small partial derivatives have a relatively small decrease in their learning rate.”

Thus progress along different directions with large difference in gradient amplitudes is evened out as the number of iterations increases.
185
185

185

It was stated in

[
54
]
: “progress along each dimension evens
out over time. This is very beneficial for training deep neural networks since the scale of the gradients in each layer is
often different by several orders of magnitude, so the optimal
Learning rate should take that into account.” Such observation made more sense than saying “The net effect is greater progress in the more gently sloped directions of parameter space” as did
the authors of

[
78
]
, p. 299, who referred to AdaDelta in Section 8.5.4, p. 302, through the work of other authors, but might not read
[
54
]
.

shows the convergence of some adaptive learning-rate algorithms:

,

,

,
Adam
.

Forecasting time series, exponential smoothing

At this point, all of the subsequent adaptive learning-rate algorithms made use of an important technique in forecasting known as exponential smoothing of time series, without using this terminology, but instead referred to such technique as
“exponential decaying average”
[
54
]
,
[
78
]
, p. 300,
“exponentially decaying average”
[
80
]
,
“exponential moving average”
[
170
]
,
[
182
]
,
[
205
]
[
205
]
,
[
162
]
,
“exponential weight decay”
[
56
]
.

Figure 69
:

Dow Jones Industrial Average
(DJIA, Section
0.6.5
) stock index year-to-date (YTD) chart as from 2019.01.01 to 2019.11.30, Google Finance.

“Exponential smoothing methods have been around since the 1950s, and are still the most popular forecasting methods used in business and industry” such as “minute-by-minute stock prices, hourly temperatures at a weather station, daily numbers of arrivals at a medical clinic, weekly sales of a product, monthly unemployment figures for a region, quarterly imports of a country, and annual turnover of a company”
[
206
]
[
206
]
. See Figure
69
for the chart of a stock index showing noise.

“Exponential smoothing was proposed in the late 1950s (Brown, 1959; Holt, 1957; Winters, 1960), and has motivated some of the most successful forecasting methods. Forecasts produced using exponential smoothing methods are weighted averages of past observations, with the weights decaying exponentially as the observations get older. In other words, the more recent the observation the higher the associated weight. This framework generates reliable forecasts quickly and for a wide range of time series, which is a great advantage and of major importance to applications in industry”
[
207
]
, Chap. 7, “Exponential smoothing”. See Figure
70
for an example of “exponential-smoothing” curve that is not “smooth”.

Figure 70
:

Saudi Arabia oil production during 1996-2013
(Section
0.6.5
). Piecewise linear data (black) and fitted curve (red), despite the name “smoothing”. From
[
207
]
, Chap. 7.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

For neural networks, early use of exponential smoothing dates back at least to 1998 in
[
165
]
and
[
166
]
.
186
186

186

We thank Lawrence Aitchison for informing us about these references; see also
[
168
]
.

For adaptive learning-rate algorithms further below (
RMSProp
,
AdaDelta
,
Adam
, etc.), let
\{x(t),\,t=1,2,\ldots\}
be a noisy raw-data time series as in Figure
69
, and
\{s(t),\,t=1,2,\ldots\}
its smoothed-out counterpart. The following recurrence relation is an exponential smoothing used to predict
s(t+1)
based on the known value
s(t)
and the data
x(t+1)
:

\displaystyle s(t+1)=\beta s(t)+(1-\beta)x(t+1)\ ,\text{ with }\beta\in[0,1)

(222)

Eq. (
222
) is a convex combination between
s(t)
and
x(t+1)
. a value of
\beta
closer to 1, e.g.,
\beta=0.9
and
1-\beta=0.1
, would weigh the smoothed-out past data
s(t)
more than the future raw data point
x(t+1)
. From Eq. (
222
), we have

\displaystyle s(1)=\beta s(0)+(1-\beta)x(1)

(223)

\displaystyle s(2)=\beta^{2}s(0)+(1-\beta)[\beta x(1)+x(2)]

(224)

\displaystyle s(t)=\beta^{t}s(0)+(1-\beta)\sum_{i=1}^{t}\beta^{t-i}x(i)\ ,

(225)

where the first term in Eq. (
225
) is called the bias, which is set by the initial condition:

\displaystyle s(0)=x(0)\ ,\text{ and as }t\rightarrow\infty\Rightarrow\beta^{t}x(0)\rightarrow 0\ .

(226)

For finite time
t
, if the series started with
s(0)=0\neq x(0)
, then there is a need to correct the estimate for the non-zero bias
x(0)\neq 0
(see Eq. (
240
) and Eq. (
242
) in the Adam algorithm below).
The coefficients in the series in Eq. (
225
) are exponential functions, and thus the name “exponential smoothing”.

Eq. (
225
) is the discrete counterpart of the linear part of Volterra series in Eq. (
512
), used widely in neuroscientific modeling; see Remark
0.13.2
. See also the “small heavy sphere” method or SGD with momentum Eq. (
158
).

It should be noted, however, that for
forecasting
(e.g.,
[
207
]
), the following recursive equation, slightly different from Eq. (
222
), is used instead:

\displaystyle s(t+1)=\beta s(t)+(1-\beta){\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}x(t)}\ ,\text{ with }\beta\in[0,1)\ ,

(227)

where
x(t)
(shown in purple) is used instead of
x(t+1)
, since if the data at
(t+1)
were already known, there would be no need to forecast.

RMSProp: Root Mean Square Propagation

Since
“
AdaGrad
shrinks the learning rate according to the entire history of the squared gradient and may have made the learning rate too small before arriving at such a convex structure’’,
187
187

187

See
[
78
]
, p. 300.

The authors of

[
53
]
188
188

188

Almost all authors, e.g.,
[
80
]

[
55
]

[
162
]
, attributed RMSProp to
Ref.
[
53
]
, except for Ref.
[
78
]
,
where only
Hinton’s 2012 Coursera lecture was referred to. Tieleman was Hinton’s student; see the video and the lecture notes in
[
53
]
, where Tieleman’s contribution was noted as unpublished.
The authors of

[
162
]
indicated that both RMSProp and AdaDelta (next section) were developed independently at about the same time to fix problems in AdaGrad.

fixed the problem of continuing decay of the learning rate by introducing RMSProp
189
189

189

Neither
[
162
]
, nor
[
80
]
, nor
[
78
]
, p. 299, provided the meaning of the acronym RMSProp, which stands for “Root Mean Square Propagation”.

with the following functions for Algorithm
5
:

\displaystyle\phi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\chi_{\phi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ (Identity)}\Rightarrow\bm{m}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}=\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}\ ,

(228)

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=\beta\bm{V}_{k-1}+(1-\beta)(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})^{2}\text{ with }\beta\in[0,1)\text{ (element-wise square)}\ ,

(229)

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=(1-\beta)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}\,(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i})^{2}=\psi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})\ ,

(230)

\displaystyle\chi_{\psi_{k}}=\bm{I}\text{ and }\delta=10^{-6}\Rightarrow\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\bm{V}_{k}\text{ and }\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}+\delta}}\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ ,

(231)

where the running average of the squared gradients is given in Eq. (
229
) for efficient coding, and in Eq. (
230
) in fully expanded form as a series with exponential coefficients
\beta^{k-i}
, for
i=1,\ldots,k
.
Eq. (
229
) is the exact counterpart of exponential smoothing recurrence relation in Eq. (
222
), and Eq. (
230
) has its counterpart in Eq. (
225
) if
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{0}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{0}^{2}=0
; see Section
0.6.5
on forecasting time series and exponential smoothing.

Figure
68
shows the convergence of some adaptive learning-rate algorithms:
AdaGrad
,
RMSProp
,

SGDNesterov
,
AdaDelta
,
Adam
.

RMSProp
still depends on a global learning rate
\epsilon(k)=\epsilon_{0}
constant, a tuning hyperparameter. Even though
RMSProp
was one of the go-to algorithms for machine learning, the pitfalls of
RMSProp
, along with other adaptive learning-rate algorithms, were revealed in
[
55
]
.

AdaDelta: Adaptive Delta (parameter increment)

The name “AdaDelta” comes from the adaptive parameter increment
\Delta\bm{\theta}
in Eq. (
236
).
In parallel and independently,
AdaDelta
proposed in
[
54
]

not only fixed the problem of continuing decaying learning rate of
AdaGrad
, but also removed the need for a global learning rate
\epsilon_{0}
, which
RMSProp
still used.
By accumulating the squares of the parameter increments, i.e.,
(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k})^{2}
, AdaDelta would fit in the unified framework of Algorithm
5
if the symbol
\bm{m}_{k}
in Eq. (
210
) were interpreted as the accumulated 2nd moment
(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k})^{2}
, per Remark
0.6.11
.

The weaknesses of
AdaGrad
was observed in
[
54
]
: “Since the magnitudes of gradients are factored out in
AdaGrad
, this method can be sensitive to initial conditions of the parameters and the corresponding gradients. If the initial gradients are large, the learning rates will be low for the remainder of training. This can be combatted by increasing the global learning rate, making the
AdaGrad
method sensitive to the choice of learning rate. Also, due to the continual accumulation of squared gradients in the denominator, the learning rate will continue to decrease throughout training, eventually decreasing to zero and stopping training completely.”

AdaDelta was then introduced in
[
54
]
as an improvement over AdaGrad with two goals in mind: (1) to avoid the continuing decay of the learning rate, and (2) to avoid having to specify
\epsilon(k)
, called the “global learning rate”, as a constant. Instead of summing past squared gradients over a finite-size window, which is not efficient in coding,
exponential smoothing was employed in
[
54
]
for both the squared gradients
(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})^{2}
and for the squared increments
(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k})^{2}
, with the increment used in the update
\bm{\theta}_{k+1}=\bm{\theta}_{k}+\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k}
, by choosing the following functions for Algorithm
5
:

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=\beta\bm{V}_{k-1}+(1-\beta)(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})^{2}\text{ with }\beta\in[0,1)\text{ (element-wise square)}

(232)

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=(1-\beta)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}\,(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i})^{2}=\psi_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})\ ,

(233)

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=\beta\bm{m}_{k-1}+(1-\beta)(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k})^{2}\text{ (element-wise square)}

(234)

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=(1-\beta)\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}\,(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{i})^{2}\ .

(235)

Thus, exponential smoothing (Section
0.6.5
) is used for two second-moment series:
\{(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i})^{2},\,i=1,2,\ldots\}
and
\{(\Delta\bm{\theta}_{i})^{2},\,i=1,2,\ldots\}
.
The update of the network parameters from
\bm{\theta}_{k}
to
\bm{\theta}_{k+1}
is carried out as follows:

\displaystyle\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k}=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\,\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}=[\sqrt{\bm{m}_{k-1}}+\delta]\,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\text{ and }\bm{\theta}_{k+1}=\bm{\theta}_{k}+\Delta\bm{\theta}_{k}\ ,

(236)

where
\epsilon(k)
in Eq. (
231
) is fixed to 1 in Eq. (
236
), eliminating the hyperparameter
\epsilon(k)
. Another nice feature of AdaDelta is the consistency of units (physical dimensions), in the sense that the fraction factor of the gradient
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}
in Eq. (
236
) has the unit of step length (learning rate):
190
190

190

In spite of the nice features in
AdaDelta
, neither
[
78
]
, nor
[
80
]
, nor
[
162
]
,
had a review of

AdaDelta
, except for citing
[
54
]
, even though
the authors of

[
78
]
, p. 302, wrote: “While the results suggest that the family of algorithms with adaptive learning rates (represented by
RMSProp
and
AdaDelta
) performed fairly robustly, no single best algorithm has emerged,” and “Currently, the most popular optimization algorithms actively in use include
SGD
,
SGD with momentum
,
RMSProp
,
RMSProp
with momentum,
AdaDelta
, and Adam.”
The authors of

[
80
]
, p. 286, did not follow the historical development, briefly reviewed
RMSProp
, then cited in passing references for
AdaDelta
and
Adam
, and then mentioned the “popular
AdaGrad
algorithm” as a “member of this family”; readers would lose sight of the gradual progress made starting from
AdaGrad
, to
RMSProp
,
AdaDelta
, then
Adam
, and to the recent
AdamW
in
[
56
]
, among others, alternating with pitfalls revealed and subsequent fixes, and then more pitfalls revealed and more fixes.

\displaystyle\left[\frac{\sqrt{\bm{m}_{k-1}}+\delta}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\right]=\left[\frac{\Delta\bm{\theta}}{\widetilde{\bm{g}}}\right]=[\epsilon]\ ,

(237)

where the enclosing square brackets denote units (physical dimensions), but that was not the case in Eq. (
231
) of RMSProp:

\displaystyle[\bm{\epsilon}_{k}]=\left[\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}+\delta}}\right]\neq[\epsilon]\ .

(238)

shows the convergence of some adaptive learning-rate algorithms:

Adam: Adaptive moments

Both both 1st moment Eq. (
239
) and 2nd moment Eq. (
241
) are adaptive.
To avoid possible large step sizes and non-convergence of
RMSProp
,
the following functions were selected for Algorithm
5

[
170
]
:

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=\beta_{1}\bm{m}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{1})\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\beta_{1}\in[0,1)\text{ and }\bm{m}_{0}=0\ ,

(239)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}=\frac{1}{1-(\beta_{1})^{k}}\,\bm{m}_{k}\text{ (bias correction)}\ ,

(240)

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=\beta_{2}\bm{V}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{2})(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})^{2}\text{ (element-wise square)}\ ,\text{ with }\beta_{2}\in[0,1)\text{ and }\bm{V}_{0}=0\ ,

(241)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\frac{1}{1-(\beta_{2})^{k}}\,\bm{V}_{k}\text{ (bias correction)}\ ,

(242)

\displaystyle\bm{\epsilon}_{k}=\frac{\epsilon(k)}{\sqrt{\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}}+\delta}\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ .

(243)

with the following recommended values of the parameters:

\displaystyle\beta_{1}=0.9\ ,\beta_{2}=0.999\ ,\epsilon_{0}=0.001\ ,\delta=10^{-8}\ .

(244)

Remark 0.6.13
.

RMSProp
is a particular case of Adam, when
\beta_{1}=0
, together with the absence of the bias-corrected 1st moment Eq. (
240
) and bias-corrected 2nd moment Eq. (
242
). Moreover, the get
RMSProp
from Adam, choose the constant
\delta
and the learning rate
\bm{\epsilon}_{k}
as in Eq. (
231
), instead of Eq. (
243
) above, but this choice is a minor point, since either choice should be fine. On the other hand,
for deep-learning applications, having the 1st moment (or momentum), and thus requiring
\beta>0
, would be useful to “significantly boost the performance”
[
182
]
, and hence an advantage of Adam over
RMSProp
.

\hfill\blacksquare

It follows from Eq. (
225
) in Section
0.6.5
on exponential smoothing of time series that the recurrence relation for gradients (1st moment) in Eq. (
239
) leads to the following series:

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=(1-\beta_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}\,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}\ ,

(245)

since
\bm{m}_{0}=0
. Taking the expectation, as defined in Eq. (
80
), on both sides of Eq. (
245
) yields

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\bm{m}_{k}]

\displaystyle=(1-\beta_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}\,\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}]\cdot(1-\beta_{1})\sum_{i=1}^{k}\beta^{k-i}+\text{\sc d}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}]\cdot[1-(\beta_{1})^{2}]+\text{\sc d}\ ,

(246)

where
d
is the drift from the expected value, with
\text{\sc d}=0
for stationary random processes.
191
191

191

A random process is stationary when its mean and standard deviation stay constant over time.

For non-stationary processes,
it was suggested in
[
170
]
to keep

d
small by choosing small
\beta_{1}
so only past gradients close to the present iteration
k
would contribute, so to keep any change in the mean and standard deviation in subsequent iterations small.
By dividing both sides by
[1-(\beta_{1})^{2}]
, the bias-corrected 1st moment
\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}
shown in Eq. (
240
) is obtained, showing that the expected value of
\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}
is the same as the expected value of the gradient
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}
plus a small number, which could be zero for stationary processes:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}]=\mathbb{E}\left[\frac{\bm{m}_{k}}{1-(\beta_{1})^{2}}\right]=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{i}]+\frac{\text{\sc d}}{1-(\beta_{1})^{2}}\ .

(247)

The argument to obtain the bias-corrected 2nd moment
\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}
in Eq. (
242
) is of course the same.

The authors of
[
170
]
pointed out the lack of bias correction in
RMSProp
(Remark
0.6.13
), leading to “very large step sizes and often divergence”, and provided numerical experiment results to support their point.

Figure
68
shows the convergence of some adaptive learning-rate algorithms:
AdaGrad
,
RMSProp
,

SGDNesterov
,
AdaDelta
,
Adam
. Their results show the superior performance of
Adam
compared to other adaptive learning-rate algorithms.
See Figure
151
in Section
0.14.7
on “Lack of transparency and irreproducibility of results” in recent deep-learning papers.

AMSGrad: Adaptive Moment Smoothed Gradient

The authors of
[
182
]
stated that
Adam
(and other variants such as
RMSProp
,
AdaDelta
, Nadam) “failed to converge to an optimal solution (or a critical point in non-convex settings)” in many applications with large output spaces, and constructed a simple convex optimization for which
Adam
did not converge to the optimal solution.

An earlier version of
[
182
]
received one of the three Best Papers at the ICLR 2018
192
192

192

Sixth International Conference on Learning Representations (
Website
).

conference, in which it was suggested to fix the problem by endowing the mentioned algorithms with “long-term memory” of past gradients, and by selecting the following functions for Algorithm
5
:

\displaystyle\bm{m}_{k}=\beta_{1k}\bm{m}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{1k})\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}=\phi_{k}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{m}_{0}=0\ ,

(248)

\displaystyle\beta_{1k}=\beta_{11}\in[0,1)\ ,\text{ or }\beta_{1k}=\beta_{11}\lambda^{k-1}\text{ with }\lambda\in(0,1)\ ,\text{ or }\beta_{1k}=\frac{\beta_{11}}{k}\ ,

(249)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{m}}_{k}=\bm{m}_{k}\text{ (no bias correction)}\ ,

(250)

\displaystyle\bm{V}_{k}=\beta_{2}\bm{V}_{k-1}+(1-\beta_{2})(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k})^{2}=\psi_{k}\text{ (element-wise square)}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{V}_{0}=0\ ,

(251)

\displaystyle\beta_{2}\in[0,1)\text{ and }\frac{\beta_{11}}{\sqrt{\beta_{2}}}<1\Leftrightarrow\beta_{2}>(\beta_{1})^{2}\ ,

(252)

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k}=\max(\widehat{\bm{V}}_{k-1},\bm{V}_{k})\text{ (element-wise max, ``long-term memory'')}\ ,\text{ and }\delta\text{ not used}\ ,

(253)

The parameter
\lambda
was not defined in

.
For the step-length (or size) schedule

, even though

First-time learners in this field could be overwhelmed by complex-looking equations in this kind of paper, so it would be helpful to elucidate some key results that led to the above expressions, particularly for

It was

The second choice

, and is written in our notation as:
194
194

194

To write this term in the notation used in

\displaystyle\frac{D_{\infty}^{2}}{(1-\beta_{1})^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{T}}\frac{\beta_{1k}\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}}{\epsilon(k)}\ ,

(254)

where

is the maximum number of iterations in the ②
for
loop in Algorithm

, and

is the total number of network parameters defined in Eq. (
38

The factor
D_{\infty}^{2}/(1-\beta_{1})^{2}
is a constant, and the following bound on the component
\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}
is a consequence of an assumption in Theorem 4 in
[
182
]
:

\displaystyle\parallel\nabla J_{k}\parallel_{\infty}\leq G_{\infty}\text{ for all }k\in\{1,\ldots,k_{max}\}\Rightarrow\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}\leq G_{\infty}\text{ for any }i\in\{1,\ldots,P_{T}\}\ ,

(255)

where
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{\infty}
is the infinity (max) norm, which is clearly consistent with the use of element-wise maximum components for “long-term memory” in Eq. (
253
). Intuitively,
\widehat{V}_{k,i}
has the unit of gradient squared, and thus
\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}
has the unit of gradient. Since
G_{\infty}
is the upperbound of the maximum component of the gradient
195
195

195

The notation “
G
” is clearly mnemonic for “gradient”, and the uppercase is used to designate upperbound.

\nabla J_{k}
of the cost function
J_{k}
at any iteration
k
, it follows that
\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}\leq G_{\infty}
.
We refer to
[
183
]
, p. 10, Lemma 4.2, for a formal proof of the inequality in Eq. (
255
). Once
\widehat{V}_{k,i}^{1/2}
is replaced by
G_{\infty}^{2}
, which is then pulled out as a common factor in expression (
254
), where upon substituting

\beta_{11}=\beta_{1}

\displaystyle(\ref{eq:reddi-2nd-term-theorem-4})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D_{\infty}^{2}G_{\infty}}{(1-\beta_{1})^{2}}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}\sum_{i=1}^{P_{T}}\frac{\beta_{1}\sqrt{k}\lambda^{k-1}}{\epsilon_{0}}=\frac{D_{\infty}^{2}G_{\infty}\beta_{1}P_{T}}{(1-\beta_{1})^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}\sqrt{k}\lambda^{k-1}

(256)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{D_{\infty}^{2}G_{\infty}\beta_{1}P_{T}}{(1-\beta_{1})^{2}\epsilon_{0}}\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}k\lambda^{k-1}=\frac{D_{\infty}^{2}G_{\infty}{\color[rgb]{0,0,1}\beta_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}P_{T}}}{(1-\beta_{1})^{2}{\color[rgb]{0.75,0,0.25}\epsilon_{0}}}\frac{1}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}\ ,

(257)

where the following series expansion had been used:

See also

\displaystyle\frac{1}{(1-\lambda)^{2}}=\sum_{k=1}^{k_{max}}k\lambda^{k-1}\ .

(258)

Figure 71

The first two figures on the left were the results of using logistic regression (network with one layer with logistic sigmoid activation function), whereas the figure on the right is by using a neural network with three layers (input layer, hidden layer, output layer). The cost function decreased faster for AMSGrad compared to that of Adam. For logistic regression, the difference between the two cost values also decreased with the iteration number, and became very small at iteration 5000. For the three-layer neural network, the cost difference between AMSGrad and Adam stayed more or less constant, as the cost went down to more than one tenth of the initial cost at about 0.3, and after 5000 iterations, the AMSGrad cost (
\approx 0.01

\approx 0.02

AdamW: Adaptive moment with weight decay

The authors of

[
56
]
, aware of the work
in

[
55
]
, wrote:
It was suggested in

[
55
]

“that adaptive gradient methods do not generalize as well as SGD with momentum when tested on a
diverse set of deep learning tasks, such as image classification, character-level language modeling and constituency parsing.” In particular,
it was shown in
[
56
]

that “ a major factor of the poor generalization of the most popular adaptive gradient method,
Adam
, is due to the fact that
L_{2}
regularization is not nearly as effective for it as for SGD,” and proposed to move the weight decay from the gradient (
L_{2}
regularization) to the parameter update (original weight decay regularization). So what is “
L_{2}
regularization” and what is “weight decay” ? (See also Section
0.6.3
on weight decay.)

Figure 75
:

Cosine annealing
(Sections
0.6.3
,
0.6.5
). Annealing factor
\mathfrak{a}_{k}
as a function of epoch number. Four annealing cycles
p=1,\ldots,4
, with the following schedule for
T_{p}
in Eq. (
167
):
(1) Cycle 1,
T_{1}=100
epochs, epoch 0 to epoch 100,
(2) Cycle 2,
T_{2}=200
epochs, epoch 101 to epoch 300,
(3) Cycle 3,
T_{3}=400
epochs, epoch 301 to epoch 700,
(4) Cycle 4,
T_{4}=800
epochs, epoch 701 to epoch 1500.
From
[
56
]
. See Figure
74
in which the curves for AdamWR and SGDWR ended at epoch 1500.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Briefly, “
L_{2}
regularization’’ is aiming at decreasing the overall weights to avoid overfitting, which simply means that the network model tries to fit through as many data points as possible, including noise.
202
202

202

See
[
78
]
, p. 107, Section 5.2 on “Capacity, overfitting and underfitting”, and p. 115 provides a good explanation and motivation for regularization, as in Gupta 2017, ‘Deep Learning: Overfitting’, 2017.02.12,
Original website
,
Internet archive
.

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}_{reg}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}})=\widetilde{J}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}})+\frac{\mathfrak{d}}{2}(\parallel\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}\parallel_{2})^{2}

(261)

The magnitude of the coefficient
\mathfrak{d}
regulates (or regularizes) the behavior of the network:
\mathfrak{d}=0
would lead to overfitting (Figure
72
right), a moderate
\mathfrak{d}
may yield appropriate fitting (Figure
72
middle), a large
\mathfrak{d}
may lead to underfitting (Figure
72
left). The gradient of the regularized cost
\widetilde{J}_{reg}
is then

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{reg}:=\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}_{reg}}{\partial\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}}=\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}}{\partial\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}}+\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}+\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}

(262)

and the update becomes:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}(\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}+\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k})=(1-\epsilon_{k}\mathfrak{d})\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}-\epsilon_{k}\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ ,

(263)

which is equivalent to decaying the parameters (including the weights in)
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
when
(1-\epsilon_{k}\mathfrak{d})\in(0,1)
, but with varying decay parameter
\mathfrak{d}_{k}=\epsilon_{k}\mathfrak{d}\in(0,1)
depending on the step length
\epsilon_{k}
, which itself would decrease toward zero.

The same equivalence between
L_{2}
regularization Eq. (
261
) and weight decay Eq. (
263
), which is linear with respect to the gradient, cannot be said for adaptive methods due to the nonlinearity with respect to the gradient in the update procedure using Eq. (
213
) and Eq. (
214
). See also the parameter update in lines
5
-
5
of the unified pseudocode for adaptive methods in Algorithm
5
and Footnote
177
. Thus,
it was proposed in
[
56
]

to explicitly add weight decay to the parameter update Eq. (
214
) for AdamW (lines
5
-
5
in Algorithm
5
) as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}=\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\mathfrak{a}_{k}(\bm{\epsilon}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}-\mathfrak{d}\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k})\text{ (element-wise operations)}\ ,

(264)

where the parameter
\mathfrak{a}_{k}\in[0,1]
is an annealing multiplier defined in Section
0.6.3
on weight decay:

\displaystyle\mathfrak{a}_{k}=0.5+0.5\cos(\pi T_{cur}/T_{p})\in[0,1]\ ,\text{ with }T_{cur}:=j-\sum_{q=1}^{q=p-1}T_{q}\ ,

(
167
)

The
results of
the
numerical experiments on the CIFAR-10 dataset
using
Adam
, AdamW, SGDW (Weight decay), AdamWR (Warm Restart), and SGDWR
were reported in Figure
74

[
56
]
.

Remark 0.6.14
.

Limitation of cyclic annealing.

The 5th cycle of annealing not shown in Figure
74
would end at epoch
1500+1600=3100
, which is well beyond epoch budget of
1800
. In view of the diminishing return in the decrease of the training error at the end of each cycle, in addition to an increase in the test error by the end of the 4th cycle, as shown in Figure
74
, it is unlikely that it is worthwhile to start the 5th cycle, since not only the computation would be more expensive, due to the warm restart, the increase in the test error indicated that the end of the 3rd cycle was optimal, and thus the reason for
[
56
]
to stop at the end of the 4th cycle.

\hfill\blacksquare

If
AMSGrad
generated “a lot of noise for nothing” compared to
Adam
and
AdamW
, according to
[
209
]
, then does “marginal value” mean that adaptive methods in general generated a lot of noise for not much, compared to
SGD
?

The work
in

[
55
]
,
[
168
]
, and
[

]
proved once more that a classic like SGD introduced
by Robbins & Monro (1951b)

[
49
]
never dies, and would be motivation to generalize classical deterministic first and second-order optimization methods together with line search methods to add stochasticity. We will review in detail two papers along this line:
[

and
[
