0.6.6
SGD with Armijo line search and adaptive minibatch

In parallel to the deterministic choice of step length based on Armijo’s rule in Eq. (
138
) and Eq. (
139
), we have the following respective stochastic version proposed by
[
144
]
:
203
203

203

It is not until Section 4.7 in
[
144
]
that this version is presented for the general descent of nonconvex case, whereas the pseudocode in their Algorithm 1 at the beginning of their paper, and referred to in Section 4.5, was restricted to steepest descent for convex case.

\displaystyle\epsilon(\bm{\theta})=\min_{j}\{\beta^{j}\,\rho\,|\,\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta}+\beta^{j}\,\widetilde{\bm{d}})-\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})\leq\alpha{\beta^{j}}\rho\,\widetilde{\bm{g}}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\widetilde{\bm{d}}\}\ ,

(265)

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta}+\epsilon\widetilde{\bm{d}})\leq\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})+\alpha\epsilon\,\widetilde{\bm{g}}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\widetilde{\bm{d}}\ ,

(266)

where the overhead tilde of a quantity designates an estimate of that quantity based on a randomly selected minibatch, i.e.,
\widetilde{J}
is the cost estimate,
\widetilde{\bm{d}}
the descent-direction estimate, and
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
the gradient estimate, similar to those in Algorithm
4
.

There is a difference though: The standard SGD in Algorithm
4
uses a fixed minibatch for the computation of the cost estimate and the gradient estimate, whereas Algorithm
6

in

[
144
]
uses adaptive subprocedure to adjust the size of the minibatches to achieve a desired (fixed) probability
p_{J}
that the cost estimate is close to the true cost, and a desired probability
p_{g}
that the gradient estimate is close to the true gradient. These adaptive-minibatch subprocedures are also functions of the learning rate (step length)
\epsilon
, conceptually written as:

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}=\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta},\epsilon,p_{J})\text{ and }\widetilde{\bm{g}}=\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\bm{\theta},\epsilon,p_{g})\ ,

(267)

which are the counterparts to the fixed-minibatch procedures in Eq. (
152
) and Eq. (
153
), respectively.

Remark 0.6.15
.

Since the appropriate size of the minibatch depends on the gradient estimate, which is not known and which is computed based on the minibatch itself, the adaptive-minibatch subprocedures for cost estimate
\widetilde{J}
and for gradient estimate
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
in Eq. (
267
) contain a loop, started by guessing the gradient estimate, to gradually increase the size of the minibatch by adding more samples until certain criteria are met.
204
204

204

See
[
144
]
, p. 7, below Eq. (2.4).

In addition, since both the cost estimate
\widetilde{J}
and the gradient estimate
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
depend on the step size
\epsilon
, the Armijo line-search loop to determined the step length
\epsilon
—denoted as the ② for loop in the deterministic Algorithm
2
—is combined with the iteration loop
k
in Algorithm
6
, where these two combined loops are denoted as the ② ③
for
loop.

\hfill\blacksquare

The same relationship between
\widetilde{\bm{d}}
and
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
as in Eq. (
134
) holds:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\widetilde{\bm{d}}=\sum_{i}\sum_{j}\widetilde{g}_{ij}\widetilde{d}_{ij}<0\ .

(268)

For SGD, the descent-direction estimate
\widetilde{\bm{d}}
is identified with the steepest-descent direction estimate
(-\widetilde{\bm{g}})
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{d}}=-\widetilde{\bm{g}}\ ,

(269)

For Newton-type algorithms, such as in
[
145
]

[
146
]
, the descent direction estimate
\widetilde{\bm{d}}
is set to equal to the Hessian estimate
\widetilde{\bm{H}}
multiplied by the steepest-descent direction estimate
(-\widetilde{\bm{g}})
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{d}}=\widetilde{\bm{H}}\cdot(-\widetilde{\bm{g}})\ ,

(270)

Remark 0.6.16
.

In the SGD with Armijo line search and adaptive minibatch Algorithm
6
, the reliability parameter
\delta_{k}
and its use is another difference between Algorithm
6
and Algorithm
2
, the deterministic gradient descent with Armijo line search, and similarly for Algorithm
4
, the standard SGD.
The
reason was provided in
[
144
]
: Even when the probability of gradient estimate and cost estimate is near 1, it is not guaranteed that the expected value of the cost at the next iterate
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k+1}
would be below the cost at the current iterate
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
, due to arbitrary increase of the cost.
“Since random gradient may not be representative of the true gradient the function estimate accuracy and thus the expected improvement needs to be controlled by a different quantity,”
\delta_{k}^{2}
.

\hfill\blacksquare

The authors of

[
144
]
provided a rigorous convergence analysis of their proposed Algorithm
6
, but had not implemented their method, and thus had no numerical results at the time of this writing.
205
205

205

Based on our private communications with the authors of
[
144
]
on 2019.11.16.

Without empirical evidence that the algorithm works and is competitive compared to SGD (see adaptive methods and their criticism in Section
0.6.5
), there would be no adoption.

Figure 78
:

Stochastic Newton with Armijo-like 2nd order line search
(Section
0.6.7
). IJCNN1 dataset from the LIBSVM library. Three batch sizes were used (1%, 5%, 100%) for both SGD and ALAS (stochastic Newton Algorithm
7
). The exact gradient norm for each of these six cases was plotted against the training epochs on the left, and against the iteration numbers on the right. An epoch is the number of non-overlapping minibatches (and thus iterations) to cover the whole training set. One epoch for a minibatch size of s% (respectively 1%, 5%, 100%) of the training set is equivalent to
100/s
(respectively 100, 20, 1) iterations. Thus, for SGD-ALGO (1%), as shown, 10 epochs is equivalent to 1,000 iterations, with the same gradient norm. The markers on the curves were placed every 100 epochs (left) and 800 iterations (right). For the same number of epochs, say 10, SGD with smaller minibatches yielded lower gradient norm. The same was true for Algorithm
7
for number of epochs less than 10, but the gradient norm plateaued out after that with a lot of noise. Second-order Algorithm
7
converged faster than 1st-order SGD.
See
[
145
]
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

0.6.7
Stochastic Newton method with 2nd-order line search

The stochastic Newton method in Algorithm
7
, described in
[
145
]
, is a generalization of the deterministic Newton method in Algorithm
3
to add stochasticity via random selection of minibatches and Armijo-like 2nd-order line search.
206
206

206

The Armijo line search itself is 1st order; see Section
0.6.2
on full-batch deterministic optimization.

Upon a random selection of a minibatch as in Eq. (
150
), the computation of the estimates for the cost function
\widetilde{J}
in Eq. (
152
), the gradient
\widetilde{\bm{g}}
in Eq. (
153
), and the Hessian
\widetilde{\bm{H}}
(new quantity) can proceed similar to that used for the standard SGD in Algorithm
4
, recalled below for convenience:

\displaystyle\mathbb{B}^{|\text{m}|}=\{\bm{x}^{|i_{1}|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|i_{\text{m}}|}\}\subseteq\mathbb{X}=\{\bm{x}^{|1|},\cdots,\bm{x}^{|\text{M}|}\}\ ,

(
150
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}J_{i_{k}}(\bm{\theta})\ ,\text{ with }J_{i_{k}}(\bm{\theta})=J(f(\bm{x}^{|i_{k}|},\bm{\theta}),\bm{y}^{|i_{k}|})\ ,\text{ and }\bm{x}^{|i_{k}|}\in\mathbb{B}^{|\text{m}|}\ ,\bm{y}^{|i_{k}|}\in\mathbb{T}^{|\text{m}|}\ ,

(
152
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{g}}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}\frac{\partial\widetilde{J}_{i_{k}}(\bm{\theta})}{\partial\bm{\theta}}\ ,

(
153
)

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{H}}(\bm{\theta})=\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{J}(\bm{\theta})}{(\partial\bm{\theta})^{2}}=\frac{1}{\text{m}}\sum_{k=1}^{k=\text{m}\leq\text{M}}\frac{\partial^{2}\widetilde{J}_{i_{k}}(\bm{\theta})}{(\partial\bm{\theta})^{2}}\ .

(271)

In the above computation, the minibatch in Algorithm
7
is fixed, not adaptive such as in Algorithm
6
.

If the current iterate
\bm{\theta}_{k}
is in a flat region (or plateau) or at the bottom of a local convex bowl, then the smallest eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}
of the Hessian estimate
\widetilde{\bm{H}}
would be close to zero or positive, respectively, and the gradient estimate would be zero (line
7
in Algorithm
7
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}\geq-\delta^{1/2}\text{ and }\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}\parallel=0\ ,

(272)

where
\delta
is a small positive number. In this case, no step (or update) would be taken, which is equivalent to setting step length to zero or descent direction to zero
207
207

207

See Step 2 of Algorithm 1 in
[
145
]
.

, then go to the next iteration
(k+1)
. Otherwise, i.e., the conditions in Eq. (
272
) are not met, do the remaining steps.

If the current iterate
\bm{\theta}_{k}
is on the downward side of a saddle point, characterized by the condition that the smallest eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
is clearly negative (line
7
in Algorithm
7
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}<-\delta^{1/2}<0\ ,

(273)

then find the eigenvector
\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}
corresponding to
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
, and scale it such that its norm is equal to the absolute value of this negative smallest eigenvalue, and such that this eigenvector forms an obtuse angle with the gradient
\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}
, then select such eigenvector as the descent direction
\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}\text{ such that }\parallel\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}\parallel=-\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\text{ and }\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}<0\Rightarrow\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=\widetilde{\bm{v}}_{k}

(274)

When the iterate
\bm{\theta}_{k}
is in a local convex bowl, the Hessian
\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{k}
is positive definite, i.e., the smallest eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
is strictly positive, then use the Newton direction as descent direction
\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}
(line
7
in Algorithm
7
):

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}>\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}>0\Rightarrow\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{k}\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}

(275)

The remaining case is when the iterate
\bm{\theta}_{k}
is close to a saddle point such that the smallest eigenvalue
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
is bounded below by
-\delta^{1/2}
and above by
\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}
, then
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
is nearly zero, and thus the Hessian estimate
\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{k}
is nearly singular. Regularize (or stabilize) the Hessian estimate, i.e, move its smallest eigenvalue away from zero, by adding a small perturbation diagonal matrix using the sum of the bounds
\delta^{1/2}
and
\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}
. The regularized (or stabilized) Hessian estimate is no longer nearly singular, thus invertible, and can be used to find the Newton descent direction (lines
7
-
7
in Algorithm
7
for stochastic Newton and line
3
in Algorithm
3
for deterministic Newton):

\displaystyle-\delta^{1/2}\leq\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\leq\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}\Rightarrow 0<\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}+\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}+\delta^{1/2}\Rightarrow\left[\widetilde{\bm{H}}_{k}+\left(\parallel\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\parallel^{1/2}+\delta^{1/2}\right)\bm{I}\right]\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}=-\widetilde{\bm{g}}_{k}\ .

(276)

If the stopping criterion is not met, use Armijo’s rule to find the step length
\epsilon_{k}
to update the parameter
\bm{\theta}_{k}
to
\bm{\theta}_{k+1}
, then go to the next iteration
(k+1)
. (line
7
in Algorithm
7
).
The authors of

[
145
]
provided a detailed discussion of their stopping criterion. Otherwise, the stopping criterion is met, accept the current iterate
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}
as local minimizer estimate
\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}^{\star}
, stop the Newton-descent ②
for
loop to end the current training epoch
\tau
.

From Eq. (
138
), the deterministic 1st-order Armijo’s rule for steepest descent can be written as:

\displaystyle J(\bm{\theta}_{k}+\epsilon_{k}\,\bm{d}_{k})\leq J(\bm{\theta}_{k})-\alpha\beta^{a}\rho\parallel\bm{d}\parallel^{2}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{d}=-\bm{g}\ ,

(277)

with
a
being the minimum power for Eq. (
277
) to be satisfied. In Algorithm
7
, the Armijo-like 2nd-order line search reads as follows:

\displaystyle\widetilde{J}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k}+\epsilon_{k}\,\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k})\leq\widetilde{J}(\widetilde{\bm{\theta}}_{k})-\frac{1}{6}(\beta^{a})^{3}\rho\parallel\widetilde{\bm{d}}_{k}\parallel^{3}\ ,

(278)

with
a
being the minimum power for Eq. (
278
) to be satisfied. The parallelism between Eq. (
278
) and Eq. (
277
) is clear; see also Table
4
.

Figure
78
shows the numerical results of Algorithm
7
on the IJCNN1 dataset (
[
211
]
) from the Library for Vector Support Machine (LIBSVM) library by
[
212
]
. It is not often to see plots versus epochs side by side with plots versus iterations. Some papers may have only plots versus iterations (e.g.,
[
182
]
); other papers may rely only on plots versus epochs to draw conclusions (e.g.,
[
56
]
). Thus Figure
78
provides a good example to see the differences, as noted in Remark
0.6.17
.

Remark 0.6.17
.

Epoch counter vs global iteration counter in plots.

When plotted gradient norm versus epochs (left of Figure
78
), the three curves for
SGD
were separated, with faster convergence for smaller minibatch sizes Eq. (
148
), but the corresponding three curves fell on top of each other when plotted versus iterations (right of Figure
78
). The reason was the scale on the horizontal axis was different for each curve, e.g., 1 iteration for full batch was equivalent to 100 iterations for minibatch size at 1% of full batch. While the plot versus iterations was the zoom-in view, but for each curve separately. To compare the rates of convergence among different algorithms and different minibatch sizes, look at the plots versus epochs, since each epoch covers the whole training set. It is just an optical illusion to think that
SGD
with different minibatch sizes had the same rate of convergence.

\hfill\blacksquare

[
145
]
planned to test their Algorithm

on large datasets such as the CIFAR-10, and report the results in 2020.

0.7
Dynamics, sequential data, sequence modeling

0.7.1
Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs)

In many fields of physics, the respective governing equations that describe the response of a system to (external) stimuli follow a common pattern.
The temporal and/or spatial change of some quantity of a system is balanced by sources that cause the change, which is why we refer to equations of this kind as balance relations.
The balance of linear momentum in mechanics, for instance, establishes a relationship between the temporal change of a body’s linear momentum and the forces acting on the body.
Along with kinematic relations and constitutive laws, the balance equations provide the foundation to derive the equations of motion of some mechanical system.
For linear problems, the equations of motion constitute a system of second-order ODEs in appropriate (generalized) coordinates
\bm{d}
, which, in case of continua, is possibly obtained by some spatial discretization,

\bm{M}{\vbox{\hbox{\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{\kern-0.09995pt$\bullet$}}\hbox{$\bm{d}$\lower 1.29167pt\hbox{}}}}+\bm{D}{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{d}$}}}+\bm{C}\bm{d}=\bm{f}.

(279)

In the above equation,
\bm{M}
denotes the mass matrix,
\bm{D}
is a damping matrix and
\bm{C}
is the stiffness matrix;
\bm{f}
is the vector of (generalized) forces.
The equations of motion can be rewritten as a system of first-order ODEs by introducing the vector of (generalized) velocities
\bm{v}
,

\begin{bmatrix}{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{d}$}}}\\
{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{v}$}}}\end{bmatrix}=\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}&\bm{I}\\
-\bm{M}^{-1}\bm{C}&-\bm{M}^{-1}\bm{D}\end{bmatrix}}_{\bm{A}}\begin{bmatrix}\bm{d}\\
\bm{v}\end{bmatrix}+\underbrace{\begin{bmatrix}\bm{0}\\
\bm{M}^{-1}\end{bmatrix}}_{\bm{B}}\bm{f}\ .

(280)

In control theory,
\bm{A}
and
\bm{b}=\bm{B}\bm{f}
are referred to as state matrix and input vector, respectively.
209
209

209

The state-space representation of time-continuous LTI-systems in control theory, see, e.g., Chapter 3 of
[
213
]
, “State Variables and the State Space Description of Dynamic Systems” is typically written as

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{x}$}}}=\bm{A}\bm{x}+\bm{B}\bm{u}
, with the output equation

\bm{y}=\bm{C}\bm{x}+\bm{D}\bm{u}
.
The state vector is denoted by
\bm{x}
,
\bm{u}
and
\bm{y}
are the vectors of inputs and outputs, respectively.
The ODE describes the (temporal) evolution of the system’s state.
The output of the system
\bm{y}
is a linear combination of states
\bm{x}
and the inputs
\bm{u}
.

If we gather the (generalized) coordinates and velocities in the state vector
\bm{q}
,

\bm{q}=\begin{bmatrix}\bm{d}^{T}&\bm{v}^{T}\end{bmatrix}^{T},

(281)

we obtain a compact representation of the equations of motion, which relates the temporal change of the system’s state

\bullet
\bm{q}

to its current state
\bm{q}
and the input
\bm{b}
linearly by means of

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{q}$}}}=\bm{A}\bm{q}+\bm{B}\bm{f}=\bm{A}\bm{q}+\bm{b}.

(282)

We
found
similar relations in neuroscience,
210
210

210

See Section
0.13.2
on “Dynamic, time dependence, Volterra series”.

where the dynamics of neurons
was
accounted for, e.g., in the pioneering work

[
214
]
,
whose author
modeled a neuron as electrical circuit with capacitances.
Time-continuous RNNs were considered in a paper on back-propagation
[
215
]
.
The temporal change of an RNN’s state is related to the current state
\bm{y}
and the input
\bm{x}
by

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{y}$}}}=-\bm{y}+a(\bm{W}\bm{y})+\bm{x},

(283)

where
a
denotes a non-linear activation function as, e.g., the sigmoid function, and
\bm{W}
is the weight matrix that describes the connection among neurons.
211
211

211

See the general time-continuous neural network with a continuous delay described by Eq. (
529
).

Returning to mechanics, we are confronted with the problem that the equations of motion do not admit closed-form solutions in general.
To construct approximate solution, time-integration schemes need to be resorted to, where we
mention a few examples such as Newmark’s method

[
216
]
,
the Hilber-Hughes-Taylor (HHT) method

[
217
]
, and
the generalized-
\alpha
method

[
218
]
.
For simplicity, we have a closer look at the classical trapezoidal rule, in which the time integral of some function
f
over a time step
\Delta t=t_{n+1}-t_{n}
is approximated by the average of the function values
f(t_{n+1})
and
f(t_{n})
.
If we apply the trapezoidal rule to the system of ODEs in Eq. (
282
), we obtain a system of algebraic relations that reads

\bm{q}^{[n+1]}=\bm{q}^{[n]}+\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left(\bm{A}\bm{q}^{[n]}+\bm{b}^{[n]}+\bm{A}\bm{q}^{[n+1]}+\bm{b}^{[n+1]}\right).

(284)

Rearranging the above relation for the new state gives the update equation for the state vector,

\bm{q}^{[n+1]}=\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{I}+\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right)\bm{q}^{[n]}+\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{b}^{[n]}+\bm{b}^{[n+1]}\right),

(285)

which determines the next state
\bm{q}^{[n+1]}
in terms of the state
\bm{q}^{[n]}
as well as the inputs
\bm{b}^{[n]}
and
\bm{b}^{[n+1]}
.
212
212

212

We can regard the trapezoidal rule as a combination of Euler’s explicit and implicit methods.
The
explicit Euler
method approximates time-integrals by means of rates (and inputs) at the beginning of a time step.
The next state (at the end of a time step) is obtained from previous state and the previous input as

\bm{q}_{n+1}=\bm{q}_{n}+\Delta t\,f(\bm{q}_{n},\bm{b}_{n})

=\left(\bm{I}+\Delta t\,\bm{A}\right)\bm{q}_{n}+\Delta t\,\bm{b}_{n}.

On the contrary, the
implicit Euler
method uses rates (and inputs) at the end of a time step, which leads to the update relation

\bm{q}_{n+1}=\bm{q}_{n}+\Delta t\,f(\bm{q}_{n+1},\bm{b}_{n+1})

=\left(\bm{I}-\Delta t\,\bm{A}\right)^{-1}\bm{q}_{n}+\Delta t\,\bm{b}_{n+1}.

To keep it short, we introduce the matrices
\bm{W}
and
\bm{U}
as

\bm{W}=\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right)^{-1}\left(\bm{I}+\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right),\qquad\bm{U}=\frac{\Delta t}{2}\left(\bm{I}-\frac{\Delta t}{2}\bm{A}\right)^{-1},

(286)

which allows us to rewrite
Eq. (
285
) as

\bm{q}^{[n+1]}=\bm{W}\bm{q}^{[n]}+\bm{U}\left(\bm{b}^{[n]}+\bm{b}^{[n+1]}\right).

(287)

The update equation of time-discrete RNNs is similar to the discretized equations of motion
Eq. (
287
).
Unlike feed-forward neural networks, the state
\bm{h}
of an RNN at the
n
-th time step,
213
213

213

Though the elements
\bm{x}^{[n]}
of a sequence are commonly referred to as “time steps”, the nature of a sequence is not necessarily temporal.
The time step index
t
then merely refers to a position within some given sequence.

which is denoted by
\bm{h}^{[n]}
, does not only depend on the current input
\bm{x}^{[n]}
, but also on the state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
of the previous time step
n-1
.
Following the notation
in

[
78
]
, we introduce a
transition function

f
that produces the new state,

\bm{h}^{[n]}=f(\bm{h}^{[n-1]},\bm{x}^{[n]};\bm{\theta}).

(288)

Remark 0.7.1
.

In
[
78
]
, there is a distinction
between the “hidden state” of an RNN cell at the
n
-th step denoted by
\bm{h}^{[n]}
, where “
h
” is mnemonic for “hidden”, and the cell’s output
\bm{y}^{[n]}
.
The output of a multi-layer RNN is a linear combination of the last layer’s hidden state.
Depending on the application, the output is not necessarily computed at every time step, but the network can ‘‘summarize’’ sequences of inputs to produce an output after a certain number of steps.
214
214

214

cf.
[
78
]
, p. 371, Figure 10.5.

If the output is identical to the hidden state,

\displaystyle\bm{y}^{[n]}\equiv\bm{h}^{[n]},

(289)

\bm{h}^{[n]}
and
\bm{y}^{[n]}
can be used interchangeably.
In the current Section
0.7
on “Dynamics, sequential data sequence modeling”,
the notation
\bm{h}^{[n]}
is used, whereas in Section
0.4
on “Static, feedforward networks”, the notation
\bm{y}^{(\ell)}
is used to designate the output of the “hidden layer”
(\ell)
, keeping in mind the equivalence in Eq. (
26
) in Remark
0.4.2
.
whenever necessary, readers are reminded of the equivalence in Eq. (
289
) to avoid possible confusion when reading deep-learning literature.

\blacksquare

The above relation is illustrated as a circular graph in Figure
79
(left), where the delay is explicit in the superscript of
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
.
The hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
at
n-1
, in turn, is a function of the hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-2]}
and the input
\bm{x}^{[n-1]}
,

\bm{h}^{[n]}=f(f(\bm{h}^{[n-2]},\bm{x}^{[n-1]};\bm{\theta}),\bm{x}^{[n]};\bm{\theta}).

(290)

Continuing this
unfolding
process repeatedly until we reach the beginning of a sequence, the recurrence can be expressed as a function
g^{[n]}
,

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{[n]}=g^{[n]}(\bm{x}^{[n]},\bm{x}^{[n-1]},\bm{x}^{[n-2]},\ldots,\bm{x}^{[2]},\bm{x}^{[1]},\bm{h}^{[0]};\bm{\theta})=g^{[n]}(\{\bm{x}^{[k]},k=1,\ldots,n\},\bm{h}^{[0]};\bm{\theta}),

(291)

which takes the entire sequence up to the current step
n
, i.e.,
\{\bm{x}^{[k]},k=1,\ldots,n\}
(along with an initial state
\bm{h}^{[0]}
and parameters
\bm{\theta}
), as input to compute the current state
\bm{h}^{[n]}
.
The unfolded graph representation is illustrated in Figure
79
(right).

Figure 79
:

Folded and unfolded discrete RNN

(Section
0.7.1
,
0.13.2
).

Left:

at configuration (or state) number
[k]
