k
is an integer, with input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
to a multilayer neural network
f(\cdot)=f^{(1)}\circ f^{(2)}\circ\cdots\circ f^{(L)}(\cdot)
as in Eq. (
22
), having a feedback loop
\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
with delay by one step, to produce output
\bm{h}^{[k]}
.

Right:

Unfolded discrete RNN
, where the feedback loop is unfolded, centered at
k=n
, as represented by Eq. (
288
).
This graphical representation, with
f(\cdot)
being a multilayer neural network, is more general than Figure 10.2 in

[
78
]
, p. 366, and is a particular case of Figure 10.13b in
[
78
]
, p. 388.
See also the continuous RNN explained in Section
0.13.2
on “Dynamic, time dependence, Volterra series”, Eq. (
529
), Figure
135
, for which we refer readers to Remark
0.7.1
and the notation equivalence
\bm{y}^{[k]}\equiv\bm{h}^{[k]}
in Eq. (
289
).

As an example, consider the default
(“vanilla”)
single-layer RNN provided by PyTorch
215
215

215

See PyTorch documentation: Recurrent layers,
Original website

(
Internet archive
)

and TensorFlow
216
216

216

See TensorFlow API: TensorFlow Core r1.14: tf.keras.layers.SimpleRNN,
Original website

(
Internet archive
)

,
which is also described in
[
78
]
, p. 370:

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{[n]}

\displaystyle=\bm{b}+\bm{W}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+\bm{U}\bm{x}^{[n]},

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{[n]}

\displaystyle=a(\bm{z}^{[n]})=\tanh(\bm{z}^{[n]}).

(292)

First,
\bm{z}^{[n]}
is formed from an affine transformation of the current input
\bm{x}^{[n]}
, the previous hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
and the bias
\bm{b}
using weight matrices
\bm{U}
and
\bm{W}
, respectively.
Subsequently, the hyperbolic tangent is applied to the elements of
\bm{z}^{[n]}
as activation function that produces the new hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n]}
.

A common design pattern of RNNs adds a linear output layer to the simplistic example in Figure
79
, i.e., the RNN has a recurrent connection between its hidden units, which represent the state
\bm{h}
,
217
217

217

For this reason,
\bm{h}
is typically referred to as the
hidden state
of an RNN.

and produces an output at each time step.
218
218

218

Such neural network is
universal
, i.e., any function computable by a
Turing machine
can be computed by an RNN of finite size, see
[
78
]
, p. 368.

Figure
80
shows a two-layer RNN, which extends our above example by a second layer, i.e., the first layer is identical to Eq. (
0.7.1
),

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{[n]}_{1}

\displaystyle=\bm{b}+\bm{W}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}_{1}+\bm{U}\bm{x}^{[n]},

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{[n]}_{1}

\displaystyle=a_{1}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{1})=\tanh(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{1}).

(293)

and the second layer forms a linear combination of the first layer’s output
\bm{h}^{[n]}_{1}
and the bias
\bm{c}
using weights
\bm{V}
. Assuming the output
\bm{h}^{[n]}_{2}
is meant to represent probabilities, it is input to a
\softmax(\cdot)
activation (a derivation of which is provided in Remark
0.5.3
in Section
0.5.1
):

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{[n]}_{2}

\displaystyle=\bm{c}+\bm{V}\bm{h}^{[n]}_{1},

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{[n]}_{2}

\displaystyle=a_{2}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{2})=\softmax(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{2})\ ,

(294)

which was given in Eq. (
97
); see
[
78
]
, p. 369.
The parameters of the network are the weight matrices
\bm{W}
,
\bm{U}
and
\bm{V}
, as well as the biases
\bm{b}
and
\bm{c}
of the recurrence layer and the output layer, respectively.

Figure 80
:

RNN with two multilayer neural networks (MLNs),
(Section
0.7.1
) denoted by
f_{1}(\cdot)
and
f_{2}(\cdot)
, whose outputs are fed into the loss function for optimization. This RNN is a generalization of the RNN in Figure
79
, and includes the RNN in Figure 10.3 in
[
78
]
, p. 369, as a particular case, where Eq. (
295
) of the first layer is simply
\bm{h}_{1}^{[n]}=f_{1}(\bm{x}^{[n]})=a_{1}(\bm{z}_{1}^{[n]})
, with
\bm{z}_{1}^{[n]}=\bm{b}+\bm{W}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}_{1}+\bm{U}\bm{x}^{[n]}
and
a_{1}(\cdot)=\tanh(\cdot)
, whereas Eq. (
295
) is
\bm{h}_{2}^{[n]}=f_{2}(\bm{x}^{[n]})=a_{2}(\bm{z}_{2}^{[n]})
, with
\bm{z}_{2}^{[n]}=\bm{c}+\bm{V}\bm{h}^{[n]}_{1}
and
a_{2}(\cdot)=\text{softmax}(\cdot)
for the second layer.
The general relation for both MLNs is
\bm{h}_{j}^{[n]}=f_{j}(\bm{h}^{[n-1]}_{j},\bm{h}^{[n]}_{j-1})=a_{j}(\bm{z}_{j}^{[n]})
, for
j=1,2
.

Irrespective of the number of layers, the hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n]}_{j}
of the
j
-th layer is gennerally computed from the previous hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}_{j}
and the input to the layer
\bm{h}^{[n]}_{j-1}
,

\bm{h}^{[n]}_{j}=f_{j}(\bm{h}^{[n-1]}_{j},\bm{h}^{[n]}_{j-1})=a_{j}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{j}).

(295)

Other design patterns for RNNs show, e.g., recurrent connections between the hidden units but produce a single output only.
RNNs may also have recurrent connections from the output at one time step to the hidden unit of next time step.

Comparing the recurrence relation in Eq. (
290
) and its unfolded representation in Eq. (
291
), we can make the following observations:

•

The unfolded representation after
n
steps
g^{[n]}
can be regarded as a factorization into repeated applications of
f
.
Unlike
g^{[n]}
, the transition function
f
does not depend on the length of the sequence and always has the same input size.

•

The same transition function
f
with the same parameters
\bm{\theta}
is used in every time step.

•

A state
\bm{h}^{[n]}
contains information about the whole past sequence.

Remark 0.7.2
.

Depth of RNNs.

For the above reasons and Figures
79
-
80
, “RNNs, once unfolded in time, can be seen as very deep feedforward networks in which all the layers share the same weights”
[
13
]
.
See Section
0.4.6
on network depth and Remark
0.4.5
.

\hfill\blacksquare

By nature, RNNs are typically employed for the processing of sequential data
\bm{x}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{x}^{[\tau]}
, where the sequence length
\tau
typically need not be constant.
To process data of variable length, parameter sharing is a fundamental concept that characterizes RNNs.
Instead of using separate parameters for each time step in a sequence, the same parameters are shared across several time-steps.
The idea of parameter sharing does not only allows us to process sequences of variable length (and possibly not seen during training), the
“statistical strength”
219
219

219

see
[
78
]
, p. 363.

is also shared across different positions in time, which is important if relevant information occurs at different positions within a sequence.
A fully-connected feedforward neural network that takes each element of a sequence as input instead needs to learn all its rules separately for each position in the sequence.

Comparing the update equations Eq. (
287
) and Eq. (
0.7.1
), we note the close resemblance of dynamic systems and RNNs.
Let aside the non-linearity of the activation function and the presence of the bias vector, both have state vectors with recurrent connections to the previous states.
Employing the trapezoidal rule for the time-discretization, we find a recurrence in the input, which is not present in the type of RNNs described above.
The concept of parameter sharing in RNNs translates into the notion of time-invariant systems in dynamics, i.e., the state matrix
\bm{A}
does not depend on time.
In the computational mechanics context, typical outputs of a simulation could be, e.g., the displacement of a structure at some specified point or the von-Mises stress field in its interior.
The computations required for determining the output from the state (e.g., nodal displacements of a finite-element model) depend on the respective nature of the output quantity and need not be linear.

The crucial challenge in RNNs is to learn long-term dependencies, i.e., relations among distant elements in input sequences.
For long sequences, we face the problem of vanishing or exploding gradients when training the network by means of back-propagation.
To understand vanishing (or exploding) gradients, we can draw analogies between RNNs and dynamic systems once again.
For this purpose, we consider an RNN without inputs whose activation function is the identity function:

\bm{h}^{[n]}=\bm{W}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}.

(296)

From the dynamics point of view, the above update equation corresponds to a linear autonomous system, whose time-discrete representation is given by

\bm{h}_{n+1}=\bm{W}\bm{h}_{n}.

(297)

Clearly, the equilibrium state of the above system is the trivial state
\bm{h}=\mathbf{0}
.
Let
\bm{h}_{0}
denote a perturbation of the equilibrium state.
An equilibrium state is called
Lyapunov stable
if trajectories of the system, i.e., the states at times
t\geq 0
, remain bounded.
If the trajectory eventually arrives at the equilibrium state for
t\rightarrow\infty
(i.e., the trajectory is attractive), the equilibrium of the system is called
asymptotically stable
.
In other words, an initial perturbation
\bm{h}_{0}
from the equilibrium state (i.e., the initial state) vanishes over time in the case of asymptotic stability.
Linear time-discrete systems are asymptotically stable if all eigenvalues
\lambda_{i}
of
\bm{W}
have an absolute value smaller than one.
From the unfolded representation in Eq. (
291
) (see also Figure
79
(right)), it is understood that we observe a similar behavior in the RNN described above.
At step
n
, the initial state
\bm{h}^{[0]}
has been multiplied
n
times with the weight matrix
\bm{W}
, i.e.,

\bm{h}^{[n]}=\bm{W}^{n}\bm{h}^{[0]}.

(298)

If eigenvalues of
\bm{W}
have absolute values smaller than one,
\bm{h}^{[0]}
exponentially decays to zero in long sequences.
On the other hand, we encounter exponentially increasing values for eigenvalues of
\bm{W}
with magnitudes greater than one, which is equivalent to an unstable system in dynamics.
When performing back-propagation to train RNNs, gradients of the loss function need to be passed backwards through the unfolded network, where gradients are repeatedly multiplied with
\bm{W}
as is the initial state
\bm{h}^{[0]}
in the forward pass.
The exponential decay (or increase) therefore causes gradients to vanish (or explode) in long sequences, which makes it difficult to learn long-term dependencies among distant elements of a sequence.

Figure 81
:

Folded Recurrent Neural Network (RNN) with Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) cell
(Section
0.7.2
,
0.11.3
).
The cell state at
[k]
is denoted by
\bm{z}_{s}^{[k]}\equiv\bm{c}^{[k]}
. Two feedback loops, one for cell state
\bm{z}_{s}
and one for hidden state
\bm{h}
, with one-step delay
[k-1]
.
The key unified recurring relation is
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\bm{z_{\alpha}^{[k]}})
, with
\alpha\in\{s\text{ (state)},f\text{ (forget)},\mathcal{I}\text{ (Input)},g\text{ (external input)},\mathcal{O}\text{ (Output)}\}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
is a sigmoidal activation (squashing) function, and
\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{[k]}
is a linear combination of some inputs with weights plus biases at cell state
[k]
.
See Figure
82
for unfolded RNN with LSTM cells, and
also Figure
43
in Section
0.2.3
.

0.7.2
Long Short-Term Memory (LSTM) unit

The vanishing (exploding) gradient problem prevents us from effectively learning long-term dependencies in long input sequences by means of conventional RNNs.
Gated RNNs as the long short-term memory (LSTM) and networks based on the gated recurrent unit (GRU) have proven to successfully overcome the vanishing gradient problem in diverse applications.
The common idea of gated RNNs is to create paths through time along which gradients neither vanish nor explode.
Gated RNNs can accumulate information in their state over many time steps, but, once the information has been used, they are capable to forget their state by, figuratively speaking, “closing gates” to stop the information flow.
This concept bears a resemblance to residual networks, which introduce skip connections to circumvent vanishing gradients in deep feed-forward networks; see Section
0.4.6
on network “Architecture”.

Remark 0.7.3
.

What is “short-term” memory?

The vanishing gradient at the earlier states of an RNN (or layers in the case of a multilayer neural network) makes it that information in these earlier states (or layers) did not propagate forward to contribute to adjust the predicted outputs to track the labeled outputs, so to decrease the loss. A state
[k]
with two feedback loops is depicted in Figure
81
. The reason for information in the earlier states not propagating forward was because the weights in these layers did not change much (i.e., did not learn), due to very small gradients, due to repeated multiplications of numbers with magnitude less than 1. As a result, information in earlier states (or “events”) played little role in decreasing the loss function, and thus had only “short-term” effects, rather than the needed long-term effect to be carried forward to the output layer. Hence, we had a short-term memory problem.
See also Remark
0.5.5
in Section
0.5
on back-propagation for vanishing or exploding gradient in multilayer neural networks.

\blacksquare

In their pioneering work on LSTM,
the authors of

[
24
]
presented a mechanism that allows information (inputs, gradients) to flow over a long duration by introducing additional states, paths and self-loops.
The additional components are encapsulated in so-called
LSTM cells
.
LSTM cells are the building blocks for LSTM networks, where they are connected recurrently to each other analogously to hidden neurons in conventional RNNs.
The introduction of a
cell state
220
220

220

The cell state is denoted with the variable
s
in
[
78
]
, p. 399, Eq. (10.41).

\bm{c}
is one of the key ingredients to LSTM.
The schematic cell representation (Figure
81
) shows that the cell state can propagate through an LSTM cell without much interference, which is why this path is described as “conveyor belt” for information
in

[
219
]
.

Another way to explain that could contribute to elucidate the concept is: Since information in RNN cannot be stored for a long time, over many subsequent steps, LSTM cell corrected this short-term memory problem by remembering the inputs for a long time:

“A special unit called the memory cell acts like an accumulator or a gated leaky neuron: it has a connection to itself at the next time step that has a weight of one, so it copies its own real-valued state and accumulates
the external signal, but this self-connection is multiplicatively gated by another unit that learns to decide when to clear the content of the memory.”

[
13
]
.

In a unified manner, the various relations in the original LSTM unit depicted in Figure
81
can be expressed in a single key generic recurring-relation that is more easily remembered:

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\bm{x},\bm{h})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\bm{z}_{\alpha})\text{ with }\alpha\in\{s\text{ (state)},f\text{ (forget)},\mathcal{I}\text{ (Input)},g\text{ (external input)},\mathcal{O}\text{ (Output)}\}\ ,

(299)

where

\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{[k]}
is the input at cell state
[k]
,

\bm{h}=\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
the hidden variable at cell state
[k-1]
,

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
(with “
\mathcal{A}
” being mnemonic for “activation”) is a sigmoidal activation (squashing) function—which can be either the logistic sigmoid function or the hyperbolic tangent function (see Section
0.5.3
)—and
\bm{z}_{\alpha}=\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{[k]}
is a linear combination of some inputs with weights plus biases at cell state
[k]
.
The choice of the notation in Eq. (
299
) is to be consistent with the notation in the relation
\widetilde{\bm{y}}=f({\bm{x}})=a({\bm{W}}{\bm{x}}+{\bm{b}})=a(\bm{z})
in the caption of Figure
32
.

In Figure
81
,
two types of squashing functions are used: One type (three blue boxes with
\alpha\in\{f,\mathcal{I},\mathcal{O}\}
) squashes inputs into the range
(0,1)
(e.g., the logistic sigmoid, Eq. (
126
)), and the other type (purple box with
\alpha=g
, brown box with
\alpha=s
) squashes inputs into the range
(-1,+1)
(e.g., the hyperbolic tangent, Eq. (
127
)).
The
gates
are the activation functions
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
with
\alpha\in\{f,\mathcal{I},g,\mathcal{O}\}
(3 blue and 1 purple boxes), with argument containing the input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
(through
{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{\alpha}^{[k]}{}
).

Remark 0.7.4
.

The activation function
\mathcal{A}_{s}
(brown box in Figure
81
) is a hyperbolic tangent, but is not called a gate, since it has the cell state
{{\mbox{\boldmath$c$}}}^{[k]}{}
, but not the input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
, as argument. In other words, a gate has to take in the input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
in its argument.

\hfill\blacksquare

There are two feedback loops, each with a delay by one step.
The cell-state feedback loop (red) at the top involves the LSTM cell state
\bm{z}_{s}^{[k-1]}\equiv\bm{c}^{[k-1]}
, with a delay by one step. Since the cell state
\bm{z}_{s}^{[k-1]}\equiv\bm{c}^{[k-1]}
is
not
squashed by a sigmoidal activation function, vanishing or exploding gradient is avoided; the “short-term” memory would last longer, thus the name “Long Short-Term Memory”.
See Remark
0.5.5
on vanishing or exploding gradient in back-propagation, and Remark
0.7.3
on short-term memory.

In the hidden-state feedback loop (green) at the bottom, the combination
\bm{z}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[k]}
of the input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
and the previous hidden state
\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
is squashed into the range
(0,1)
by the processor
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}
to form a factor that filters out less important information from the cell state
\bm{z}_{s}^{[k]}\equiv\bm{c}^{[k]}
, which had been squashed into the range
(-1,+1)
.
See Figure
82
for an unfolded RNN with LSTM cells.
See also Appendix
.16
and Figure
152
for an alternative block diagram.

Figure 82
:

Unfolded RNN with LSTM cells
(Sections
0.2.3
,
0.7.2
,
0.12.1
):
In this unfolded RNN, the cell states are centered at the LSTM cell
[k=n]
, preceded by the LSTM cell
[k=n-1]
, and followed by the LSTM cell
[k=n+1]
.
See Eq. (
303
) for the recurring relation among the successive cell states, and Figure
81
for a
folded
RNN with details of an LSTM cell.
Unlike conventional RNNs, in which the hidden state is repeatedly multiplied with its shared weights, the additional cell state of an LSTM network can propagate over several time steps without much interference. For this reason, LSTM networks typically perform significantly better on long sequences as compared to conventional RNNs, which suffer from the problem of vanishing gradients when being trained.
See also
Figure
117

in Section
0.12.2
.

As the term suggests, the presence of gates that control the information flow are a further key concept in gated RNNs and LSTM, in particular.
Gates are constructed from a linear layer with a sigmoidal function (logistic sigmoid or hyperbolic tangent) as activation function that squashes the components of a vector into the range
(0,1)
(logistic sigmoid) or
(-1,+1)
(hyperbolic tangent).
A component-wise multiplication of the sigmoid’s output with the cell state controls the evolution of the cell state (forward pass) and the flow of its gradients (backward pass), respectively.
Multiplication with 0 suppresses the propagation of a component of the cell state, whereas a gate value of 1 allows a component to pass.

To understand the function of LSTM, we follow the paths information is routed through an LSTM cell.
At time
n
, we assume that the hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
and the cell state
\bm{c}^{[n-1]}
from the previous time step along with the current input
\bm{x}^{[n]}
are given.
The hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
and the input
\bm{x}^{[n]}
are inputs to the
forget gate
, i.e., a fully connected layer with a sigmoid non-linearity, and the general expression Eq. (
299
), with
k=n
(see Figure
82
), becomes (Figure
81
, where the superscript
[k]
on
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
was omitted to alleviate the notation)

\alpha=f\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{f}^{[n]}=\mathcal{A}_{f}(\bm{z}_{f}^{[n]})\rightarrow\bm{f}^{[n]}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{f}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n]}_{f}=\bm{W}_{f}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+\bm{U}_{f}\bm{x}^{[n]}+\bm{b}_{f}.

(300)

The weights associated with the hidden state and the cell state are
\bm{W}_{f}
and
\bm{U}_{f}
, respectively; the bias vector of the forget gate is denoted by
\bm{b}_{f}
.
The forget gate determines which and to what extent components of the previous cell state
\bm{c}^{[n-1]}
are to be kept subsequently.
Knowing which information of the cell state to keep, the next step is to determine how to update the cell state.
For this purpose, the hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
and the input
\bm{x}^{[n]}
are input to a linear layer with a hyperbolic tangent as activation function, called the
external input gate
, and the general expression Eq. (
299
), with
k=n
, becomes (Figure
81
)

\alpha=g\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{g}^{[n]}=\mathcal{A}_{g}(\bm{z}_{g}^{[n]})\rightarrow\bm{g}^{[n]}=\tanh(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{g}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n]}_{g}=\bm{W}_{g}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+\bm{U}_{g}\bm{x}^{[n]}+\bm{b}_{g}.

(301)

Again,
\bm{W}_{g}
and
\bm{U}_{g}
are linear weights and
\bm{b}_{g}
represents the bias.
The output vector of the
\tanh
layer, which is also referred to as
cell gate
, can be regarded as a candidate for updates to the cell state.

The actual updates are determined by a component-wise multiplication of the candidate values
\bm{g}^{[n]}
with the
input gate
, which has the same structure as the forget gate but has its own parameters (i.e.,
\bm{W}_{i}
,
\bm{U}_{i}
,
\bm{b}_{i}
), and the general expression Eq. (
299
) becomes (Figure
81
)

\alpha=i\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{i}^{[n]}=\mathcal{A}_{i}(\bm{z}_{i}^{[n]})\rightarrow\bm{i}^{[n]}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{i}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n]}_{i}=\bm{W}_{i}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+\bm{U}_{i}\bm{x}^{[n]}+\bm{b}_{i}\ .

(302)

The new cell state
\bm{c}^{[n]}
is formed by summing the scaled (by the forget gate) values of the previous cell state and scaled (by the input gate) values of the candidate values,

\bm{c}^{[n]}=\bm{f}^{[n]}\odot\bm{c}^{[n-1]}+\bm{i}^{[n]}\odot\bm{g}^{[n]}\ ,

(303)

where the component-wise multiplication of matrices, which is also known by the name Hadamard product, is indicated by a “
\odot
”.

Remark 0.7.5
.

The path of the cell state
\bm{c}^{[k]}
is reminiscent of the identity map that jumps over some layers to create a residual map inside the jump in the building block of a residual network; see Figure
44
and also Remark
0.4.7
on the identity map in residual networks.

\hfill\blacksquare

Finally, the hidden state of the LSTM cell is computed from the cell state
\bm{c}^{[n]}
.
The cell state is first squashed into the range
(-1,+1)
by a hyperbolic tangent
\tanh
, i.e., the general expression Eq. (
299
) becomes (Figure
81
)

\displaystyle\alpha=s\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{s}^{[n]}=\mathcal{A}_{s}(\bm{z}_{s}^{[n]})=\mathcal{A}_{s}(\bm{c}^{[n]})=\tanh\bm{c}^{[n]}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{z}_{s}^{[n]}\equiv\bm{c}^{[n]}\ ,

(304)

before the result
\mathcal{F}_{s}^{[n]}
is multiplied with the output of a third sigmoid gate, i.e., the
output gate
, for which the general expression Eq. (
299
) becomes (Figure
81
)

\alpha=\mathcal{O}\Rightarrow\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n]}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{z}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n]})\rightarrow\bm{o}^{[n]}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{[n]}_{o}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n]}_{o}=\bm{W}_{o}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+\bm{U}_{o}\bm{x}^{[n]}+\bm{b}_{o}\ .

(305)

Hence, the output, i.e., the new hidden state
\bm{h}^{[n]}
, is given by

\bm{h}^{[n]}=\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n]}\odot\mathcal{F}_{s}^{[n]}=\bm{o}^{[n]}\odot\tanh\bm{c}^{[n]}.

(306)

For the LSTM cell, we get an intuition for respective choice of the activation function.
The hyperbolic tangent is used to normalize and center information that is to be incorporated into the cell state or the hidden state.
The forget gate, input gate and output gate make use of the sigmoid function, which takes values between 0 and 1, to either discard information or allow information to pass by.

Figure 83
:

Folded RNN with Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU)
(Section
0.7.3
).
The cell state at
[k-1]
, i.e.,
(\bm{x}^{[k-1]},\bm{h}^{[k-1]})
are inputs to produce the hidden state
\bm{h}^{[k]}
. One feedback loop for the hidden state
\bm{h}
, with one-step delay
[k-1]
.
The key unified recurring relation is
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\bm{z_{\alpha}^{[k-1]}})
, with
\alpha\in\{r\text{ (reset)},u\text{ (update)},\mathcal{O}\text{ (Output)}\}
, where
\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
is a logistic sigmoi activation function, and
\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{[k-1]}
is a linear combination of some inputs with weights plus biases at cell state
[k-1]
.
Compare to the LSTM cell in Figure
81
.

0.7.3
Gated Recurrent Unit (GRU)

In a unified manner, the various relations in the GRU depicted in Figure
83
can be expressed in a single key generic recurring-relation similar to the LSTM Eq. (
299
):

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}(\bm{x},\bm{h})=\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}(\bm{z}_{\alpha})\text{ with }\alpha\in\{r\text{ (reset)},u\text{ (update)},\mathcal{O}\text{ (Output)}\}\ ,

(307)

where

\bm{x}=\bm{x}^{[k-1]}
is the input at cell state
[k-1]
,

\bm{h}=\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
the hidden variable at cell state
[k-1]
,

\mathcal{A}_{\alpha}
the logistic sigmoid activation function, and
\bm{z}_{\alpha}=\bm{z}_{\alpha}^{[k-1]}
a linear combination of some inputs with weights plus biases at cell state
[k-1]
.

To facilitate a direct comparison between the GRU cell and the LSTM cell,
the locations of the boxes (gates) in the GRU cell in Figure
83
are identical to those in the LSTM in Figure
81
. It can be observed that in the GRU cell (1) There is no feedback loop for the cell state, (2) The input is
\bm{x}^{[k-1]}
(instead of
\bm{x}^{[k]}
), (3) The
reset gate
replaces the LSTM forget gate, (4) The
update gate
replaces the LSTM input gate, (5) The
identity map
(no effect on
\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
) replaces the LSTM external input gate, (6) The complement of the update gate, i.e.,
(1-\mathcal{F}_{u})
replaces the LSTM state activation
\mathcal{A}_{s}=\tanh
. There are fewer activations in the GRU cell compared to the LSTM cell.

The GRU was introduced in
[
220
]
, and tested against LSTM and
\tanh
-RNN in
[
221
]
, with concise GRU schematics, and thus not easy to follow, unlike Figure
83
. The GRU relations below follow
[
78
]
, p. 400.

The hidden variable
\bm{h}^{[n]}
, with
k=n
, is obtained from the GRU cell state at
[n-1]
, including the input
\bm{x}^{[n-1]}
, and is a convex combination of
\bm{h}^{[n-1]}
and the GRU
output-gate
effect
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n-1]}
, using the GRU
update-gate
effect
\mathcal{F}_{u}^{[n-1]}
as coefficient

\displaystyle\bm{h}^{[n]}=\mathcal{F}_{u}^{[n-1]}\odot\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+(1-\mathcal{F}_{u}^{[n-1]})\odot\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n-1]}\ .

(308)

For the GRU
update-gate
effect
\mathcal{F}_{u}^{[n-1]}
, the generic relation Eq. (
307
) becomes (Figure
83
, where the superscript
[k-1]
on
\mathcal{F}_{\alpha}
was omitted to alleviate the notation), with
\alpha=u

\mathcal{F}_{u}^{[n-1]}=\mathcal{A}_{u}(\bm{z}_{u}^{[n-1]})\rightarrow{{\mbox{\boldmath$u$}}}_{[n-1]}{}=\mathfrak{s}({{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{u}^{[n-1]}{}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n-1]}_{u}={{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{u}{}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$U$}}}_{u}{}\bm{x}^{[n-1]}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{u}{}\ .

(309)

For the GRU
output-gate
effect
\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n-1]}
, the generic Eq. (
307
) becomes (Figure
83
), with
\alpha=\mathcal{O}
,

\mathcal{F}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[n-1]}=\mathcal{A}_{\mathcal{O}}(\bm{z}_{\mathcal{O}}^{[k-1]})\rightarrow\bm{o}^{[n]}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{[n-1]}_{o}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n-1]}_{o}=\bm{W}_{o}(\mathcal{F}_{r}^{[n-1]}\odot\bm{h}^{[n-1]})+\bm{U}_{o}\bm{x}^{[n-1]}+\bm{b}_{o}.

(310)

For the GRU
reset-gate
effect
\mathcal{F}_{r}^{[n-1]}
, the generic Eq. (
307
) becomes (Figure
83
), with
\alpha=r

\mathcal{F}_{r}^{[n-1]}=\mathcal{A}_{r}(\bm{z}_{r}^{[n-1]})\rightarrow{{\mbox{\boldmath$r$}}}^{[n-1]}{}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{[n-1]}_{r}),\text{ with }\bm{z}^{[n-1]}_{r}={{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{r}{}\bm{h}^{[n-1]}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$U$}}}_{r}{}\bm{x}^{[n-1]}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{r}{}\ .

(311)

Remark 0.7.6
.

GRU has fewer activation functions compared to LSTM, and is thus likely to be more efficient, even though it was stated in
[
221
]
that no concrete conclusion could be made as to “which of the two gating units was better.” See Remark
0.7.7
on the use of GRU to solve hyperbolic problems with shock waves.

\hfill\blacksquare

0.7.4
Sequence modeling, attention mechanisms, Transformer

RNN and LSTM have been well-established for use in sequence modeling and “transduction” problems such as language modeling and machine translation. Attention mechanism, introduced in

[
57
]

[
222
]
, allowed for “modeling of dependencies without regard to their distance in the input and output sequences,” and has been used together with RNNs
[
31
]
. Transformer is a much more efficient architecture that only uses an attention mechanism, but without the RNN architecture, to “draw global dependencies between input and output.”
Each of these concepts is discussed in detail below.

Sequence modeling, encoder-decoder

The term
neural machine translation
describes the approach of using a single neural network to translate a sentence.
221
221

221

See, e.g.,
[
57
]
.

Machine translation is a special kind of sequence-to-sequence modeling problem, in which a
source sequence
is “translated” into a
target sequence
.
Neural machine translation typically relies on
encoder–decoder
architectures.
222
222

222

Autoencoders
are a special kind of encoder-decoder networks, which are trained to reproduce the input sequence, see Section
0.12.4
.

The encoder network converts (encodes) the essential information of the input sequence
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}=\{\bm{x}^{[k]},k=1,\ldots,n\}
into an intermediate, typically fixed-length vector representation, which is also referred to as
context
.
The decoder network subsequently generates (decodes) the output sequence
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}=\{\bm{y}^{[k]},k=1,\ldots,n\}
from the encoded intermediate vector.
The intermediate context vector in the encoder–decoder structure allows for input and output sequences of different length.
Consider, for instance, an RNN composed from LSTM cells (Section
0.7.2
) as encoder network that generates the intermediate vector by sequentially processing the elements (words, characters) of the input sequence (sentence).
The decoder is a second RNN that accepts the fixed-length intermediate vector generated by the encoder, e.g., as the initial hidden state, and subsequently generates the output sequence (sentences, words) element by element (words, characters).
223
223

223

For more details on different types of RNNs, see, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 385, Section 10.4 “Encoder-decoder sequence-to-sequence architectures.”

To make things clearer, we briefly sketch the structure of a typical RNN encoder-decoder model following
[
57
]
.
The encoder
\mathcal{E}
is composed from a recurrent neural network
{\mathbbm{f}}
and some non-linear feedforward function
{\mathbbm{e}}
.
In terms of our notation, let
\bm{x}^{[k]}
denote the
k
-th element in the sequence of input vectors,
\mathcal{X}
, and let
\bm{h}^{[k-1]}
denote the corresponding hidden state at time
k-1
, the hidden state at time
k
follows from the recurrence relation (Figure
79
)

\bm{h}^{[k]}={\mathbbm{f}}(\bm{h}^{[k-1]},\bm{x}^{[k]}).

(312)

In Section
0.7.1
, we referred to
{\mathbbm{f}}
as the transition function (denoted by
f
in Figure
79
).
The context vector
\bm{c}
is generated by another generally non-linear function
{\mathbbm{e}}
, which takes the sequence of hidden states
\bm{x}^{[k]}
,
k=1,\ldots,n
as input:

\bm{c}={\mathbbm{e}}(\{\bm{h}^{[k]},k=1,\ldots,n\}).

(313)

Note that
{\mathbbm{e}}
could as well be a function of just the final hidden state in the encoder-RNN.
224
224

224

See, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 385.

From a probabilistic point of view, the joint probability of the entire output sequence (i.e., the “translation”)
{\bf{y}}
can be decomposed into conditional probabilities of the
k
-th output item
\bm{y}^{[k]}
given its predecessors
\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]}
and the input sequence
{\bf{x}}
, which in term can be approximated using the context vector
\bm{c}
:

P(\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[n]})=\prod_{k=1}^{n}P(\bm{y}^{[k]}\mid\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\mathbf{x})\approx\prod_{k=1}^{n}P(\bm{y}^{[k]}\mid\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{c}).

(314)

Accordingly, the decoder is trained to predict the next item (word, character) in the output sequence
\bm{y}^{[k]}
given the previous items
\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]}
and the context vector
\bm{c}
.
In analogy to the encoder
\mathcal{E}
, the decoder
\mathcal{D}
comprises a recurrence function
{\mathbbm{g}}
and a non-linear feedforward function
{\mathbbm{d}}
.
Practically, RNNs provide an intuitive means to realize functions of variable-length sequences, since the current hidden state in RNNs contains information of all previous inputs.
Accordingly, the decoder’s hidden state
\bm{s}^{[k]}
at step
k
follows from the recurrence relation
{\mathbbm{g}}
as

\bm{s}^{[k]}={\mathbbm{g}}(\bm{s}^{[k-1]},\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{c}).

(315)

To predict the conditional probability of the next item
\bm{y}^{[k]}
by means of the function
{\mathbbm{d}}
, the decoder can therefore use only the previous item
\bm{y}^{[k-1]}
and the current hidden state
\mathbf{s}^{[k]}
as inputs (along with the context vector
\bm{c}
) rather than all previously predicted items
\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]}
:

P(\bm{y}^{[k]}\mid\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{c})={\mathbbm{d}}(\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{s}^{[k]},\bm{c}).

(316)

We have various choices of how the context vector
\bm{c}
and inputs
\bm{y}^{[k]}
are fed into to the decoder-RNN.
225
225

225

See, e.g.,
[
78
]
, p. 386.

The context vector
\bm{c}
, for instance, can either be used as the decoder’s initial hidden state
\bm{s}^{[1]}
or, alternatively, as the first input.

Attention

As the authors of
[
57
]
emphasized, the encoder
“needs to be able to compress all the necessary information of a source sentence into fixed-length vector”
.
For this reason, long sentences pose a challenge in neural machine translation, in particular, as sentences to be translated are longer than the sentences networks have seen during training, which was confirmed by the observations
in

[
223
]
.
To cope with long sentences,
an encoder–decoder architecture,
“which learns to align and translate jointly,”
was proposed in
[
57
]
.
Their approach is motivated by the observation that individual items of the target sequence correspond to different parts of the source sequence.
To account for the
fact that only a subset of the source sequence is relevant when generating a new item of the target sequence,
two key ingredients (alignment and translation) to the conventional encoder–decoder architecture described above were introduced in
[
57
]
, and will be presented below.

☛The
first key ingredient
to their concept of “alignment”
is the idea of
using a distinct context vector
\bm{c}_{k}
for each output item
\bm{y}^{[k]}
instead of a single context
\bm{c}
.
Accordingly, the recurrence relation of the decoder, Eq. (
315
), is modified and takes the context
\bm{c}_{k}
as argument

\displaystyle\bm{s}^{[k]}=g(\bm{s}^{[k-1]},\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{c}_{k}),

(317)

as is the conditional probability of the output items, Eq. (
316
),

P(\bm{y}^{[k]}\mid\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]},{\bf{x}})=P(\bm{y}^{[k]}\mid\bm{y}^{[1]},\ldots,\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{c}_{k})\approx{\mathbbm{d}}(\bm{y}^{[k-1]},\bm{s}^{[k]},\bm{c}_{k}),

(318)

i.e., it is conditioned on distinct context vectors
\bm{c}_{k}
for each output
\bm{y}^{[k]}
,
k=1,\ldots,n
.

The
k
-th context vector
\bm{c}_{k}
is supposed to capture the information of that part of the source sequence
{\bf{x}}
which is relevant to the
k
-th target item
\bm{y}^{[k]}
.
For this purpose,
\bm{c}_{k}
is computed as weighted sum of all hidden states
\bm{h}^{[l]}
,
l=1,\ldots,n
of the encoder:

\bm{c}_{k}=\sum_{l=1}^{n}\alpha_{kl}\bm{h}^{[l]}.

(319)

The
k
-th hidden state of a conventional RNN obeying the recurrence given by Eq. (
312
) only includes information about the preceding items (
1,\ldots,k
) in the source sequence, since the remaining items (
k+1,\ldots,n
) still remain to be processed.
When generating the
k
-th output item, however, we want information about all source items, before
and
after, to be contained in the
k
-th hidden state
\bm{h}^{[k]}
.

For this reason,
using a
bidirectional RNN
226
226

226

See
[
224
]
.

as encoder was proposed in
[
57
]
.
A bidirectional RNN combines two RNNs, i.e., a
forward RNN
and
backward RNN
, which independently process the source sequence in the original and in reverse order, respectively.
The two RNNs generate corresponding sequences of forward and backward hidden states,

{{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm fwd}^{[k]}{}={{\mathbbm{f}}}_{\rm fwd}{}({{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm fwd}^{[k-1]}{},\bm{x}^{[k]}),\qquad{{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm rev}^{[k]}{}={{\mathbbm{f}}}_{\rm rev}{}({{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm rev}^{[k-1]}{},\bm{x}^{[n-k]}).

(320)

In each step, these vectors are concatenated to a single hidden state vector
\bm{h}^{[k]}
, which the authors of
[
57
]
refer to as “
annotation
” of the
k
-th source item:

\bm{h}^{[k]}=\left[\left({{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm fwd}^{[k]}{}\right)^{T},\left({{\mbox{\boldmath$h$}}}_{\rm rev}^{[k]}{}\right)^{T}\right]^{T}.

(321)

They mentioned
“the tendency of RNNs to better represent recent inputs”
as reason why the annotation
\bm{h}^{[k]}
focuses around the
k
-th encoder input
\bm{x}^{[k]}
.

☛As the
second key ingredient
,
the authors of

[
57
]
proposed a so-called
“alignment model”
, i.e., a function
\mathfrak{a}
to compute weights
\alpha_{kl}
needed for the context
\bm{c}_{k}
, Eq. (
319
),

a_{kl}=\mathfrak{a}(\bm{s}^{[k-1]},\bm{h}^{[l]}),

(322)

which is meant to quantify (“score”) the relation, i.e., the
alignment
, between the
k
-th decoder output (target) and inputs
“around”
the
l
-th position of the source sequence.
The score is computed from the decoder’s hidden state of the previous output
\bm{s}^{[k-1]}
and the annotation
\bm{h}^{[l]}
of the
l
-th input item, so it
“reflects the importance of the annotation
\bm{h}^{[l]}
with respect to the previous hidden state
\bm{s}^{[k-1]}
in deciding the next state
\bm{s}^{[k]}
and generating
\bm{y}^{[k]}
.”

The alignment model is represented by a feedforward neural network, which is jointly trained along with all other components of the encoder–decoder architecture.
The weights of the annotations, in turn, follow from the alignment scores upon exponentiation and normalization (through
\softmax(\cdot)
(Section
0.5.1
) along the second dimension):

\alpha_{kl}=\frac{\exp\left(a_{kl}\right)}{\sum_{j=1}^{n}\exp\left({a}_{kj}{}\right)}.

(323)

The weighting in Eq. (
319
) is interpreted as a way
“to compute an expected annotation, where the expectation is over possible alignments"

[
57
]
.
From this perspective,
\alpha_{kl}
represents the probability of an output (target) item
\bm{y}^{[k]}
being aligned to an input (source) item
\bm{x}^{[l]}
.

In neural machine translation it was possible to show
that
the
attention model in
[
57
]
significantly outperformed conventional encoder–decoder architectures, which encoded the entire source sequence into a single fixed-length vector.
In particular, this proposed approach turned out to perform better in translating long sentences, where it could achieve performance on par with phrase-based statistical machine translation approaches of that time.

Transformer architecture

Despite improvements, the attention model
in

[
57
]
shared the fundamental drawback intrinsic to all RNN-based models: The sequential nature of RNNs is adverse to parallel computing making training less efficient as with, e.g., feed-forward or convolutional neural networks, which lend themselves to massive parallelization.
To overcome this drawback,
a novel model architecture, which entirely dispenses with recurrence, was proposed in
[
31
]
.
As the title
“Attention Is All You Need”
already reveals, their approach to neural machine translation is exclusively based on the concept of attention (and some feedforward-layers), which is repeatedly used in the proposed architecture referred to as
“Transformer”
.

In what follows, we describe the individual components of the Transformer architecture, see Figure
85
.
Among those, the
scaled dot-product attention
, see Figure
84
, is a fundamental building block.
Scaled-dot product attention, which is represented by a function
{A}
, compares a query vector
{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}
and a set of
m
key vectors
{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{k}}
to determine the weighting of value vectors
{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
corresponding to the keys.
As opposed to the additive alignment model used
in

[
57
]
, see Eq. (
322
), scaled-dot product attention combines query and key vectors in a multiplicative way.

Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}=[{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{m}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d_{k}}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}=[{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{m}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d_{v}}
denote key and value matrices formed from the individual vectors, where query and key vectors share the dimension
d_{k}
and value vectors are
d_{v}
-dimensional.
The attention model produces a context vector
{\mbox{\boldmath$c$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}}
by weighting the values
{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, according to the multiplicative alignment of the query
q
with keys
{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m
:

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$c$}}={A}({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{k}}}\sum_{i=1}^{m}\frac{\exp({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{i})}{\sum_{j=1}^{m}\exp\left({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}\cdot{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{j}\right)}\,{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{i}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{k}}}\softmax\left({\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}^{T}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}^{T}\right){\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}.

(324)

Scaling with the square root of the query/key dimension is supposed to prevent pushing the
\softmax(\cdot)
function to regions of small gradients for large
d_{k}
as scalar products grow with the dimension of queries and keys.
The above attention model can be simultaneously applied to multiple queries.
For this purpose, let
{\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}=[{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{k}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d_{k}}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}=[{\mbox{\boldmath$c$}}_{1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$c$}}_{k}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d_{v}}
denote matrices of query and context vectors, respectively.
We can rewrite the attention model using matrix multiplication as follows:

{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}={A}({\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}})=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d_{k}}}\softmax\left({\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}^{T}\right){\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d_{k}}.

(325)

Note that
{\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}^{T}
gives a
k\times m
matrix, for which the
\softmax(\cdot)
is computed along the second dimension.

Figure 84
:

Scaled dot-product attention and multi-head attention
(Section
0.7.4
).
Scaled-dot product attention (left) is the elementary building block of the Transformer model.
It compares query vectors (Q) against a set of key vectors (K) to produce a context vector by weighting to value vectors (V) that correspond to the keys.
For this purpose,
\softmax(\cdot)
function is applied to the inner product (MatMul) of the query and key vectors (scaled by a constant).
The output of the
\softmax(\cdot)
represent the weighting by which value vectors are scaled taking the inner product (MatMul), see Eq.(
324
).
To prevent attention functions of the decoder, which generates the output sequence item by item, from using future items of the output sequence, a masking layer is introduced. By the masking, all scores beyond the current time/position are set to
-\infty
.

Multi-head attention (right) combines several (
h
) scaled-dot product attention functions in parallel, each of which is referred to as “head”.
For this purpose, queries, keys and values are projected by means of a head-specific linear layers (Linear), whose outputs are input to the individual scaled dot-product attention functions, see Eq.(
326
).
The context vectors produced by each head are concatenated before being fed into one more linear layer, see Eq.(
327
).

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Based on the concept of scaled dot-product attention,
the idea of using multiple attention functions in parallel rather than just a single one was proposed in
[
31
]
, see Figure
84
.
In their concept of
“Multi-Head Attention”
, each “head” represents a separate context
{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}_{j}
computed from scaled dot-product attention, Eq. (
325
), on queries
{\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}_{j}
, keys
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
and values
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}
, respectively.
The inputs to the individual scaled dot-product attention functions
{\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}_{j}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}
,
j=1,\ldots,h
, in turn, are head-specific (learned) projections of the queries
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Q}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
, keys
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
and values
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{V}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
.
Assuming that queries
{\mbox{\boldmath$q$}}_{i}
,
{\mbox{\boldmath$k$}}_{i}
and
{\mbox{\boldmath$v$}}_{i}
share the dimension
d
, the projections are represented by matrices
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{Q}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{k}}
,
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{K}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{k}}
and
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{V}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d\times d_{v}}
:
227
227

227

Unlike the use of notation in

[
31
]
,
we use different symbols for the arguments of the scaled dot-product attention function, Eq. (
325
), and those of the multi-head attention, Eq. (
327
), to emphasize their distinct dimensions.

{\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}_{j}={\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Q}$}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{Q}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d_{k}},\qquad{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{j}={\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{K}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d_{k}},\qquad{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{j}={\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{V}$}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{j}^{V}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d_{k}}.

(326)

Multi-head attention combines the individual “heads”
{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}_{i}
through concatenation (along the second dimension) and subsequent projection by means of
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}^{O}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{v}h\times d}
,

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}={A}_{\rm mh}({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Q}$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{V}$}})=[{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}_{1},\ldots,{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}_{h}]{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}^{O}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d},\qquad{\mbox{\boldmath$C$}}_{i}={A}({\mbox{\boldmath$Q$}}_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$K$}}_{i},{\mbox{\boldmath$V$}}_{i}),

(327)

where
h
denotes the number of heads, i.e., individual scaled dot-product attention functions used in parallel.
Note that the output of multi-head attention
\mathcal{H}
has the same dimensions of as the input queries
\mathcal{Q}
, i.e.,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Q}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{k\times d}
.

Figure 85
:

Transformer architecture
(Section
0.7.4
).
The Transformer is a sequence-to-sequence model without recurrent connections. Encoder and decoder are entirely built upon scaled dot-product attention.
Items of source and target sequences are numerically represented as vectors, i.e., embeddings.
Positional encodings furnish embeddings with information on their positions within the respective sequences.
The encoder stack comprises
N
layers, each of which is composed from two sub-layers: The first sub-layer is a multi-head attention function used as a
self-attention
, by which relations among the items of one and the same sequence are learned.
The second sub-layer is a
position-wise
fully-connected network.
The decoder stack is also built from
N
layers consisting of three sub-layers.
The first and third sub-layers are identical to the encoder except for masking in the self-attention function.
The second sub-layer is a multi-head attention function using the encoder’s output as keys and values to relate items of the source and target sequences.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

To understand why the projection is essential in the Transformer architecture, we shift our attention (no pun intended) to the encoder-structure illustrated in Figure
85
.
The encoder combines a stack of
N
identical layers, which, in turn, are composed from two sub-layers each.
To stack the layers without further projection, all inputs and outputs of the encoder layers share the same dimension.
The same holds true for each of the sub-layers, which are also designed to preserve the input dimensions.
The first sub-layer is a multi-head
self-attention
function, in which the input sequence attends to itself.
The concept of self-attention is based on the idea to relate different items of a single sequence to generate a representation of the input, i.e.,
“each position in the encoder can attend to all positions in the previous layer of the encoder.”

In the context of multi-head attention, self-attention implies that one and the same sequence multiply serves as queries, keys and values, respectively.
Let
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}=[{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{1}{},\ldots,{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{n}{}]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
denote the input to the self-attention (sub-)layer and
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
the corresponding output, where
n
is the length of the sequence and
d
is the dimension of single items (
“positions”
), self-attention can be expressed in terms of the multi-head attention function as

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}={A}_{\rm self}({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}})={A}_{\rm mh}({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(328)

The authors of
[
31
]
introduced a residual connection around the self-attention (sub-)layer, which, in view of the same dimensions of heads and queries, reduces to a simple addition.

To prevent values from growing upon summation, the residual connection is followed by
layer normalization
as proposed
in

[
225
]
, which scales the input to zero mean and unit variance:

N({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{T}$}})=\frac{1}{\sigma}\left({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{T}$}}-\mu{\bf{\mathbb{I}}}\right),

(329)

where
\mu
and
\sigma
denote the mean value and the standard deviation of the input components;

{\bf{\mathbb{I}}}
is a matrix of ones with the same dimension as the input
\mathcal{T}
.
The normalized output of the encoder’s first sub-layer therefore follows from the sum of inputs to self-attention function
\mathcal{X}
and its outputs
\mathcal{H}
as

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}=N({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}+{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(330)

The output of the first sub-layer is the input to the second sub-layer within the encoder stack, i.e.,
a
“position-wise feed-forward network.”

Position-wise means that a fully connected feedforward network (see Section
0.4
on “Static, feedforward networks”), which is subsequently represented by the function
F
, is applied to each item (i.e.,
“position”
) of the input sequence
{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{i}{}\in{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}=[{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{1}{},\ldots,{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{n}{}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}

“separately and identically.”

In particular,
a network with a single hidden layer and a linear rectifier unit (see Section
0.5.3
on “Rectified linear function (ReLU)”) as activation function was used in
[
31
]
to compute the vectors
{{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}}_{i}{}
:
228
228

228

Eq. (
331
) is meant to reflect the idea off
position-wise
computations of the second sub-layer, since single vectors
{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{i}{}
are input to the feedforward network.
From the computational point of view, however, all “positions” can be processed simultaneously using the same weights and biases as in Eq. (
331
):

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}=\max(\mathbf{0},{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{1}^{T}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{1}{}){{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{2}^{T}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{2}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

Note that the addition of bias vectors
{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{1}{}
,
{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{2}{}
needs to be computed for all positions, i.e., they are added row-wisely to the matrix-valued projections of the layer inputs by means
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{1}^{T}{}
and
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{2}^{T}{}
, respectively.

{{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}}_{i}{}=F({{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{i}{})={{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{2}{}\max(\mathbf{0},{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{1}{}{{\mbox{\boldmath$z$}}}_{i}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{1}{})+{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{2}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d},

(331)

where
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{1}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{h\times d_{ff}}
,
{{\mbox{\boldmath$W$}}}_{2}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{ff}\times h}
denote the connection weights and
{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{1}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{h}
and
{{\mbox{\boldmath$b$}}}_{2}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
are bias vectors.
The individual outputs
{{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}}_{i}{}
, which correspond to the respective items
{{\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}}_{i}{}
of the input sequence
\mathcal{X}
, form the matrix
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}=[{{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}}_{1}{},\ldots,{{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}}_{n}{}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
.

As for the first sub-layer,
the authors of

[
31
]
introduced a residual connection followed by layer normalization around the feedforward network.
The ouput of the encoder’s second sublayer, which, at the same time, is the output of the encoder layer, is given by

{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}=N({\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}+{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(332)

Within the transformer architecture (Figure
85
), the encoder is composed from
N
encoder layers, which are “stacked”, i.e.,
the output of the
\ell
-th layer is input to the subsequent layer
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell+1)}{}={{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}
.
Let
{{\mbox{\boldmath$E$}}}^{(\ell)}{}
denote the
\ell
-th encoder layer composed from the layer-specific self-attention function
{A}_{\rm self}^{(\ell)}
, which takes
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
as input, and the layer-specific feedforward network
F^{(\ell)}
, the layer’s output
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}
is computed as follows:

{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}={{\mbox{\boldmath$E$}}}^{(\ell)}{}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{})=N(F^{(\ell)}(N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}+A_{\rm self}^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{})))+N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}+A_{\rm self}^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}))),

(333)

or, alternatively, using a step-wise representation,

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}

\displaystyle=A_{\rm self}^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(334)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle=N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{X}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}}^{(\ell)}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(335)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle=F^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(336)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}

\displaystyle=N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}^{{(\ell})}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(337)

Note that inputs and outputs of all components of an encoder layer share the same dimensions, which facilitates several layers to be stacked without additional projections in between.

The decoder’s structure within the transformer architecture is similar to that of the encoder, see the right part in Figure
85
.
As the encoder, it is composed from
N
identical layers, each of which combines three sub-layers (as opposed to two sub-layers in the encoder).
In addition to the self-attention sub-layer (first sub-layer) and the fully-connected position-wise feed-forward network (third sub-layer)
, the decoder inserts a
second sub-layer that
“performs multi-head attention over the output of the encoder stack”
in between.
Attending to outputs of the encoder enables the decoder to relate items of the source sequence to items of the target sequence.
Just as in the encoder, residual connections are introduced around each of the decoder’s sub-layers.

Let
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{1}^{{(\ell})}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
denote the input to the
\ell
-th decoder layer, which, for
\ell>1
, is the output
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{D}$}}}^{(\ell-1)}{}\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}
of the previous layer.
The output
{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{D}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}
of the
\ell
-th decoder layer is then obtained through the following computations.
The first sub-layer performs (multi-headed) self-attention over items of the output sequence, i.e., it establishes a relation among them:

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}}_{1}^{(\ell)}{}

\displaystyle=A_{\rm self}^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{1}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(338)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{1}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle=N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{H}$}}}_{1}^{(\ell)}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(339)

The second sub-layer relates items of the source and the target sequences by means of multi-head attention:

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{2}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle={A}_{\rm mh}^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{},{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{E}$}}}^{(\ell)}{},{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{1}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(340)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{2}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle=N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{1}^{{(\ell})}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{2}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(341)

The third sub-layer is a fully-connected feed-forward network (with a single hidden layer) that is applied to each element of the sequence (position-wisely)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{3}^{{(\ell})}{}

\displaystyle=F^{(\ell)}({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{2}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d},

(342)

\displaystyle{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{D}$}}}^{(\ell)}{}

\displaystyle=N({{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Z}$}}}_{2}^{{(\ell})}{}+{{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{Y}$}}}_{3}^{{(\ell})}{})\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times d}.

(343)

The output of the last (
\ell=N
) decoder-layer within the decoder-stack is projected onto a vector that has the dimension of the “vocabulary”, i.e., the set of all feasible output items.
Taking the
\softmax(\cdot)
over the components of the vector produces probabilities over elements of the vocabulary to be item of the output sequence (see Section
0.5.1
).

A Transformer-model is trained on the complete source and target sequences, which are input to the encoder and the decoder, respectively.
The target sequence is shifted right by one position, such that a special token indicating the start of a new sequence can be placed at the beginning, see Fig.
85
.
To prevent the decoder from attending to future items of the output sequence, the (multi-headed) self-attention sub-layer needs to be
“masked”
.
The masking is realized by setting those inputs to the
\softmax(\cdot)
function of the scaled dot-product attention, see Eq. (
324
), for which query vectors are aligned with keys that correspond to items in the output sequence beyond the respective query’s position, set to
-\infty
.
As loss function, the negative log-likelihood is typically used; see Section
0.5.1
on maximum likelihood (probability cost) and Section
0.5.1
on classification loss functions.

As the transformer architecture does not have recurrent connections,

positional encodings
were added to the inputs of both encoder and decoder
[
31
]
, see Figure
85
.
The positional encodings supply the individual items of the inputs to the encoder and the decoder with information about their positions within the respective sequences.
For this purpose, the authors of

[
31
]
proposed to add vector-valued positional encodings
{\mbox{\boldmath$p$}}_{i}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
, which have the same dimension
d
as the input embedding of an item, i.e., its numerical representation as a vector.
They used sine and cosine functions of an items position (
i
) within respective sequence, whose frequency varies (decreases) with the component index (
j
):

p_{i,2j}=\sin\theta_{ij},\qquad p_{i,2j-1}=\cos\theta_{ij},\qquad\theta_{ij}=\frac{j}{10000^{2i/d}},\qquad j=1,\ldots,d.

(344)

The trained Transformer-model produces one item of the output sequence at a time.
Given the input sequence and all outputs already generated in previous steps, the Transformer predicts probabilities for the next item in the output sequence.
Models of this kind are referred to as “auto-regressive”.

The authors of
[
31
]
varied parameters of the Transformer model to study the importance of individual components.
Their “base model” used
N=6
encoder and decoder layers.
Inputs and outputs of all sub-layers are sequences of vectors, which have a dimension of
d=512
.
All multi-head attention functions of the Transformer, see Eq.(
334
), Eq.(
338
) and Eq.(
340
), have
h=$8$
heads each.
Before the alignment scores are computed by each head, the dimensions of queries, keys and values were reduced to
d_{k}=d_{v}=$64$
.
The hidden layer of the fully-connected feedforward network, see Eq.(
331
), was chosen as
d_{ff}=$2048$
neurons wide.
With this set of (hyper-)parameters, the Transformer model features a total of
65\text{\times}{10}^{6}
parameters.
The training cost (in terms of FLOPS) of the attention-based Transformer was shown to be (at least) two orders of magnitudes smaller than comparable models at that time while the performance was maintained.
A refined (“big”) model was able to outperform all previous approaches (based on RNNs or convolutional neural networks) in English-to-French and English-to-German translation tasks.

As of 2022, the
Generative Pre-trained Transformer 3
(GPT-3) model
[
226
]
, which is based on the Transformer architecture, belongs to the most powerful language models.
GPT-3 is an autoregressive model that produces text from a given (initial) text prompt, whereby it can deal with different tasks as translation, question-answering, cloze-tests
229
229

229

A cloze is “a form of written examination in which candidates are required to provide words that have been omitted from sentences, thereby demonstrating their knowledge and comprehension of the text”, see
Wiktionary version 11:23, 2 January 2022
.

and word-unscrambling, for instance.
The impressive capabilities of GPT-3 are enabled by its huge capacity of
175

billion
parameters, which is 10 times more than preceding language models.

Remark 0.7.7
.

Attention mechanism, kernel machines, physics-informed neural networks (PINNs).

In
[
227
]
, a new attention architecture (mechanism) was proposed by using kernel machines discussed in Section
0.8
, whereas in
[
228
]
, the gated recurrent units (GRU, Section
0.7.3
) and the attention mechanism (Section
0.7.4
) were used in conjunction with Physics-Informed Neural Networks (PINNs, Section
0.9.5
) to solve hyperbolic problems with shock waves; Remark
0.9.5
and Remark
0.11.11
.

\hfill\blacksquare

0.8
Kernel machines (methods, learning)

Researchers have observed that as the number of parameters increased beyond the interpolation threshold, or as the number of hidden units in a layer (i.e., layer width) increased, the test error decreased, i.e., such network generalized well; see Figures
60
and
61
. So, as a first step to try to understand why deep-learning networks work (Section
0.14.2
on “Lack of understanding”), it is natural to study the limniting case of infinite layer width first, since it would be relatively easier than the case of finite layer width

[
229
]
; see Figure
148
.

In doing so, a connection between networks with infinite width and the kernel machines or kernel methods, was revealed
[
230
]

[
231
]

[
232
]
. See also the connection between kernel methods and Support Vector Machines (SVM) in Footnote
31
.

“A neural network is a little bit like a Rube Goldberg machine. You don’t know which part of it is really important. … reducing [them] to kernel methods—because kernel methods don’t have all this complexity—somehow allows us to isolate the engine of what’s going on”
[
230
]
.

Quanta Magazine described the discovery of such connection as the
2021 breakthrough in computer science
[
233
]
.

Covariance functions, or covariance matrices, are kernels in Gaussian processes (Section
0.8.3
), an important class of methods in machine learning
[
234
]

[
130
]
.
A kernel method in terms of the time variable was discussed in Section
0.13.2
in connection with the continuous temperal summation in neuroscience. Our aim here is only to provide first-time learners background material on kernel methods in terms of space variables (specifically the “Setup” in
[
235
]
) in preparation to read more advanced references mentioned in this section, such as
[
235
]

[
232
]

[
236
]
etc.

0.8.1
Reproducing kernel: General theory

A kernel
\mathcal{K}(\cdot,y)
is called
reproducing
if its scalar (
L_{2}
) product with a function
f(y)
reproduces the same function
f(\cdot)
itself
[
237
]
:

\displaystyle f(x)=\langle f(y),\mathcal{K}(x,y)\ ,\rangle_{y}=\int f(y)\mathcal{K}(x,y)dy

(345)

where the subscript
y
in the scalar product
\langle\cdot,\cdot\rangle_{y}
indicates the integrand.

Let
\{{\phi}_{1}{}(x),\ldots,{\phi}_{n}{}(x)\}
be a set of linearly independent basis functions. A function
f(x)
can be expressed in this basis as follows:

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\zeta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)\ .

(346)

The scalar product of two functions
f
and
g
is given by:

\displaystyle g(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\eta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)\Rightarrow\langle f,g\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\eta_{i}\langle{\phi}_{i}{},{\phi}_{j}{}\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\eta_{i}{\Gamma}_{ij}{}\ ,

(347)

where

\displaystyle{\Gamma}_{ij}{}=\langle{\phi}_{i}{},{\phi}_{j}{}\rangle\ ,\quad{\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}{}=[{\Gamma}_{ij}{}]>0\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}

(348)

is the Gram matrix,
230
230

230

The stiffness matrix in the displacement finite element method is a Gram matrix.

which is strictly positive definite, with its inverse (also strictly positive definite) denoted by

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$\Delta$}}{}={\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}{}^{-1}=[{\Delta}_{ij}{}]>0\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\ .

(349)

Then a reproducing kernel can be written as
[
237
]
231
231

231

See Eq. (6), p. 346, in
[
237
]
.

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x,y)=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}{\Delta}_{ij}{}{\phi}_{i}{}(x){\phi}_{j}{}(y)={\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}{}^{T}(x){\mbox{\boldmath$\Delta$}}{}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}{}(y)\ ,\text{ with }{\mbox{\boldmath$\Phi$}}{}^{T}(x)=[{\phi}_{1}{}(x),\ldots,{\phi}_{n}{}(x)]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}\ .

(350)

Remark 0.8.1
.

It is easy to verify that the function
\mathcal{K}(x,y)
in Eq. (
350
) is a reproducing kernel:

\displaystyle\langle f(y),\mathcal{K}(y,x)\rangle_{y}

\displaystyle=\langle\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}{\phi}_{i}{}(y),\sum_{j,k}{\Delta}_{jk}{}{\phi}_{j}{}(y){\phi}_{k}{}(x)\rangle_{y}=\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}\sum_{j,k}\langle{\phi}_{i}{}(y),{\phi}_{j}{}(y)\rangle_{y}{\Delta}_{jk}{}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)

(351)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}\sum_{j,k}{\Gamma}_{ij}{}{\Delta}_{jk}{}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)=\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}\sum_{k}\delta_{ik}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)=\sum_{i}\zeta_{i}{\phi}_{i}{}(x)=f(x)\ ,

(352)

using Eqs. (
348
) and (
349
).

\hfill\blacksquare

From Eq. (
347
), the norm of a function
f
can be defined as

\displaystyle\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}=\langle f,f\rangle=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}\zeta_{i}\Gamma_{ij}\zeta_{j}=\bm{\zeta}^{T}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}{}\bm{\zeta}=\bm{\zeta}^{T}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Delta$}}{}^{-1}\bm{\zeta}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{\zeta}^{T}=[\zeta_{1},\ldots,\zeta_{n}]\ ,

(353)

where
\parallel\cdot\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}
denotes the norm induced by the kernel
\mathcal{K}
, and where the matrix notation in Eq. (
11
) was used.

When the basis functions in
\Phi
are mutually orthogonal, then the Gram matrix
\Gamma
in Eq. (
348
) is diagonal, and the reproducing kernel
\mathcal{K}
in Eq. (
350
) takes the simple form:

\displaystyle\mathcal{K}(x,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\Delta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x){\phi}_{k}{}(y)\ ,\text{ with }\Delta_{k}={\Delta}_{ik}{}\delta_{ik}=\Delta_{(kk)}>0\ ,\text{ for }k=1,\ldots,\infty\ ,

(354)

where
\delta_{ij}
is the Kronecker delta, and
where the summation convention on repeated indices, except when enclosed in parentheses, was applied. In the case of infinite-dimensional space of functions, Eq. (
354
), Eq. (
346
) and Eq. (
347
) would be written with
n\rightarrow\infty
:
232
232

232

Eq. (
355
)
1
, and
Eqs. (
356
)
1,2
were given as Eq. (5.46), Eq. (5.47), and Eq. (5.45), respectively, in
[
238
]
, pp. 168-169, where the basis functions
{\phi}_{i}{}(x)
were the “eigen-functions,” and where the general case non-orthogonal basis presented in Eq. (
350
) was not discussed. Technically, Eq. (
356
)
2
is accompanied by the conditions
\Delta_{i}\geq 0
, for
i=1,\ldots,\infty
, and
\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\Delta_{k})^{2}<\infty
, i.e., the sum of the squared coefficients is finite
[
238
]
, p. 188.

\displaystyle f(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)\ ,\quad\langle f,g\rangle=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{k}\eta_{k}\Gamma_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{k}\eta_{k}/\Delta_{k}={{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}{}}^{T}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}{}{{\mbox{\boldmath$\eta$}}{}}

(355)

\displaystyle\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\zeta_{k})^{2}\Gamma_{k}=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\zeta_{k})^{2}/\Delta_{k}={{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}{}}^{T}{\mbox{\boldmath$\Gamma$}}{}{{\mbox{\boldmath$\zeta$}}{}}\ ,\quad\mathcal{K}(x,y)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x){\phi}_{k}{}(y)\ .

(356)

Let
L(y,f(x))
be the loss (cost) function, with
x
being the data,
f(x)
the predicted output, and
y
the label. Consider the regularized minimization problem:

\displaystyle\min_{f}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(y_{i},f(x_{i}))+\lambda\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}\right]=\min_{\{\zeta_{i}\}_{1}^{\infty}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}L(y_{i},\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{j}{\phi}_{j}{}(x_{i}))+\lambda\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}(\zeta_{j})^{2}/\Delta_{j}\right]\ ,

(357)

which is a ‘‘ridge’’ penalty method
233
233

233

See
[
238
]
, Eq. (3.41), p. 61, which is in Section 3.4.1 on “Ridge regression,” which “shrinks the regression coefficients by imposing a penalty on them.” Here the penalty is imposed on the kernel-induced norm of
f
, i.e.,
\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}
.

with
\lambda
being the penalty coefficient (or regularization parameter), with the aim of forcing the norm of the minimizer, i.e,
\parallel f^{\star}\parallel^{2}
, to be as small as possible, by penalizing the objective function (cost
L
plus penalty
\lambda\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}
) if it were not.
234
234

234

In classical regularization, the loss function (1st term) in Eq. (
357
) is called the “empirical risk” and the penalty term (2nd term) the “stabilizer”
[
239
]
.

What is remarkable is that even though the minimization problem is
infinite
dimensional, the minimizer (solution)
f^{\star}
of Eq. (
357
) is
finite
dimensional
[
238
]
:

\displaystyle f^{\star}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha_{i}^{\star}\mathcal{K}(x,x_{i})\ ,

(358)

where
\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{i})
is a basis function, and
\alpha_{i}^{\star}
the corresponding coefficient, for
i=1,\ldots,n
.

Remark 0.8.2
.

Finite-dimensional solution to infinite-dimentional problem.

Since

f(x)
is expressed as in Eq. (
355
)
1
, and

\mathcal{K}(x,y)
as in Eq. (
356
)
2
, it follows that

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{K}(y,x_{i}),f(y)\rangle_{y}=f(x_{i})\ ,

(359)

following the same argument as in Remark
0.8.1
. As a result,

\displaystyle\langle\mathcal{K}(y,x_{i}),\mathcal{K}(y,x_{j})\rangle_{y}=\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x_{j})\ ,\text{ with }i,j=1,\ldots,n\ .

(360)

Thus
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}=\left[\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is the Gram matrix of the set of functions
\{\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{1}),\ldots,\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{n})\}
. To show that
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}>0
, i.e., positive definite, for any set
\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}
, consider

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i}\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x_{j})a_{j}=\sum_{i,j=1}^{n}a_{i}\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{p}{\phi}_{p}{}(x_{i}){\phi}_{p}{}(x_{j})a_{j}=\sum_{p=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{p}(b_{p})^{2}\geq 0\ ,

(361)

\displaystyle b_{p}:=\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}{\phi}_{p}{}(x_{i})\ ,\text{ for }p=1,\ldots,\infty\ ,

(362)

which is equivalent to the matrix
\mathcal{K}
being positive definite, i.e.,
{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}>0
,
235
235

235

See, e.g.,
[
240
]
, p. 11.

and thus the functions
\{\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{1}),

\ldots,

\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{n})\}
are linearly independent, and form a basis, making expression such as Eq. (
358
) possible.

A goal now is to show that the solution to the
infinite
-dimensional regularized minimization problem Eq. (
357
) is

finite
dimensional, for which the coefficients
\alpha_{i}^{\star}
,
i=1,\ldots,n
, in Eq. (
358
) are to be determined. It is also remarkable that the solution of the form Eq. (
358
) holds in general for
any
type of differentiable loss function
L
in Eq. (
357
), and not necessarily restricted to the squared-error loss
[
241
]

[
239
]
.

For notation compactness, let the objective function (loss plus penalty) in Eq. (
357
) be written as

\displaystyle\overline{L}[f]:=\sum_{k=1}^{n}L(y_{k},f(x_{k}))+\lambda\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}L(y_{k},\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{j}{\phi}_{j}{}(x_{k}))+\lambda\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}(\zeta_{k})^{2}/\Delta_{j}\ ,

(363)

and set the derivative of
\overline{L}
with respect to the coefficients
\zeta_{p}
, for
p=1,\ldots,\infty
, to zero to solve for these coefficients:

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\overline{L}}{\partial\zeta_{p}}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial{L}(y_{k},f(x_{k}))}{\partial f}\frac{\partial f(x_{k})}{\partial\zeta_{p}}+2\lambda\frac{\partial\parallel f\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}}{\partial\zeta_{p}}=-\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial{L}(y_{k},f(x_{k}))}{\partial f}{\phi}_{p}{}(x_{k})+2\lambda\frac{\zeta_{p}}{\Delta_{p}}=0

(364)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\zeta_{p}^{\star}=\frac{\Delta_{p}}{2\lambda}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\frac{\partial{L}(y_{k},f(x_{k}))}{\partial f}{\phi}_{p}{}(x_{k})=\Delta_{p}\sum_{k=1}^{n}\alpha^{\star}_{k}{\phi}_{p}{}(x_{k})\ ,\text{ with }\alpha^{\star}_{k}:=\frac{1}{2\lambda}\frac{\partial{L}(y_{k},f(x_{k}))}{\partial f}\ ,

(365)

\displaystyle\Rightarrow f^{\star}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\zeta_{k}^{\star}{\phi}_{k}{}(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{k}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha^{\star}_{i}{\phi}_{k}{}(x_{i}){\phi}_{k}{}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha^{\star}_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{\infty}\Delta_{k}{\phi}_{k}{}(x_{i}){\phi}_{k}{}(x)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}\alpha^{\star}_{i}\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x)\ ,

(366)

where the last expression in Eq. (
366
) came from using the kernel expression in Eq. (
356
)
2
, and the end result is Eq. (
358
), i.e., the solution (minimizer)
f^{\star}
is of finite dimension.
236
236

236

See also
[
238
]
, p. 169, Eq. 5.50, and p. 185.

\hfill\blacksquare

For the squared-error loss,

\displaystyle L({\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{},f({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}{}))=\parallel{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}-f({\mbox{\boldmath$x$}}{})\parallel_{\mathcal{K}}^{2}=\sum_{k=1}^{n}\left[y_{k}-f(x_{k})\right]^{2}=\left[{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}-{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{}^{\star}\right]^{T}\left[{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}-{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{}^{\star}\right]\ ,\text{ with }

(367)

\displaystyle{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}^{T}=\left[y_{1},\ldots,y_{n}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{1\times n}\ ,\quad{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}=\left[\mathcal{K}_{ij}\right]=\left[\mathcal{K}(x_{i},x_{j})\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\ ,\quad{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{}^{\star T}=\left[\alpha^{\star}_{1},\ldots,\alpha^{\star}_{n}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}\ ,

(368)

the coefficients
\alpha^{\star}_{k}
in Eq. (
366
) (or Eq. (
358
)) can be computed using Eq. (
365
)
2
:

\displaystyle\alpha^{\star}_{k}=\frac{1}{\lambda}\left[y_{k}-f^{\star}(x_{k})\right]\Rightarrow\lambda\alpha^{\star}_{k}=y_{k}-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}_{kj}\alpha^{\star}_{j}\Rightarrow\left[{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}+\lambda{\mbox{\boldmath$I$}}{}\right]{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{}^{\star}={\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}\Rightarrow{\mbox{\boldmath$\alpha$}}{}^{\star}=\left[{\mbox{\boldmath$\mathcal{K}$}}{}+\lambda{\mbox{\boldmath$I$}}{}\right]^{-1}{\mbox{\boldmath$y$}}{}\ .

(369)

It is then clear from the above that the “Setup” section in
[
235
]
simply corresponded to the particular case where the penalty parameter was zero:

\displaystyle\lambda=0\Rightarrow y_{k}=f^{\star}(x_{k})=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\mathcal{K}_{kj}\alpha_{j}^{\star}\ ,

(370)

i.e.,
f^{\star}
is interpolating.

For technical jargon such as Reproducing Kernel Hilbert Space (RKHS), Riesz Representation Theorem,
\mathcal{K}(\cdot,x_{i})
as a representer of evaluation at
x_{i}
, etc. to describe several concepts presented above, see
[

]

]

]

]
.

]
.
