).
For each physical problem (ISO or MHD):
(a) Each dominant POD mode has a network to predict
\alpha_{i}(t+t^{\prime})
, with
t^{\prime}
, given
\alpha_{i}(t)
;
(b) All
m
POD dominant modes share the same network to predict
\{\alpha_{i}(t+t^{\prime}),\ i=1,\ldots,m\}
, with
t^{\prime}
, given
\{\alpha_{i}(t),\ i=1,\ldots,m\}

[
26
]
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the author.)

Figure 120
:

Hurst exponent vs POD-mode rank for Isotropic Turbulence (ISO)
(Sections
0.12.3
).
POD modes with larger eigenvalues (Eq. (
451
)) are higher ranked, and have lower rank number, e.g., POD mode rank 7 has larger eigenvalue, and thus more dominant, than POD mode rank 50.
The Hurst exponent, even though fluctuating, trends downward with the POD mode rank, but not monotonically, i.e., for two POD modes sufficiently far apart (e.g., mode 7 and mode 50), a POD mode with lower rank generally has a lower Hurst exponent.
The first 800 POD modes for the ISO problem have the Hurst exponents higher than 0.5, and are thus persistent
[
26
]
.

(Adapted with permission of the author.)

0.12.1
Proper orthogonal decomposition (POD)

The presentation of the
continuous
formulation of POD in this section follows
[
306
]
.
Consider the separation of variables of a time-dependent function
u(\bm{x},t)
, which could represent a component of a velocity field or a magnetic potential in a 3-D domain
\mathcal{B}
, where
\bm{x}\in\mathcal{B}
, and
t
a time parameter:

\displaystyle u(\bm{x},t)=\alpha(t)\phi(\bm{x})\ ,

(442)

where
\alpha(t)
is the time-dependent amplitude, and
\phi(\bm{x})
a function of
\bm{x}
representing the most typical spatial structure of
u(\bm{x},t)
. The goal is to find the function
\phi(\bm{x})
that maximizes the square of the amplitude
\alpha(t)
(or component of
u(\bm{x},t)
along
\phi(\bm{x})
):

\displaystyle\alpha(t)

\displaystyle=\frac{(u,\phi)}{(\phi,\phi)}\ ,\text{ with }(u,\phi):=\int_{\mathcal{B}}u(\bm{y},t)\phi(\bm{y})d\mathcal{B}(\bm{y})\ ,

(443)

\displaystyle(\phi,\phi)

\displaystyle=1\Rightarrow\alpha(t)=(u,\phi)\ ,

(444)

i.e., if
\phi(\bm{x})
is normalized to 1, then the amplitude
\alpha(t)
is the spatial scalar product between
u(\bm{x},t)
and
\phi(\bm{x})
, with
(\cdot,\cdot)
designating the spatial scalar product operator.
Let
\langle\cdot\rangle
designate the time average operator, i.e.,

\displaystyle\langle f(t)\rangle=\frac{1}{T}\int_{t=0}^{t=T}f(t)\,dt\ ,

(445)

where
T
designates the maximum time. The goal now is to find
\phi(\bm{x})
such that the time average of the square of the amplitude
\alpha(t)
is maximized:

\displaystyle\phi(\bm{x})=\underset{\phi}{\text{argmax}}(\langle\alpha^{2}(t)\rangle)=\underset{\phi}{\text{argmax}}(\langle(u,\phi)^{2}\rangle)\ ,

(446)

which is equivalent to maximizing the amplitude
\alpha(t)
, and thus the information content of
u(\bm{x},t)
in
\phi(\bm{x})
, which in turn is also called “coherent structure”. The square of the amplitude in Eq. (
446
) can be written as

\displaystyle\lambda:=\alpha^{2}(t)=(u,\phi)^{2}=\int\limits_{\mathcal{B}}\left[\int\limits_{\mathcal{B}}u(\bm{x},t)u(\bm{y},t)\phi(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}\right]\,\phi(\bm{x})\,d\bm{x}\ ,

(447)

so that
\lambda
is the component (or projection) of the term in square brackets in Eq. (
447
) along the direction
\phi(\bm{x})
. The component
\lambda
is maximal if this term in square brackets is colinear with (or “parallel to”)
\phi(\bm{x})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\int\limits_{\mathcal{B}}u(\bm{x},t)u(\bm{y},t)\phi(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=\lambda\phi(\bm{x})\Rightarrow\int\limits_{\mathcal{B}}\langle u(\bm{x},t)u(\bm{y},t)\rangle\phi(\bm{y})\,d\bm{y}=\lambda\phi(\bm{x})\ ,

(448)

which is a
continuous
eigenvalue problem with the eigenpair being
(\lambda,\phi(\bm{x}))
. In practice, the dynamic quantity
u(\bm{x},t)
is sampled at
k
discrete times
\{t_{1},\ldots,t_{k}\}
to produce
k
snapshots, which are functions of
\bm{x}
, assumed to be linearly independent, and ordered in matrix form as follows

\displaystyle\{u(\bm{x},t_{1})\ldots,u(\bm{x},t_{k})\}=:\{u_{1}(\bm{x}),\ldots,u_{k}(\bm{x})\}=:\bm{u}(\bm{x})\ .

(449)

The coherent structure
\phi(\bm{x})
can be expressed on the basis of the snapshots as

\displaystyle\phi(\bm{x})=\sum_{i=1}^{i=k}\beta_{i}u_{i}(\bm{x})=\bm{\beta}\,{\scriptscriptstyle{\bullet}}\,\bm{u}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{\beta}=\{\beta_{1},\ldots,\beta_{k}\}\ .

(450)

As a result of the discrete nature of Eq. (
449
) and Eq. (
450
), the eigenvalue problem in Eq. (
448
) is discretized into

\displaystyle\bm{C}\bm{\beta}=\lambda\bm{\beta}\ ,\text{ with }\bm{C}:=\left[\frac{1}{k}\int\limits_{\mathcal{B}}\bm{u}(\bm{y})\otimes\bm{u}(\bm{y})d\bm{y}\right]\ ,

(451)

where the matrix
\bm{C}
is symmetric, positive definite, leading to positive eigenvalues in
k
eigenpairs
(\lambda_{i},\bm{\beta}_{i})
, with
i=1,\ldots,k
.
With
\phi(\bm{x})
now decomposed into
k
linearly independent directions
\phi_{i}(\bm{x}):=\beta_{i}u_{i}(\bm{x})
according to Eq. (
450
), the dynamic quantity
u(\bm{x},t)
in Eq. (
442
) can now be written as a linear combination of
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
, with
i=1,\ldots,k
, each with a different time-dependent amplitude
\alpha_{i}(t)
, i.e.,

\displaystyle u(\bm{x},t)=\sum_{i=1}^{i=k}\alpha_{i}(t)\phi_{i}(\bm{x})\ ,

(452)

which is called a proper orthogonal decomposition of
u(\bm{x},t)
, and is recorded in Figure
18
as “Full POD reconstruction”. Technically, Eq. (
450
) and Eq. (
452
) are approximations of infinite-dimensional functions by a finite number of linearly-independent functions.

Usually, a subset of
m\ll k
POD modes are selected such that the error committed by truncating the basis as done in Eq. (
8
) would be small compared to Eq. (
452
), and recalled here for convenience:

\displaystyle u(\bm{x},t)\approx\sum_{j=1}^{j=m}\phi_{i_{j}}(\bm{x})\alpha_{i_{j}}(t)\ ,\text{ with }m\ll k\text{ and }i_{j}\in\{1,\ldots,k\}\ .

(
8
)

One way is to select the POD modes corresponding to the highest eigenvalues (or energies) in Eq. (
448
); see Step
(2)
in Section
0.12.2
.

Remark 0.12.1
.

Reduced-order POD.

Data for two physical problems were available from numerical simulations: (1) the Force Isotropic Turbulence (ISO) dataset, and (2) the Magnetohydrodynamic Turbulence (MHD) dataset
[
105
]
[
105
]
.
For each physical problem,
the authors of

[
26
]
employed

N=6
equidistant 2-D planes (slices, Figure
116
), with 5 of those 2-D planes used for training, and 1 remaining 2-D plane used for testing (see Section
0.6.1
).
The same sub-region of the 6 equidistant 2-D plane (yellow squares in Figure
116
) was used to generate 6 training / testing datasets.
For each training / testing dataset,

k=5,023
snapshots for the ISO dataset and
k=1,024
snapshots for the MHD dataset were used in
[
26
]
. The reason was because the ISO dataset contained 5,023 time steps, whereas the MHD dataset contained 1,024 time steps. So the number of snapshots was the same as the number of time steps. These snapshots were reduced to
m\in\{5,\ldots,10\}
POD modes with highest eigenvalues (thus energies), which were much fewer than the original
k
snapshots, since
m\ll k
.
See Remark
0.12.3
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Remark 0.12.2
.

Another method of finding the POD modes
without forming the symmetric matrix
\bm{C}
in Eq. (
451
) is by using the Singular Value Decomposition (SVD) directly on the rectangular matrix of the sampled snapshots, discrete in both space and time.
The POD modes are then obtained from the left singular vectors times the corresponding singular values. A reduced POD basis is obtained next based on an information-content matrix.
See
[
306
]
,
where
POD was applied to efficiently solve nonlinear electromagnetic problems governed by Maxwell’s equations with nonlinear hysteresis at low frequency (10 kHz), called static hysteresis,
discretized by a finite-element method.
See also Remark
0.12.4
.

\hfill\blacksquare

0.12.2
POD with LSTM-Reduced-Order-Model

Typically, once the dominant POD modes of a physical problem (ISO or MHD) were identified, a reduced-order model (ROM) can be obtained by projecting the governing partial differential equations (PDEs) onto the basis of the dominant POD modes using, e.g., Galerkin projection (GP).
Using this method,
the authors of

[
306
]
employed full-order simulations of the governing electro-magnetic PDE with certain input excitation to generate POD modes, which were then used to project similar PDE with different parameters and solved for the coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
under different input excitations.

Goal for using neural network

Instead of using GP on the dominant POD modes of a physical problem to solve for the coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
as described above,
deep-learning neural network was used in
[
26
]
to predict the next value of
\alpha_{i}(t+t^{\prime})
, with
t^{\prime}>0
, given the current value
\alpha_{i}(t)
, for
i=1,\ldots,m
.

To achive this goal,
LSTM/BiLSTM networks (Figure
117
) were trained using thousands of paired short input / output signals obtained by segmenting the time-dependent signal
\alpha_{i}(t)
of the dominant POD mode
\phi_{i}

[
26
]
.

Data generation, training and testing procedure

The following procedure was adopted in
[
26
]
to develop their LSTM-ROM for two physical problems, ISO and MHD; see Remark
0.12.1
.
For each of the two physical problems (ISO and MHD), the following steps were used:

(1)

From the 3-D computational domain of a physical problem (ISO or MHD), select
N
equidistant 2-D planes that slice through this 3-D domain, and select the same subregion for all of these planes, the majority of which is used for the training datasets, and the rest for the test datasets (Figure
116
and Remark
0.12.1
for the actual value of
N
and the number of training datasets and test datasets employed in
[
26
]
).

(2)

For each of the training datasets and test datasets,
extract from
k
snapshot a few
m\ll k
dominant POD modes
\phi_{i}
,
i=1,\ldots,m

(with the highest energies / eigenvalues)
and their corresponding coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
,
i=1,\ldots,m
, by solving the eigenvalue problem Eq. (
451
), then use Eq. (
450
) to obtain the POD modes
\phi_{i}
, and Eq. (
444
) to obtain
\alpha_{i}(t)
for use in Step
(3)
.

(3)

The time series of the coefficient
\alpha_{i}(t)
of the dominant POD mode
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
of a
training
dataset is chunked into thousands of small samples with
t\in[t_{k},t_{k}^{spl}]
, where
t_{k}
is the time of the
kth
snapshot, by a moving window. Each sample is subdivided into two parts: The input part with time length
t_{inp}
and the output part with time length
t_{out}
, Figure
118
. These thousands of input/output pairs were then used to train LSTM/BiLSTM networks in Step
(4)
.
See Remark
0.12.3
.

(4)

Use the input/output pairs generated from the training datasets in Step
(3)
to train LSTM/BiLSTM-ROM networks. Two methods were considered in
[
26
]
:

(a)

Multiple-network method:

Use a separate RNN for each of the
m
dominant POD modes to separately predict
the coefficient
\alpha_{i}(t)
, for
i=1,\ldots,m
, given a sample input, see Figure
119(a)
and Figure
118
.
Hyperparameters (layer width, learning rate, batch size) are tuned for the most dominant POD mode and reused for training the other neural networks.

(b)

Single-network method:

Use the same RNN to predict the coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
,
i=1,\ldots,m
of all
m
dominant POD modes at once, given a sample input, see Figure
119(b)
and Figure
118
.

The single-network method better captures the inter-modal interactions that describe the energy transfer from larger to smaller scales.
Vortices that spread over multiple dominant POD modes also support the single-network method, which does not artificially constrain flow features to separate POD modes.

(5)

Validation:
Input/output pairs similar to those for training in Step
(3)
were generated from the
test
dataset for validation.
280
280

280

The authors of

[
26
]
did not have a validation dataset as defined in Section
0.6.1
.

With a short time series of the coefficient
\alpha_{i}(t)
of the dominant POD mode
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
of the
test
dataset, with
t\in[t_{k},t_{k}+t_{inp}]
, where
t_{k}
is the time of the
kth
snapshot, and
0<t_{inp}
, as input, use the trained LSTM/BiLSTM networks in Step
(4)
to predict the values of
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}(t)
, with
t\in[t_{k}+t_{inp},t_{k}^{spl}]
, where
t_{k}^{spl}
is the time at the end of the sample, such that the sample length is
t_{k}^{spl}-t_{k}=t_{inp}+t_{out}
and
0<t_{out}\leq t_{inp}
; see Figure
118
. Compute the error between the predicted value
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}(t+t_{out})
and the target value of
\alpha_{i}(t+t_{out})
from the test dataset. Repeat the same prediction procedure for all
m
dominant POD modes chosen in Step
(2)
.
See Remark
0.12.3
.

(6)

At time
t
, use the predicted coefficients
\tilde{\alpha}_{i}(t)
together with the dominant POD modes
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
, for
i=1,\ldots,m
, to compute the flow field dynamics
u(\bm{x},t)
using Eq. (
8
).

0.12.3
Memory effects of POD coefficients on LSTM models

The results and deep-learning concepts used in
[
26
]
were presented in the motivational Section
0.2.3
above,
Figure
20
. In this section, we discuss some details of the formulation.

Remark 0.12.3
.

Even though
the value of
t_{inp}=t_{out}=0.1\,sec
was given in
[
26
]
as an example, all LSTM/BiLSTM networks in their numerical examples were trained using input/output pairs with
t_{inp}=t_{out}=10\times\Delta t
, i.e., 10 time steps for both input and output samples. With the overall simulation time for both physical problems (ISO and MHD) being
2.056\,secs
, the time step size is
\Delta t_{\text{ISO}}=2.056/5023=4.1\times 10^{-4}\,sec
for ISO, and
\Delta t_{\text{MHD}}=2.056/1024=2\times 10^{-3}\,sec=5\Delta t_{\text{ISO}}
for MHD. See also Remark
0.12.1
.

space
\hfill\blacksquare

The “U-velocity field for all results” was mentioned in
[
26
]
,
but without
a definition of “U-velocity”, which was possibly the
x
component of the 2-D velocity field in the 2-D planes (slices) used for the datasets, with “V-velocity” being the corresponding
y
components.

The BiLSTM networks in the numerical examples were not as accurate as the LSTM networks for both physical problems (ISO and MHD), despite having more computations; see Figure
117
above and Figure
20
in Section
0.2.3
.
The authors of

[
26
]
conjectured that a reason could be due to the randomness nature of turbulent flows, as opposed to the high long-term correlation found in natural human languages, for which BiLSTM was designed to address.

Since LSTM architecture was designed specifically for sequential data with memory,
it was
sought in
[
26
]
to quantify whether there was “memory” (or persistence) in the time series of the coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
of the POD mode
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
.
To this end, the Hurst exponent
281
281

281

“Hurst exponent”, Wikipedia,

version 22:09, 30 October 2020
.

was used to quantify the presence or absence of long-term memory in the time series
\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)

\displaystyle:=\{\alpha_{i}(t_{k+1}),\ldots,\alpha_{i}(t_{k+n})\},\ \underset{i,n\ \text{fixed}}{\mathbb{E}}\left(\frac{R_{i}(k,n)}{\sigma_{i}(k,n)}\right)=C_{i}e^{H_{i}},

(453)

\displaystyle\ R_{i}(k,n)

\displaystyle=\max\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)-\min\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n),\ \sigma_{i}(k,n)=\text{stdev}\ [\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)],

(454)

where

\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)
is a sequence of
n
steps of
\alpha_{i}(t)
, starting at snapshot time
t_{k+1}
,

\mathbb{E}
is the expectation of the ratio of range
R_{i}
over standard deviation
\sigma_{i}
for many samples
\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)
, with different values of
k
keeping
(i,n)
fixed,

R_{i}(k,n)
the range of sequence
\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)
,

\sigma_{i}(k,n)
the standard deviation of sequence
\mathcal{S}_{i}(k,n)
,

C_{i}
a constant,

H_{i}
the Hurst exponent for POD mode
\phi_{i}(\bm{x})
.

A Hurst coefficient of
H=1
indicates persistent behavior, i.e., an upward trend in a sequence is followed by an upward trend.
If
H=0
the behavior that is represented by time series data is anti-persistent, i.e., an upward trend is followed by a downward trend (and vice versa).
The case
H=0.5
indicates random behavior, which implies a lack of memory in the underlying process.

The effects of prediction horizon and persistence on the prediction accuracy of LSTM network were studied in
[
26
]
.
Horizon is the number of steps after the input sample that a LSTM model would predict the values of
\alpha_{i}(t)
, and is proportional to the output time length
t_{out}
, assuming constant time step size.
Persistence refers to the amount of correlation among subsequent realizations within sequential data, i.e., the presence of the long-term memory.

To this end, they selected one dataset (training or testing), and followed the multiple-network method in Step
(4)
of Section
0.12.2
to develop a different LSTM network model for each POD mode with “non-negligible eigenvalue”. For both the ISO (Figure
120
) and MHD problems, the 800 highest ranked POD modes were used.

A baseline horizon of 10 steps were used, for which the prediction errors were
\{1.08,1.37,1.94,2.36,

2.03,2.00\}
for POD ranks
\mathcal{R}=\{7,15,50,100,400,800\}
, and for Hurst exponents
\{0.78,0.76,0.652,

0.653,0.56,0.54\}
, respectively. So the prediction error increased from 1.08 for POD rank 7 to 2.36 for POD rank 100, then decreased slightly for POD ranks 400 and 800. The time histories of the corresponding coefficients
\alpha_{i}(t)
, with
i\in\mathcal{R}
on the right of Figure
120
provided only some qualitative comparison between the predicted values and the true values, but did not provide the scale of the actual magnitude of
\alpha_{i}(t)
, nor the time interval of these plots. For example, the magnitude of
\alpha_{800}(t)
could be very small compared to that of
\alpha_{7}(t)
, given that the POD modes were normalized in the sense of Eq. (
444
).
Qualitatively, the predicted values compared well with the true values for POD ranks 7, 15, 50.
So even though there was a divergence between the predicted value and the true value for POD ranks 100 and 800, but if the magnitude of
\alpha_{100}(t)
and
\alpha_{800}(t)
were very small compared to those of the dominant POD modes, there would be less of a concern.

Another expected result was that for the same POD mode rank lower than 50, the error increased dramatically with the prediction horizon. For example, for POD rank 7, the errors were
\{1.08,6.86,15.03,

19.42,21.40\}
for prediction horizons of
\{10,25,50,75,100\}
steps, respectively. Thus the error (at 21.40) for POD rank 7 with horizon 100 steps was more than ten times higher than the error (at 2.00) for POD rank 800 with horizon 10 steps. For POD mode rank 50 and higher, the error did not increase as much with the horizon, but stayed at about the same order of magnitude as the error for horizon 25 steps.

A final note is whether the above trained LSTM-ROM networks could produce accurate prediction for flow dynamics with different parameters such as Reynolds number, mass density, viscosity, geometry, initial conditions, etc., particularly that both the ISO and MHD datasets were created for a single Reynolds number, which was not mentioned in
[
26
]
, who mentioned that their method (and POD in general) would work for a “narrow range of Reynolds numbers” for which the flow dynamics is qualitatively similar, and for “simplified flow fields and geometries”.

Remark 0.12.4
.

Use of POD-ROM for different systems.

The authors of

[
306
]
studied
The flexibility of POD reduced-order models to solve nonlinear electromagnetic problems by varying the excitation form (e.g., square wave instead of sine wave) and by using the undamped (without the first-order time derivative term) snapshots in the simulation of the damped case (with the first-order time derivative term).
They demonstrated via numerical examples involving nonlinear power-magnetic-component simulations that the reduced-order models by POD are quite flexible and robust. See also Remark
0.12.2
.

\hfill\blacksquare

Remark 0.12.5
.

pyMOR - Model Order Reduction with Python.

Finally, we mention the software pyMOR,
282
282

282

See pyMOR website
https://pymor.org/
.

which is “a software library for building model order reduction applications with the Python programming language. Implemented algorithms include reduced basis methods for parametric linear and non-linear problems, as well as system-theoretic methods such as balanced truncation or IRKA (Iterative Rational Krylov Algorithm). All algorithms in pyMOR are formulated in terms of abstract interfaces for seamless integration with external PDE (Partial Differential Equation) solver packages. Moreover, pure Python implementations of FEM (Finite Element Method) and FVM (Finite Volume Method) discretizations using the NumPy/SciPy scientific computing stack are provided for getting started quickly.”
It is noted that pyMOR includes POD and “Model order reduction with artificial neural networks”, among many other methods; see the documentation of pyMOR.
Clearly, this software tool would be applicable to many physical problems, e.g., solids, structures, fluids, electromagnetics, coupled electro-thermal simulation, etc.

\hfill\blacksquare

Figure 121
:
Space-time solution of inviscid 1D-Burgers’ equation
(Section
0.12.4
).
The solution shows a characteristic steep spatial gradient, which shifts and further steepens in the course of time.
The FOM solution (left) and the solution of the proposed hyper-reduced ROM (center), in which the solution subspace is represented by a nonlinear manifold in the form of a feedforward neural network (Section
0.4
) (NM-LSPG-HR), show an excellent agreement, whereas the spatial gradient is significantly blurred in the solution obtained with a hyper-reduced ROM based on a linear subspace (LS-LSPG-HR) (right).
The FOM is a obtained upon a finite-difference approximation in the spatial domain with
N_{s}=1001
grid points (i.e., degrees of freedom); the backward Euler scheme is employed for time-integration using a step size
\Delta t=$1\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$
.
Both ROMs only use
n_{s}=5
generalized coordinates. The NM-LSPG-HR achieves a maximum relative error of less than
1\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, while the maximum relative error of the LS-LSPG-HR is approximately
6\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

0.12.4
Reduced order models and hyper-reduction

Reduction of computational expense also is the main aim of
nonlinear manifold reduced-order models
(NM-ROMs), which have recently been proposed in
[
47
]
,
283
283

283

Note that the authors also published a second condensed, but otherwise similar version of their article, see
[
48
]
.

where the
approach belongs to the class of
projection-based methods
.
Projection-based methods rely on the idea that solutions to physical simulations lie in a subspace of small dimensionality as compared to the dimensionality of high-fidelity models, which we obtain upon discretization (e.g., by finite elements) of the governing equations.
In classical projection-based methods, such
“intrinsic solution subspaces”
are spanned by a set of appropriate basis vectors that capture the essential features of the full-order model (FOM), i.e., the subspace is assumed to be linear.
We refer to
[
307
]
for a survey on projection-based linear subspace methods for parametric systems.
284
284

284

As opposed to the classical
non-parametric
case, in which all parameters are fixed,
parametric
methods aim at creating ROMs which account for (certain) parameters of the underlying governing equations to vary in some given range.
Optimization of large-scale systems, for which repeated evaluations are computationally intractable, is a classical use-case for methods in parametric MOR, see Benner
et al.

[
307
]
.

Motivating example: 1D Burger’s equation

The effectiveness of linear subspace methods is directly related to the dimensionality of the basis to represent solutions with sufficient accuracy.
Advection-dominated problems and problems with solutions that exhibit large (“sharp”) gradients, however, are characterized by a large Kolmogorov
n
-width,
285
285

285

Mathematically, the dimensionality of linear subspace that ‘best’ approximates a nonlinear manifold is described by the
Kolmogorov
n
-width
, see, e.g.,
[
308
]
for a formal definition.

which is adverse to linear subspace methods.
As examples,
The authors of

[
47
]

mention hyperbolic equations with large Reynolds number, Boltzmann transport equations and traffic flow simulations.
Many approaches to construct efficient ROMs in adverse problems are based on the idea to enhance the “solution representability” of the linear subspace, e.g., by using adaptive schemes tailored to particular problems.
Such problem-specific approaches, however, suffer from limited generality and a the necessity of a-priori knowledge as, e.g., the (spatial) direction of advection.
In view of these drawbacks,
the transition to a solution representation by
nonlinear manifolds
rather than using linear subspaces in projection-based ROMs was advocated in
[
47
]
.

Burger’s equation serves as a common prototype problem in numerical methods for nonlinear partial differential equations (PDEs) and MOR, in particular.
The inviscid Burgers’ equation in one spatial dimension is given by

\frac{\partial u(x,t;\mu)}{\partial t}+u(x,t;\mu)\frac{\partial u}{\partial x}=0,\qquad x\in\Omega=[0,2],\qquad t\in[0,T].

(455)

Burgers’ equation is a first-order hyperbolic equation which admits the formation of shock waves, i.e., regions with steep gradients in field variables, which propagate in the domain of interest.
Its left-hand side corresponds to material time-derivatives in Eulerian descriptions of continuum mechanics.
In the balance of linear momentum, for instance, the field
u
represents the velocity field.
Periodic boundary conditions and non-homogeneous initial conditions were assumed
[
47
]
:

u(2,t;\mu)=u(0,t;\mu),\qquad u(x,0;\mu)=\begin{cases}1+\frac{\mu}{2}\left(\sin\left(2\pi x-\frac{\pi}{2}\right)+1\right)&\text{if}\;0\leq x\leq 1\\
1&\text{otherwise}\end{cases},

(456)

The above initial conditions are governed by a scalar parameter
\mu\in\mathcal{D}=[0.9,1.1]
.
In parametric MOR, ROMs are meant to be valid for not just a single value of the parameter
\mu
, but it is supposed to be valid for a range of
values of
\mu

in the domain
\mathcal{D}
.
For this purpose, the reduced-order space, irrespective of whether a linear subspace of FOM or a nonlinear manifold is used, is typically constructed using data obtained for different values of the parameter
\mu
.
In
the
example in
[
47
]
,
the solutions for the parameter set
\mu\in[{$0.9$,$1.1$}]
were used to construct the individual ROMs.
Note that solution data corresponding to the parameter value
\mu=1
, for which the ROMs were evaluated, was not used in this example.

Figure
121
shows a zoomed view of solutions to the above problem obtained with the FOM (left), the proposed nonlinear manifold-based ROM (NM-LSPG-HR, center) and a conventional ROM, in which the full-order solution is represented by a linear subspace.
The initial solution is characterized by a “bump” in the left half of the domain, which is centered at
x=1/2
.
The advective nature of Burgers’ problem causes the bump to move right, which also results in slopes of the bump to increase in movement direction but decrease on the averted side.
The zoomed view in Figure
121
shows the region at the end (
t=0.5
) of the considered time-span, in which the (negative) gradient of the solution has already steepened significantly.
With as little as
n_{s}=5
generalized coordinates, the proposed nonlinear manifold-based approach (NM-LSPG-HR) succeeds in reproducing the FOM solution, which is obtained by a finite-difference approximation of the spatial domain using
N_{s}=$1001$
grid points; time-integration is performed by means of the backward Euler scheme with a constant step size of
\Delta t=$1\text{\times}{10}^{-3}$
, which translates into a total of
n_{t}=500
time steps.

The ROM based on a linear subspace of the full-order solution (LS-LSPG-HR) fails to accurately reproduce the steep spatial gradient that develops over time, see Figure
121
, right
Instead, the bump is substantially blurred in the linear subspace-based ROM as compared to the FOM’s solution (left).
The maximum error over all time steps
t_{n}
relative to the full-order solution defined as:

\text{Maximum relative error}=\max_{n\in\left\{1,\ldots,n_{t}\right\}}\frac{\left\|\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}(t_{n};\mu)-\bm{x}(t_{n};\mu)\right\|_{2}}{\left\|\bm{x}(t_{n};\mu)\right\|_{2}},

(457)

where
\bm{x}
and

{\bm{x}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

denote FOM and ROM solution vectors, respectively, was considered in
[
47
]
.
In terms of the above metric, the proposed nonlinear-manifold-based ROM achieves a maximum error of approximately
1\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
, whereas the linear-subspace-based ROM shows a maximum relative error of
6\text{\,}\mathrm{\%}
.
For the given problem, the linear-subspace-based ROM is approximately
5
to
6
times faster than the FOM.
The nonlinear-manifold-based ROM, however, does not achieve any speed unless
hyper-reduction
is employed.
Hyper-reduction (HR) methods provide means to efficiently evaluated nonlinear terms in ROMs without evaluations of the FOM (see Hyper-reduction, Section
0.12.4
).
Using hyper-reduction, a factor
2
speed-up is achieved for both the nonlinear-manifold and linear-subspace-based ROMs, i.e., the effective speed-ups amount to factors of
2
and
910
, respectively.

The solution manifold can be represented by means of a shallow, sparsely connected feed-forward neural network
[
47
]

(see Statics, feedforward networks, Sections
0.4
,
0.4.6
).
The network is trained in an unsupervised manner using the concept of autoencoders (see Autoencoder, Section
0.12.4
).

Nonlinear manifold-based (hyper-)reduction

The NM-ROM approach proposed in
[
47
]
addressed nonlinear dynamical systems, whose evolution
was
governed by a set of nonlinear ODEs, which
had
been obtained by a semi-discretization of the spatial domain, e.g., by means of finite elements:

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\bm{x}$}}}=\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{x}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\bm{f}(\bm{x},t;\bm{\mu}),\qquad\bm{x}(0;\bm{\mu})=\bm{x}_{0}(\bm{\mu}).

(458)

In the above relation,
\bm{x}(t;\bm{\mu})
denotes the parameterized solution of the problem, where
\bm{\mu}\in\mathcal{D}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{n_{\mu}}
are an
n_{\mu}
-dimensional vector of parameters;
\bm{x}_{0}(\bm{\mu})
is the initial state.
The function
\bm{f}
represents the rate of change of the state, which is assumed to be nonlinear in the state
\bm{x}
and possibly also in its other arguments, i.e., time
t
and the vector of parameters
\bm{\mu}
:
286
286

286

In solid mechanics, we typically deal with second-order ODEs, which can be converted into a system of first-order ODEs by including velocities in the state space.
For this reason, we prefer to use the term ‘rate’ rather than ‘velocity’ of
\bm{x}
in what follows.

\bm{x}:[0,T]\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}},\qquad\bm{f}:\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}\times[0,T]\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}.

(459)

The fundamental idea of any projection-based ROM is to approximate the original solution space of the FOM by a comparatively low-dimensional space
\mathcal{S}
.
In view of the aforementioned shortcomings of linear subspaces, the authors of
[
47
]

proposed
a representation in terms of a
nonlinear manifold
, which
was
described by the vector-valued function
\bm{g}
, whose dimensionality
was
supposed to be much smaller than that of the FOM:

\mathcal{S}=\left\{\bm{g}(\hat{\bm{v}})\;|\;\hat{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\right\},\qquad\bm{g}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}},\qquad\dim(\mathcal{S})=n_{s}\ll N_{s}.

(460)

Using the nonlinear function
\bm{g}
, an approximation

{\bm{x}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

to the FOM’s solution
\bm{x}

was
constructed using a set of generalized coordinates
\widehat{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}
:

\bm{x}\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}),

(461)

where
\bm{x}_{ref}

denoted
a (fixed) reference solution.
The rate of change
\dot{\bm{x}}

was
approximated by

\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{x}}{\mathrm{d}t}\approx\frac{\mathrm{d}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\bm{x}}}{\mathrm{d}t},\qquad\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})=\frac{\partial\bm{g}}{\partial\widehat{\bm{x}}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times n_{s}},

(462)

where the Jacobian
\bm{J}_{g}

spanned
the tangent space to the manifold at
\widehat{\bm{x}}
.
The initial
conditions for the generalized coordinates
were
given by
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{0}=\bm{g}^{-1}(\bm{x}_{0}-\bm{x}_{ref})
, where
\bm{g}^{-1}

denoted
the inverse function to
\bm{g}
.

Note that
linear subspace
methods are included in the above relations as the special case where
\bm{g}
is a linear function, which can be written in terms of a
constant
matrix
\bm{\Phi}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times n_{s}}
, i.e.,
\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})=\bm{\Phi}\widehat{\bm{x}}
.
In this case, the approximation to the solution in Eq. (
461
) and their respective rates in Eq. (
462
) are given by

\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})=\bm{\Phi}\widehat{\bm{x}}\Rightarrow\bm{x}\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{\Phi}\widehat{\bm{x}},\qquad\frac{\mathrm{d}\bm{x}}{\mathrm{d}t}\approx\frac{\mathrm{d}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}}{\mathrm{d}t}=\bm{\Phi}\frac{\mathrm{d}\widehat{\bm{x}}}{\mathrm{d}t}.

(463)

As opposed to the nonlinear manifold Eq. (
462
), the tangent space of the (linear) solution manifold is constant, i.e.,
\bm{J}_{g}=\bm{\Phi}
; see
[
307
]
.
We also mention the example of eigenmodes as a classical choice for basis vectors of reduced subspaces
[
309
]
.

The authors of

[
47
]

defined a
residual function

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}:\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{D}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}
in the reduced set of coordinates by rewriting the governing ODE Eq. (
458
) and substituting the approximation of the state Eq. (
461
) and its rate Eq. (
462
):
287
287

287

The tilde above the symbol for the residual function

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

in Eq. (
464
) was used to indicate an approximation, consistent with the use of the tilde in the approximation of the state in Eq. (
461
), i.e.,
\bm{x}\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}
.

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}({\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}},\widehat{\bm{x}},t;\bm{\mu})=\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}){\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}}-\bm{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}),t;\bm{\mu})

(464)

As
N_{s}>n_{s}
, the system of equations
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}({\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}},\widehat{\bm{x}},t;\bm{\mu})=\mathbf{0}
is over-determined and no solution exists in general.
For this reason, a least-squares solution that minimized the square of the residual’s Euclidean norm, which was denoted by
\|(\cdot)\|_{2}
, was sought instead
[
47
]
:

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}}=\argmin_{\widehat{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}}\left\|\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}(\widehat{\bm{v}},\widehat{\bm{x}},t;\bm{\mu})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(465)

Requiring the derivative of the (squared) residual to vanish, we obtain the following set of equations,
288
288

288

As the authors of
[
47
]
omitted a step-by-step derivation, we introduce it here for the sake of completeness.

\frac{\partial}{\partial{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}}}\left\|\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}({\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}},\widehat{\bm{x}},t;\bm{\mu})\right\|_{2}^{2}=2\bm{J}_{g}^{T}\left(\bm{J}_{g}{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}}-\bm{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}),t;\bm{\mu})\right)=\mathbf{0},

(466)

which can be rearranged for the rate of the reduced vector of generalized coordinates as:

{\vbox{\moveright 1.4457pt\hbox{$\bullet$}\hbox{$\widehat{\bm{x}}$}}}=\bm{J}^{\dagger}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})\bm{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}),t;\bm{\mu}),\qquad\widehat{\bm{x}}(0;\bm{\mu})=\widehat{\bm{x}}_{0}(\bm{\mu}),\qquad\bm{J}^{\dagger}_{g}=(\bm{J}_{g}^{T}\bm{J}_{g})^{-1}\bm{J}_{g}^{T}.

(467)

The above system of ODEs, in which
\bm{J}^{\dagger}_{g}
denotes the
Moore-Penrose inverse
of the Jacobian
\bm{J}_{g}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times n_{s}}
, which is also referred to as
pseudo-inverse
, constitutes the ROM corresponding to the FOM governed by Eq. (
458
).
Equation (
466
) reveals that the minimization problem is equivalent to a projection of the full residual onto the low-dimensional subspace
\mathcal{S}
by means of the Jacobian
\bm{J}_{g}
.
Note that the rate of the generalized coordinates

\bullet
\widehat{\bm{x}}

lies in the same tangent space, which is spanned by
\bm{J}_{g}
.
Therefore, the authors of
[
47
]
described the projection Eq. (
466
) as
Galerkin projection
and the ROM Eq. (
467
) as
nonlinear manifold (NM) Galerkin ROM
.
To construct the solutions, suitable time-integration schemes needed to be applied to the semi-discrete ROM Eq. (
467
).

An alternative approach was also presented in
[
47
]
for the construction of ROMs, in which time-discretization was performed prior to the projection onto the low-dimensional solution subspace.
For this purpose a uniform time-discretization with a step size
\Delta t
was assumed; the solution at time
t_{n}=t_{n-1}+\Delta t=n\Delta t
was denoted by
\bm{x}_{n}=\bm{x}(t_{n};\bm{\mu})
.
To develop the method for implicit integration schemes, the
backward Euler
method was chosen as example
[
47
]
:

\bm{x}_{n}-\bm{x}_{n-1}=\Delta t\bm{f}_{n},

(468)

The above integration rule implies that span of rate
\bm{f}_{n}
, which is given by evaluating (or by taking the ‘snapshot’ of) the nonlinear function
\bm{f}
, is included in the span of the state (‘solution snapshot’)
\bm{x}_{n}
:

\spanop\left\{\bm{f}_{n}\right\}\subseteq\spanop\left\{\bm{x}_{n-1},\bm{x}_{n}\right\}\qquad\rightarrow\qquad\spanop\left\{\bm{f}_{1},\ldots,\bm{f}_{N_{t}}\right\}\subseteq\spanop\left\{\bm{x}_{0},\ldots,\bm{x}_{N_{t}}\right\}.

(469)

For the backward Euler scheme Eq. (
468
), a residual function was defined in
[
47
]
as the difference

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n};\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1},\bm{\mu})=\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n})-\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1})-\Delta t\bm{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}),t_{n};\bm{\mu}).

(470)

Just as in the time-continuous domain, the system of equations
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n};\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1},\bm{\mu})=\mathbf{0}
is over-determined and, hence, was reformulated as a least-squares problem for the generalized coordinates
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}
:

\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}=\frac{1}{2}\argmin_{\widehat{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}}\left\|\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{v}};\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1},\bm{\mu})\right\|_{2}^{2}.

(471)

Figure 122
:

Dense vs. shallow decoder networks
(Section
0.12.4
).
Contributing neurons (orange “nodes”) and connections (orange “edges”) lie in the “active” paths arriving at the selected outputs (solid orange “nodes”) from the decoder’s inputs.
In dense networks as the one in (a), each neuron in a layer is connected to all other neurons in both the preceeding layer (if it exists) and in the succeeding layer (if it exists).
Fully-connected networks are characterized by dense weight matrices, see Section
0.4.4
.
In sparse networks as the decoder in (b), several connections among successive layers are dropped, resulting in sparsely populated weight matrices.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

To solve the least-squares problem, the Gauss-Newton method with starting point
\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}
was applied, i.e., the residual Eq. (
470
) was expanded into a first-order Taylor polynomial in the generalized coordinates
[
47
]
:

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n};\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1},\bm{\mu})

\displaystyle\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1};\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1},\bm{\mu})+\left.\frac{\partial\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}}{\partial\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}}\right|_{\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}=\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}}\left(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\Delta t\bm{f}_{n-1}+\left(\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1})-\Delta t\bm{J}_{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}))\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1})\right)\left(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}\right)

\displaystyle=-\Delta t\bm{f}_{n-1}+\left(\bm{I}-\Delta t\bm{J}_{f}(\bm{x}_{ref}+\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}))\right)\bm{J}_{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1})\left(\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n}-\widehat{\bm{x}}_{n-1}\right).

(472)

Unlike the time-continuous case, the Gauss-Newton method results in a projection involving not only the Jacobian
\bm{J}_{g}
defined in Eq. (
462
), but also the Jacobian of the nonlinear function
\bm{f}
, i.e.,
\bm{J}_{f}=\partial\bm{f}/\partial\bm{x}_{n}
.
Therefore, the resulting reduced set of algebraic equations obtained upon a projection of the fully discrete FOM was referred to as
nonlinear manifold least-squares Petrov-Galerkin (NM-LSPG)
ROM
[
47
]
.

Autoencoder

Within
the
NS-ROM approach,
it was proposed in
[
47
]
to construct

\bm{g}
by training an
autoencoder
, i.e., a neural network that reproduces its input vector:

\bm{x}\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\mathbf{ae}(\bm{x})=\mathbf{de}(\mathbf{en}(\bm{x})).

(473)

As the above relation reveals, autoencoders are typically composed from two parts, i.e.,
\mathbf{ae}(\bm{x})=(\mathbf{de}\circ\mathbf{en})(\bm{x})
:
The
encoder
‘codes’ inputs
\bm{x}
into a so-called
latent state

\bm{h}=\mathbf{en}(\bm{x})
(not to be confused with hidden states of neural networks).
The
decoder
then reconstructs (‘decodes’) an approximation of the input from the latent state, i.e.,
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\mathbf{de}(\bm{h})
.
Both encoder and decoder can be represented by neural networks, e.g., feed-forward networks as done in

[
47
]
.
As
the authors of
[
78
]

noted, an autoencoder is “not especially useful” if it exactly learns the identity mapping for all possible inputs
\bm{x}
.
Instead, autoencoders are typically restricted in some way, e.g., by reducing the dimensionality of the latent space as compared to the dimension of inputs, i.e.,
\dim(\bm{h})<\dim(\bm{x})
.
The restriction forces an autoencoder to focus on those aspects of the input, or input ‘features’, which are essential for the reconstruction of the input.
The encoder network of such
undercomplete autoencoder
289
289

289

See

[
78
]
,
Chapter 14, p.493.

performs a
dimensionality reduction
, which is exactly
the aim of
projection-based methods for constructing ROMs.
Using nonlinear encoder/decoder networks, undercomplete autoencoders can be trained to represent low-dimensional subspaces of solutions to high-dimensional dynamical systems governed by Eq. (
458
).
290
290

290

In fact, linear decoder networks combined with MSE-loss are equivalent to the
Principal Components Analysis
(PCA) (see

[
78
]
,
p.494), which, in turn, is equivalent to the discrete variant of POD by means of singular value decomposition (see Remark
0.12.2
).

In particular, the decoder network represents the nonlinear manifold
\mathcal{S}
, which described by the function
\bm{g}
that maps the generalized coordinates of the ROM
\widehat{\bm{x}}
onto the corresponding element

{\bm{x}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

of FOM’s solution space.
Accordingly, the generalized coordinates are identified with the autoencoder’s latent state, i.e.,
\widehat{\bm{x}}=\bm{h}
; the encoder network represent the inverse mapping
\bm{g}^{-1}
, which “captures the most salient features” of the FOM.

The input data, which was formed by the snapshots of solutions
\bm{X}=[\bm{x}_{1},\ldots,\bm{x}_{m}]
, where
m
denoted the number of snapshots, was normalized when training the network
[
47
]
.
The shift by the referential state
\bm{x}_{ref}
and scaling by
\bm{x}_{scale}
, i.e.,

\bm{x}_{normal}=\bm{x}_{scale}\odot\left(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{ref}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}},

(474)

is such that each component of the normalized input ranges from
[-1,1]
or
[0,1]
.
291
291

291

The authors of
[
47
]
did not provide further details.

The encoder maps a FOM’s (normalized) solution snapshot onto a corresponding low-dimensional latent state
\widehat{\bm{x}}
, i.e., vector of generalized coordinates:

\widehat{\bm{x}}=\bm{g}^{-1}(\bm{x})=\mathbf{en}(\bm{x}_{normal})=\mathbf{en}(\bm{x}_{scale}\odot\left(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{ref}\right))\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{s}}.

(475)

The decoder reconstructs the high-dimensional solution

{\bm{x}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

from the low-dimensional state
\widehat{\bm{x}}
inverting the normalization Eq. (
474
):

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\bm{g}(\widehat{\bm{x}})=\bm{x}_{ref}+\mathbf{de}(\widehat{\bm{x}})\oslash\bm{x}_{scale}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}},

(476)

where the operator
\oslash
denotes the element-wise division.
The composition of encoder and decoder give the autoencoder, i.e.,

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=\mathbf{ae}(\bm{x})=\bm{x}_{ref}+\mathbf{de}(\mathbf{en}(\bm{x}_{scale}\odot(\bm{x}-\bm{x}_{ref})))\oslash\bm{x}_{scale},

(477)

which is trained in an
unsupervised
way to (approximately) reproduce states
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-5.12495pt\hbox{${\bm{X}}$}\hskip-5.12495pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.13892pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}=[\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{1},\ldots,\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{m}]
from input snapshots
\bm{X}
.
To train autoencoders, the
mean squared error
(see Sec.
0.5.1
) is the natural choice for the loss function, i.e.,

J=\|\bm{X}-\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-5.12495pt\hbox{${\bm{X}}$}\hskip-5.12495pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.13892pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\|_{F}/2\rightarrow\min
, where the Frobenius norm of matrices is used.

Both the encoder and the decoder are feed-forward neural networks with a single hidden layer.
As activation function,
it was
proposed in
[
47
]
to use the sigmoid function (see Figure
30
and Section
0.13.3
) or the swish function (see Figure
139
).
It remains unspecified, however, which of the two were used in the numerical examples in
[
47
]
.
Though the representational capacity of neural networks increases with depth, which is what ‘deep learning’ is all about,
the authors of
[
47
]

deliberately used shallow networks to minimize the (computational) complexity of computing the decoder’s Jacobian
\bm{J}_{g}
.
To further improve computational efficiency,
they
proposed to use a “masked/sparse decoder”, in which the weight matrix that mapped the hidden state onto the output

{\bm{x}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

was sparse.
As opposed to a fully populated weight matrix of fully-connected networks (Figure
122
(a)), in which each component of the output vector depends on all components of the hidden state, the outputs of a sparse decoder (Figure
122
(b)) only depend on the selected components of the hidden state.

Figure 123
:

Sparsity masks
(Section
0.12.4
) used to realize sparse decoders in one- and two-dimensional problems. The structure of the respective binary-valued mask matrices
\bm{S}
is inspired by grid-points required in the finite-difference approximation of the Laplace operator in one and two dimensions, respectively.

(Figure reproduced with permission of the authors.)

Using our notation for feedforward networks and activation functions introduced in Section
0.4.4
, the encoder network has the following structure:

\displaystyle\widehat{\bm{x}}=\mathbf{en}(\bm{x}_{normal}):=

\displaystyle\bm{W}_{\rm en}^{(2)}\bm{y}^{(1)}+\bm{b}_{\rm en}^{(2)},\qquad\bm{y}^{(1)}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{(1)}),

(478)

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\bm{W}_{\rm en}^{(1)}\bm{y}^{(0)}+\bm{b}_{\rm en}^{(1)},\qquad\bm{y}^{(0)}=\bm{x}_{normal},

where
\bm{W}_{\rm de}^{(i)}
,
i=1,2
are dense matrices.
The masked decoder, on the contrary, is characterized by sparse connections between the hidden layer and the output layer, which is realized as element-wise multiplication of a dense weight matrix
\bm{W}_{\rm de}^{(2)}
with a binary-valued “mask matrix”
\bm{S}
reflecting the connectivity among the two layers:

\displaystyle\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-3.29514pt\hbox{${\bm{x}}$}\hskip-3.29514pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\odot\bm{x}_{scale}-\bm{x}_{ref}=\mathbf{de}(\widehat{\bm{x}}):=\bm{S}\,\odot

\displaystyle\bm{W}_{\rm de}^{(2)}\bm{y}^{(1)}+\bm{b}_{\rm de}^{(2)},\qquad\bm{y}^{(1)}=\mathfrak{s}(\bm{z}^{(1)}),

(479)

\displaystyle\bm{z}^{(1)}=

\displaystyle\bm{W}_{\rm de}^{(1)}\bm{y}^{(0)}+\bm{b}_{\rm de}^{(1)},\qquad\bm{y}^{(0)}=\widehat{\bm{x}}.

The structure of the sparsity mask
\bm{S}
, in turn, is inspired by the pattern (“stencil") of grid-points involved in a finite-difference approximation of the Laplace operator, see Figure
123
for the one- and two-dimensional cases.

Hyper-reduction

Irrespective of how small the dimensionality of the ROM’s solution subspace
\mathcal{S}
is, we cannot expect a reduction in computational efforts in nonlinear problems.
Both the time-continuous NM-Galerkin ROM in Eq. (
467
) and the time-discrete NM-LSPG ROM in Eq. (
471
) require repeated evaluations of the nonlinear function
\bm{f}
and, in case implicit time-integration schemes, also its Jacobian
\bm{J}_{f}
, whose computational complexity is determined by the FOM’s dimensionality.
The term
hyper-reduction
subsumes techniques in model-order reduction to overcome the necessity for evaluations that scale with the FOM’s size.
Among hyper-reduction techniques, the
Discrete Empirical Interpolation Method
(DEIM)
[
310
]
and
Gauss-Newton with Approximated Tensors
(GNAT)
[
311
]
have gained significant attention in recent years.

In
[
47
]
,
a variant of GNAT relying on solution snapshots for the approximation of the nonlinear residual term

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

was used, and therefore the reason to extend the GNAT acronym with “SNS” for
’solution-based subspace’
(SNS), i.e.,
GNAT-SNS
, see
[
312
]
.
The idea of DEIM, GNAT and their SNS variants takes up the leitmotif of projection-based methods: The approximation of the full-order residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

is in turn linearly interpolated by a low-dimensional vector
\widehat{\bm{r}}
and an appropriate set of basis vectors
\bm{\phi}_{r,i}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}
,
i=1,\ldots,n_{r}
:

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\approx\bm{\Phi}_{r}\widehat{\bm{r}},\qquad\bm{\Phi}_{r}=\left[\bm{\phi}_{r,1},\ldots,\bm{\phi}_{r,n_{r}}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times n_{r}},\qquad n_{s}\leq n_{r}\ll N_{s}.

(480)

In DEIM methods, the residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

of the time-continuous problem in Eq. (
464
) is approximated, whereas

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

it to be replaced by
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}
in the above relation when applying GNAT, which builds upon Petrov-Galerkin ROMs as in Eq. (
472
).
Both DEIM and GNAT variants use
gappy POD
to determine the basis
\bm{\Phi}_{r}
for the interpolation of the nonlinear residual.
Gappy POD originates in a method for image reconstruction proposed

[
313
]

under the name of a
Karhunen-Loève procedure, in which images were reconstructed from individual pixels, i.e., from
gappy data
.
In the present context of MOR, gappy POD aims at reconstructing the full-order residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

from a small,
n_{r}
-dimensional subset of its components.

The matrix
\bm{\Phi}_{r}
was computed by a singular value decomposition (SVD) on the snapshots of data.
Unlike original DEIM and GNAT methods, their SNS variants did not use snapshots of the nonlinear residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

and
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{BE}^{n}
, respectively, but SVD was performed on solution snapshots
\bm{X}
instead.
The use of solution snapshots was motivated by the fact that the span of snapshots of the nonlinear term
\bm{f}
was included in the span of of solution snapshots for conventional time-integration schemes, see Eq. (
469
).
The vector of
“generalized coordinates of the nonlinear residual term”
minimizes the square of the Euclidean distance of selected components of full-order residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

and respective components of its reconstruction:

\widehat{\bm{r}}=\argmin_{\widehat{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{r}}}\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{Z}^{T}\left(\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}-\bm{\Phi}_{r}\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)\right\|_{2}^{2},\quad\bm{Z}^{T}=\left[\bm{e}_{p_{1}},\ldots,\bm{e}_{p_{n_{z}}}\right]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{z}\times N_{s}},\quad n_{s}\leq n_{r}\leq n_{z}\ll N_{s}.

(481)

The matrix
\bm{Z}^{T}
, which was referred to as
sampling matrix
, extracts a set of components from the full-order vectors
\bm{v}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}
.
For this purpose, the sampling matrix was built from unit vectors
\bm{e}_{p_{i}}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}}
, having the value one at the
p_{i}
-th component, which corresponded to the component of
\bm{v}
to be selected (‘sampled’), and the value zero otherwise.
The components selected by
\bm{Z}^{T}
are equivalently described by the (ordered) set of
sampling indices

\mathcal{I}=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n_{z}}\right\}
, which are represented by the vector
\bm{i}=[p_{1},\ldots,p_{n_{z}}]^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{z}}
.
As the number of components of

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

used for its reconstruction may be larger than the dimensionality of the reduced-order vector
\widehat{\bm{r}}
, i.e.,
n_{z}\geq n_{r}
, Eq. (
481
) generally constitutes a least-squares problem, the solution of which follows as

\widehat{\bm{r}}=(\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\Phi}_{r})^{\dagger}\bm{Z}^{T}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}.

(482)

Substituting the above result in Eq. (
480
),
the FOM’s residual can be interpolated using an
oblique projection matrix

\bm{\mathcal{P}}
as

\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\approx\bm{\mathcal{P}}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt},\qquad\bm{\mathcal{P}}=\bm{\Phi}_{r}(\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\Phi}_{r})^{\dagger}\bm{Z}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times N_{s}}.

(483)

The above representation in terms of the projection matrix
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
somewhat hides the main point of hyperreduction.
In fact, we do not apply
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
to the full-order residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

, which would be tautological.
Unrolling the definition of
\bm{\mathcal{P}}
, we note that
\bm{Z}^{T}\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.80324pt\hbox{${\bm{r}}$}\hskip-2.80324pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-3.55559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}\in\mathbb{R}^{n_{z}}
is a vector containing only a small subset of components of the full-order residual

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

.
In other words, to evaluate the approximation in Eq. (
483
) only
n_{z}\ll N_{s}
of components of

{\bm{r}}

\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}

need to be computed, i.e., the computational cost no longer scales with the FOM’s dimensionality.

Several methods have been proposed to efficiently construct a suitable set of sampling indices
\mathcal{I}
, see, e.g.,
[
310
,
311
,
314
]
.
These methods share the property of being
greedy algorithms
, i.e., algorithms that sequentially (“inductively”) create optimal sampling indices using some suitable metric. For instance, the authors of
[
310
]
selected the
j
-th index corresponding to the component of the gappy reconstruction of the
j
-th POD-mode
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}_{r,j}
showing the largest error compared to the original mode
\bm{\phi}_{r,j}
.
For the reconstruction, the first
j-1
POD-modes were used, i.e.,

\bm{\phi}_{r,j}\approx\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.97917pt\hbox{${\bm{\phi}}$}\hskip-2.97917pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.05559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{r,j}=\bm{\Phi}_{r}\widehat{\bm{\phi}}_{r,j},\qquad\bm{\Phi}_{r}=\left[\bm{\phi}_{r,1},\ldots,\bm{\phi}_{r,j-1}\right]\in\mathbb{R}^{N_{s}\times j-1}.

(484)

The reduced-order vector
\widehat{\bm{\phi}}_{r,j}\in\mathbb{R}^{j-1}
minimizes the square of the Euclidean distance between
j-1
components (selected by
\bm{Z}^{T}\in\mathbb{R}^{j\times N_{s}}
) of the
j
-th mode
\bm{\phi}_{r,j}
and its gappy reconstruction
\hbox{\hskip 55.55573pt\hskip-2.97917pt\hbox{${\bm{\phi}}$}\hskip-2.97917pt\hskip-55.55573pt\raisebox{-1.05559pt}{\hbox{\scalebox{1}[1.5]{$\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\displaystyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\textstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}{\scalebox{1.0}[1]{$\scriptscriptstyle\widetilde{\phantom{\;}}$}}$}}}\hskip-55.55573pt\hskip 55.55573pt}_{r,j}
:

\widehat{\bm{\phi}}_{r,j}=\argmin_{\widehat{\bm{v}}\in\mathbb{R}^{j-1}}\frac{1}{2}\left\|\bm{Z}^{T}\left(\bm{\phi}_{r,j}-\bm{\Phi}_{r}\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)\right\|^{2}_{2}.

(485)

The key idea of the greedy algorithm is to select additional indices to minimize the error of the gappy reconstruction.
Therefore, the component of the reconstructed mode that differs most (in terms of magnitude) from the original mode defines the
j
-th sampling index:

p_{j}=\argmax_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N_{s}\}\backslash\mathcal{I}}\left|(\bm{\phi}_{r,j}-\bm{\Phi}_{r}(\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\Phi}_{r})^{\dagger}\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\phi}_{r,j})_{i}\right|.

(486)

A pseudocode representation of the greedy approach for selecting sampling indices is given in Algorithm
8
.
To start the algorithm, the first sampling index is chosen according to the largest (in terms of magnitude) component of the first POD-mode, i.e.,
p_{1}=\arg\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N_{s}\}}|\left(\bm{\phi}_{r,1}\right)_{i}|
.

DEIM algorithm for construction of sampling indices

Data:

POD-basis
\{\bm{\phi}_{r,1},\ldots,\bm{\phi}_{r,n_{r}}\}
, in descending order according to singular values

Result:

Set of sampling indices
\mathcal{I}=\left\{p_{1},\ldots,p_{n_{z}}\right\}

\blacktriangleright
Initialization

Select first sampling index
p_{1}=\arg\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N_{s}\}}|\left(\bm{\phi}_{r,1}\right)_{i}|

;

1

Set
\bm{\Phi}_{r}\leftarrow[\bm{\phi}_{r,1}]
,
\bm{Z}\leftarrow[\bm{e}_{p_{1}}]
,
\bm{i}=[p_{1}]
,
\mathcal{I}=\left\{p_{1}\right\}
;

2

for

j=2,\ldots,n_{z}

do

3

\blacktriangleright
Select
j
-th sampling index
p_{j}

Solve
(\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\Phi}_{r})\widehat{\bm{v}}=\bm{Z}^{T}\bm{\phi}_{r,j}
for
\widehat{\bm{v}}
(cf. Eq. (
485
)) ;

4

Set
p_{j}=\arg\max_{i\in\{1,\ldots,N_{s}\}\backslash\mathcal{I}}|\left(\bm{\phi}_{r,j}-\bm{\Phi}_{r}\widehat{\bm{v}}\right)_{i}|
;

5

Update
\bm{\Phi}_{r}\leftarrow\begin{bmatrix}\bm{\Phi}_{r}&\bm{\phi}_{r,j}\end{bmatrix}
,

\bm{Z}\leftarrow\begin{bmatrix}\bm{Z}&\bm{e}_{p_{j}}\end{bmatrix}
,

\bm{i}\rightarrow\begin{bmatrix}\bm{i}^{T}&p_{j}\end{bmatrix}^{T}
,

\mathcal{I}\leftarrow\mathcal{I}\cup\left\{p_{j}\right\}
;

6

end for
